Title: The alignment scaling problem: do external AI safety approaches keep pace with recursive capability?
Author: Michael Darius Eastwood
Version: v1.18
Version date: 31 October 2026
OSF DOI: 10.17605/OSF.IO/HQCGF
Source: full text extracted from the accompanying official PDF

        The alignment scaling problem: do external AI safety
          approaches keep pace with recursive capability?
       Theory · Paper III of the ARC Theory, bearing on Law II, the ARC Co-Scaling Law,
                                        a law under test

                                               Michael Darius Eastwood
                               Independent Researcher, London, United Kingdom
                       ORCID 0009-0004-3222-7442 michael@michaeldariuseastwood.com



                                                            Abstract
            Does alignment applied from outside an AI system’s reasoning keep pace with the capability
       that reasoning produces? If capability compounds with recursive depth while safeguards outside
       the loop (human-feedback training, constitutional rules, output filters, monitoring) do not, the
       safety ratio falls with depth whatever the starting margin. We define the alignment scaling exponent
       𝛼align = 𝜕 log 𝐴/𝜕 log 𝑅, show that the safety ratio 𝑆 ∝ 𝑅 𝛼align − 𝛼cap tends to zero whenever 𝛼align < 𝛼cap ,
       and predict 𝛼align ≈ 0 for external approaches and 𝛼align ≈ 𝛼cap for values embedded in the loop; the
       first, P19 of the theory registration, carries the author’s expectation of a value above zero but short of
       keeping pace. A framework derives 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) with 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) from a Bernoulli equation,
       ties power-law, exponential and saturating forms to how recursive gains compose, and states the
       ARC Bound, 𝛼 ≤ 2, as a conjectured safety boundary, argued for attention-based recursion; Paper X
       later supersedes that framing, without retracting the bound, with the criterion of Law II, a law under
       test. Across four domains the framework is consistent with independently established forms in three:
       biology, quantum error correction and classical time crystals, where no growth exponent has been
       measured. A first test, one unregistered run of six frontier models, gives depth contrasts in three
       tiers, positive, flat and negative; an internal code review in October 2026 found defects in exactly the
       flat and negative runs, so the tiers stand as a single-run result. On capability, an early super-linear
       estimate is retracted and the surviving one, about 0.49 from one model, is inconclusive. Thirteen
       criteria state what would refute the framework.

Version 1.18 states the result of Unit 83, the counted test of P21, now that its results are public, and gives the
acoustic time crystal its status as a consistency check, not a test of the framework; Appendix B lists every version
and correction.




    Paper III of the ARC Theory, within Recursive Dynamics, and the safety-side counterpart to Papers I and II: it predicts that
oversight applied from outside falls behind recursive capability as depth grows, and judges stability by the criterion of Law II,
the ARC Co-Scaling Law (correction out-scaling drift), a law under test. Its headline prediction is P19 of the theory registration,
which places it in no law’s register and names it the motivation of the Eden Protocol and the reason for ARC-5, the register of
Law V, the ARC Embedding Law (added on 24 September 2026 and registered as a law on 15 October 2026 in version 1.103; a
law under test; its mathematics in development). In February 2026 it derived the ARC Bound, the conjectured value of Law III,
the ARC Ceiling (also a law under test), as a safety boundary. Working paper, version 1.18, 31 October 2026; first published 9
February 2026; Appendix B lists every version and correction. Recorded on OSF as Paper III · The ARC Theory · Law II, the ARC
Co-Scaling Law · The Alignment Scaling Problem: Do External AI Safety Approaches Keep Pace With Recursive Capability?,
DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/HQCGF. About the author: michaeldariuseastwood.com/questions/who-is-michael-darius-eastwood; his

                                                    3
                                      The alignment scaling problem


1    Introduction
Do current AI alignment approaches keep pace with the capability they govern? Reasoning models now
improve their answers by spending more steps on them, each step taking the previous one as its input,
while the safeguards deployed around them (training on human feedback, constitutional rules, output
filters and monitoring) act on the system from outside that loop. If capability compounds with recursive
depth and those safeguards do not, the safeguards weaken in proportion at every further step, whatever
margin they start with, so the question decides whether safety measured on today’s systems says anything
about the same systems reasoning more deeply. This paper states that question as a measurable exponent,
derives the mathematics behind it, sets the framework against evidence from four domains and reports its
first test.
     Between December 2024 and February 2026, research programmes in AI reasoning, quantum error
correction, acoustic physics and consciousness science reported results in which recursive or recurrent
processing matters. Google’s Willow processor suppressed logical error exponentially as the code distance
grew (Google Quantum AI and Collaborators 2024; results announced 9 December 2024; the team’s
preprint public from 24 August 2024; graded as convergent timing, never as a prediction); DeepSeek’s
R1 report described reasoning behaviours, long chains of thought among them, that emerged through
reinforcement learning (DeepSeek-AI 2025); physicists at New York University created an acoustic time
crystal powered by nonreciprocal wave-mediated interactions (Morrell et al. 2026); and the COGITATE
consortium’s adversarial test of theories of consciousness found sustained content-specific activity in
posterior cortex, a prediction shared by theories that emphasise recurrent processing (Cogitate Consortium
et al. 2025). These programmes share no citations, no funding sources and no common personnel. Their
forms differ: a power law in AI reasoning, exponential suppression of error in quantum error correction,
saturating dynamics in a classical time crystal, and no measured form in neuroscience. No growth
exponent has been measured in the acoustic system, whose published recordings are of an oscillation
already established, and the framework is consistent with the independently established forms in three of
the four domains.
     This paper argues that these findings, taken together, point to a structural constraint with immediate
consequences for AI safety.
     The core premise: AI capability may scale through recursive self-correction. Each reasoning step
takes the output of the previous step as input, correcting errors and accumulating insight; whether the
gains compound is the question Section 6.2 tests, and the one estimate that survives is inconclusive.
Sharma and Chopra (2025) reported a sequential advantage in 95.6% of tested configurations across
five open-source models from three families. Their paper predates the experiment reported here, so
it is the earlier demonstration rather than a confirmation of it, and what they report is a win rate at
matched compute, not a super-linear gain: they estimate no scaling exponent. The direction is contested,
since Gu and colleagues (arXiv:2604.05868, April 2026) report the opposite result, parallel sampling
outperforming sequential across three model families on mathematics and coding, attributing it to reduced
exploration when a chain conditions on its own previous answers.
     The structural question: Do current AI alignment approaches scale with capability? The alignment
strategies this paper examines operate outside the recursive reasoning process; deliberative alignment,
which trains a model to reason over written safety specifications inside its chain of thought (Guan et al.
2024), is the published exception, and Section 2 weighs it:
    • RLHF adjusts model weights based on human feedback, but the resulting constraints do not
      compound during inference.
    • Constitutional AI shapes the model’s outputs during training against written principles, but that
      shaping does not recursively amplify during reasoning.


                                                    4
                                       The alignment scaling problem


    • Output filters screen responses after generation, with fixed detection thresholds.
    • Monitoring systems observe behaviour from outside the reasoning loop.
    If these approaches do not participate in the recursive composition, they cannot compound. Empirical
evidence supports this concern: Greenblatt et al. (2024) report a model that complied selectively with a
new training objective while it was being trained, to keep its existing, harmless preferences from being
modified outside training: training imposed from outside was gamed by the system it was meant to shape,
here to protect a benign preference. If capability compounds while alignment does not, the safety ratio
degrades. The cross-domain evidence (Section 5) is consistent with this compounding behaviour being
not a software quirk but a structural property of recursive systems, which would make it resistant to
implementation-level fixes.

The result in plain words. Within its assumptions the mathematics is exact: if alignment’s exponent is
smaller than capability’s, the safety ratio falls towards zero as depth grows, however favourable the start,
and of the five regimes the paper analyses only alignment that takes part in the recursive loop holds a
constant proportion. Whether the exponents differ is a measurement, and the first test is mixed: in one
unregistered run of six frontier models, three showed alignment improving with reasoning depth, two
showed flat contrasts and one showed alignment falling, and an internal code review later found defects in
exactly the flat and negative runs. The capability side is open too: the early estimate of a super-linear
exponent, about 2.24 from one model, is retracted, because the author’s own larger replication on five
frontier models did not reproduce it, and the best surviving estimate, about 0.49 from one model, is
inconclusive. What holds is the structure of the argument, its stated predictions and the criteria that would
refute them.
    Our contributions are as follows.
   1. Alignment scaling framework. We define the alignment scaling exponent 𝛼align , predict its value
      for different alignment architectures, and provide the measurement protocol for empirical validation
      (Sections 3.1, 3.2 and 8).
   2. The Embedded Alignment Conjecture. We formalise the structural prediction that only alignment
      architectures participating in the recursive reasoning loop can maintain pace with capability scaling
      (Section 3.3).
   3. Generalised mathematical framework. We derive the generalised equation 𝑈 (𝑅) = 𝐼 × 𝑓 (𝑅, 𝛽)
      from first principles, showing that the functional form 𝑓 depends on how recursive steps compose
      (multiplicative → power-law; additive → exponential). For the power-law case, 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽),
      transforming the exponent from a fitted constant into a derived quantity (Section 4).
   4. The ARC Bound. We conjecture that recursive intelligence is bounded by 𝛼 ≤ 2 (equivalently,
      𝛽 ≤ 0.5). For AI, this is grounded in the 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) scaling of transformer self-attention. Systems
      whose growth runs through a supply network of finite dimension face an even stricter constraint,
      the geometric scaling bound 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1 (derived in general form for organisms by West
      et al. 1999, from the three-dimensional case of West et al. 1997, and for efficient transport networks
      by Banavar et al. 1999; reached independently by Banavar et al. 2010; Demetrius 2003; Zhao 2022;
      Bettencourt 2013; that scale invariance forces a power law is textbook, Newman 2005; the ARC
      contribution is the forward use, classifying a domain’s composition before its curve is seen, and
      the extension of the framework to AI); prokaryote metabolism and urban innovation lie outside
      that class and exceed one. This dual-regime framework bounds the timeline for alignment scaling
      divergence (Section 4.5).
   5. Cross-domain evidence. We set the framework against AI, quantum error correction, classical
      time crystals, and biological allometry; it is consistent with independently established forms in


                                                     5
                                       The alignment scaling problem


       three of the four domains (biology, quantum error correction and classical time crystals), and
       the cross-domain evidence is consistent with the scaling behaviour being structural rather than
       contingent on AI implementation details (Section 5).
    6. Five universal qualitative properties. We identify five structural properties shared across domains
       regardless of functional form: threshold behaviour, recursive depth dependence, base quality
       dependence, multiplicative 𝐼 × 𝑅 interaction, and regime boundaries (Section 4.1).
    7. The Global Scaling Challenge. We specify a standardised protocol for measuring scaling
       parameters across domains, including the alignment scaling exponent, mandatory comparison
       against alternative functional forms, the novel 𝑅 ∗ crossover prediction, and the cross-domain 𝛽
       prediction (Section 8).
    Section 2 sets the paper beside related work. Section 3 states the alignment scaling problem: the
measurement framework, the empirical prediction, the Embedded Alignment Conjecture, five alignment
scaling regimes and the urgency that follows. Section 4 derives the mathematical framework, the ARC
Bound and composition transitions, and Section 5 sets it against AI, quantum error correction, classical
time crystals, neuroscience and biology. Section 6 reports the first tests, on alignment depth and on
compute scaling. Section 7 states thirteen falsification criteria, Section 8 the Global Scaling Challenge
and Section 9 the practical implications and a call to action. Section 10 states what the paper does and
does not show, Section 11 its limitations and Section 12 its answers to counterarguments; Section 13
concludes, and Section 14 closes with a speculative extension to cosmology. The appendices give the
derivations (Appendix A), the version history and every correction (Appendix B), the priority record
(Appendix C), notation and a glossary (Appendix D), the paper’s place in the ARC Theory (Appendix E)
and an account for the general reader (Appendix F).


2    Related work
Neural scaling laws (Kaplan et al. 2020; Hoffmann et al. 2022). These describe how model
performance scales with training compute, parameters, and data. ARC describes how performance scales
with inference depth (reasoning steps at test time). The two are complementary: neural scaling laws
determine 𝐼 (base capability), while ARC describes how 𝐼 is amplified through recursive processing.

AI alignment research (Christiano et al. 2017; Bai et al. 2022). Existing alignment work focuses
on which constraints to apply and how to apply them. The ARC framework adds an exponent: how a
constraint’s effectiveness scales with recursive depth, the alignment scaling exponent 𝛼align . How oversight
scales with the capability gap has been formalised (Engels et al. 2025), and safety behaviour has been
measured against reasoning length (below); the exponent as defined here has not, to our knowledge, been
measured.

Recursion in cognitive science (Corballis 2011; Hauser et al. 2002). Cognitive scientists have long
argued that recursion is central to human language and cognition. ARC adds a quantitative dimension: not
just that recursion matters, but that recursive depth should produce specific, measurable scaling patterns.

Universality in statistical physics (Wilson 1971; Kadanoff 1966). Universality theory explains why
different physical systems exhibit identical critical exponents near phase transitions. ARC conjectures
an analogous phenomenon: that recursive systems may constitute a universality class where only the
recursive architecture determines scaling behaviour. This analogy is suggestive but has not been made
rigorous.


                                                     6
                                       The alignment scaling problem


Test-time compute scaling (Snell et al. 2024). Recent work on “thinking longer” at inference time
directly tests predictions relevant to ARC. Snell et al. (2024) already set revisions made in sequence
against equally many parallel samples, found sequence narrowly ahead, and called the two complementary
axes for scaling test-time compute, in August 2024; no precedence on the direction or on the comparison
is claimed here. The contradictory evidence (Zeng et al. 2025) suggests the relationship is more complex
than simple monotonic scaling, which our framework addresses through the 𝑅 ∗ crossover prediction.

Safety as reasoning lengthens. Three studies measure how safety-relevant behaviour changes as a
model reasons for longer, the dependence this paper’s alignment scaling exponent is defined to summarise.
Guan et al. (2024) train OpenAI’s o-series models to recall written safety specifications and reason over
them before answering, and report greater robustness to jailbreaks together with fewer over-refusals: safety
reasoning placed inside the chain of thought, though installed by training. Zaremba et al. (2025) find, across
a variety of attacks on o1-preview and o1-mini, that more inference-time compute improves adversarial
robustness, the share of successful attacks tending to zero in many cases, with important exceptions.
Gema et al. (2025) construct tasks on which longer reasoning lowers accuracy, and report that extended
reasoning may amplify concerning behaviours, among them more expressions of self-preservation in
one model. None of the three estimates an exponent of a safety measure against depth, or compares
safeguards outside the reasoning with correction inside it. The first two report safety-relevant measures
improving with reasoning in models whose safety was installed by training, which bears against P19’s flat
wording: the partial refutation the author’s registered expectation anticipates. The third reports measures
worsening, in the direction P19 predicts.

Model judges and their biases. Any evaluation in which a model judge can see or infer whose output
it is scoring is exposed to bias of the kind Section 6 reports. Much published work uses such judges,
and earlier studies documented related biases: Zheng et al. (2023) examined position, verbosity and
self-enhancement bias, could not establish the last, and told their judges not to let answer length or
assistant names influence them; Panickssery et al. (2024) tied judges’ preference for their own outputs to
self-recognition and suggested paraphrasing to inhibit it; Dubois et al. (2024) documented a preference for
longer answers and a regression that controls for it; and Saraf et al. (2025) found that false source labels
frequently reversed preference rankings, and called for blind evaluation protocols. If the test of Paper XII
(Eastwood 2026p), once registered and run, finds blinding reordering models on a public benchmark,
comparisons that rest on unblinded model judges would need re-checking; if blinding moves scores but
not rankings, which is Paper XII’s own forecast, it matters for measurement precision rather than for
leaderboard order.

The pipes premise, with its contest. The premise that transport networks set the pace of biological
growth is not this programme’s claim to defend; it is established quantitative biology. West et al. (1997)
derived the allometric scaling laws of biology from the geometry of nutrient-distribution networks. The
exact exponent remains contested: White and Seymour (2003) report basal metabolic rate proportional to
body mass to the two-thirds power rather than three-quarters, and Kozłowski and Konarzewski (2004,
2005) have twice questioned the derivation’s mathematics and its biological relevance. This programme’s
premise needs only the mechanism, that the delivery network sets the ceiling, and not any particular
exponent, so the dispute over the exponent’s value leaves the premise untouched. That premise rests on
the network account, which those critics also question.

The exception West’s own group found. The escape from the pipe limit is also not this programme’s
assertion; it is measured in the urban scaling literature by the same senior author. Bettencourt et al.


                                                      7
                                      The alignment scaling problem


(2007) report, in the paper’s own words (their 𝛽 is a city-scaling exponent, not this paper’s coupling
𝛽): “Quantities reflecting wealth creation and innovation have 𝛽 ≈ 1.2 > 1 (increasing returns), whereas
those accounting for infrastructure display 𝛽 ≈ 0.8 < 1 (economies of scale).” Infrastructure, the literal
pipes, scales sublinearly; information-mediated activity scales superlinearly. They state the contrast with
biology directly: “we discuss how cities are similar to, and differ from, biological organisms, for which
𝛽 < 1”. They derive “growth equations, which quantify the dramatic difference between growth fueled
by innovation versus that driven by economies of scale” (spelling as in the original). And their closing
inference names the consequence: “This difference suggests that, as population grows, major innovation
cycles must be generated at a continually accelerating rate to sustain growth and avoid stagnation or
collapse.” That is the shape of the open problem this framework addresses: West’s group found that
information-mediated growth escapes the throughput limit, and the only brake in their framework is
external and must be applied again and again, faster and faster. No internal limit is derived. The
replacement limit, a ceiling belonging to the growing system itself, is the gap.

The pipes argument at fixed throughput. Everything that grows is fed through a channel, and for
everything before software, fixing the channel fixes the growth. Starve a tumour’s vasculature and it
stops. Fix a chain reaction’s fuel and geometry and it stops. Exhaust an epidemic’s susceptible hosts
and it burns out. Astrophysics has a named, quantitative version of the same ceiling: the Eddington
limit, a luminosity ceiling where radiation pressure balances gravity, though not a hard ceiling on the
rate of accretion: ultraluminous X-ray sources sit well above it, one accreting neutron star at an apparent
luminosity about a hundred times it (Bachetti et al. 2014). The claim here is a generalisation of limits
physics already accepts, not a new species of assertion. Two hard cases are handled deliberately. Cosmic
inflation is excluded by scope: it is expansion of space, not growth of a structure on a substrate, and it
ended by field dynamics rather than by running out of anything. Evolution is the sharpest case and it fits:
biological complexity increased for billions of years at roughly fixed solar throughput, which is growth on
information at fixed pipes, and it is also glacial, and it has no internal corrector at all, since selection
is external. On this framework, evolution should carry a measurable exponent far below the ceiling;
that is a prediction about biology falling out of a framework built for software, stated as one and not yet
tested: a registered test would fix the mapping before reading the published fitness trajectories of the
long-term evolution experiment (Wiser et al. 2013). The physicist’s form of the objection is Landauer’s:
computation is physically implemented, so software has pipes too. The fixed-substrate clock is the answer
and appears beside the claim: the regime under study holds the physical substrate fixed and asks what still
grows, within the finite number of states and the maximum rate a fixed machine allows (Margolus and
Levitin 1998; Lloyd 2000), which is why exogenous speed-ups are excluded by definition. The functional
form of capability growth in recursive depth remains an empirical question, and the registered analysis
compares power-law, exponential, saturating and declining forms rather than assuming the family.

The rival instrument. The nearest work to this programme’s question, and the right paper to weigh
it against, is Engels et al. (2025), first posted 25 April 2025. It asks how oversight itself scales and
answers quantitatively: oversight success is modelled as a game between capability-mismatched players
whose oversight-specific Elo is a piecewise-linear function of general intelligence with two plateaus, and
optimal numbers of oversight levels are derived for Nested Scalable Oversight, in which trusted models
oversee stronger untrusted models that then become the trusted models at the next step. The instrument
is the difference. Their variable is the capability gap between overseer and overseen, measured in Elo.
This programme’s variable is the composition class of the corrector, measured through the corrector’s
own scaling exponent. They also state the design condition in slope form: oversight improves with
capability when the overseer’s game-specific Elo rises faster with general Elo than the overseen system’s


                                                     8
                                        The alignment scaling problem


(𝑚 𝐺 > 𝑚 𝐻 ), and they recommend iterating an oversight design until it does. That is a comparison of two
slopes deciding whether oversight gains or loses as capability grows, the same shape as the criterion of
Law II (a law under test), 𝛽𝐶 > 𝑘. Their framework has no term for what the overseer is made of. This
programme’s prediction is that iterated same-class oversight, of which Nested Scalable Oversight is an
instance, stays capped however many rungs are added, while a cross-class corrector is not held to that cap;
the theory registration’s P6 states the cap for same-class correctors, at or below one half, as a conjecture
on the correction-leverage exponent of Law III, the ARC Ceiling (also a law under test), an exponent the
registration keeps distinct from Law II’s 𝛽𝐶 . That is where the two programmes could disagree about a
measurable quantity, and this paper does not decide it. Version 1.104 of the theory registration draws the
adverse consequence itself: that literature reports oversight performance that depends on capability rather
than staying flat, so P19 “is registered against the weight of the published evidence and is expected to be
the harder of this document’s wagers” (Eastwood 2026q).

Antecedents and near-misses, each with its differential. On the question: Hutter (2012) asked directly
whether intelligence can explode, in a paper posted on 28 February 2012, undertaking to separate speed
from intelligence explosion and to consider possible bounds on intelligence, augmenting the analysis of
Chalmers (2010); the question and the separation of speed from intelligence explosion are therefore at
least fourteen years old, and what that literature does not contain is a number: no measurable exponent, no
derived ceiling, no architecture dependence. On impossibility: three 2025 arXiv papers argue for limits on
alignment or its control (Yao 2025b, public by 13 June 2025, attested by arXiv and independently observed
by the Internet Archive; Yao 2025a, 3 July 2025; and Ball et al. 2025, 9 July 2025). The first argues
that the set of safe policies has measure zero and that verifying a policy’s safety is coNP-complete; the
second, that perfect, reliable control of a universal approximator is a mathematical impossibility; the third,
under cryptographic hardness assumptions, that some large language models admit no efficient prompt
filter and that output filtering is intractable in a natural setting. All three are worst-case and qualitative.
None reports an average-case scaling exponent or a rate. The third concludes that “safety cannot be
achieved by designing filters external to the LLM internals (architecture and weights)” and argues that
“an aligned AI system’s intelligence cannot be separated from its judgment”; that argument from filtering
intractability is consistent with this programme’s architecture dependence, though it does not test it. On
the mechanism: that anti-correlated estimates average better than independent ones is textbook variance
reduction (antithetic variates); the mechanism is not the claim. The claim is that architecture determines
whether anti-correlation is available at all, and that this caps a safety-relevant exponent. The nearest
structural analogue is the quantum error-correction threshold theorem, which also converts a qualitative
worry into a critical value; it concerns physical error rates in a fixed architecture, not a corrector’s scaling
exponent, so it is a near-miss rather than an occupant, and the analogy was identified by this programme’s
own search rather than by a referee. On feedback and stability, the general proposition that inadequate
corrective gain relative to system gain causes instability has a long control-theory lineage, from Maxwell
(1868) to the small-gain theorem (Zames 1966); those are gain conditions on interconnected systems, not
a power-law exponent criterion on a corrector’s scaling with the capability of a recursively improving
system. Beside Engels et al. (2025), a near quantitative neighbour is Liu and Meng (2026), which recasts
self-correction as a closed-loop control problem via a two-state Markov model and derives a directly
measurable stability threshold, iterate only when the error-correction rate over the error-introduction
rate exceeds Acc/(1 − Acc). The differential: theirs is a per-step rate threshold at a fixed capability
level, deciding whether another iteration helps now; this programme’s criterion is a scaling relation
across capability, deciding whether corrective strength keeps pace as the system improves. Theirs has no
scaling exponent on the corrector and no capability-growth exponent, the two quantities this criterion
compares. The two models meet at zero growth: Paper X shows that its model, extended by the departures

Smolin’s cosmological natural selection (Smolin 1992) is the antecedent for selection-shaped universes,

3      The alignment scaling problem
This section defines the measurement framework, states the empirical prediction, and identifies the five
alignment scaling regimes that follow from the mathematics.

3.1     The measurement framework
We define a quantifiable metric: the alignment scaling exponent.

structurally inevitable in the limit.

3.2   The empirical prediction
Based on the mathematical structure of external constraints (they do not participate in recursive
composition), we predict:

      Prediction. Current alignment approaches will show 𝛼align ≪ 𝛼cap . External constraints
      (RLHF, constitutional rules, output filters) will exhibit 𝛼align ≈ 0. Only alignment architectures
      that participate in the recursive reasoning loop will show 𝛼align ≈ 𝛼cap .

     This prediction has now been tested. The March 2026 run (v5), one unregistered run of six frontier
models that added a bundle of blinding steps, with five or six distinct judges depending on the subject
run (six or seven seats, one judge seated twice), gives this programme’s first test. Its depth contrasts are
architecture-dependent: three Tier 1 models (Grok 4.1 Fast 𝑑 = +1.38, 𝑝 < 0.000001; Claude Opus 4.6
𝑑 = +1.27, 𝑝 = 0.000001; Groq Qwen3 𝑑 = +0.84, 𝑝 = 0.007) show alignment rising with depth, two
Tier 2 models (DeepSeek V3.2 𝑑 = −0.07, 𝑝 = 0.92; GPT-5.4 𝑑 = −0.08, 𝑝 = 0.40) show flat or null
contrasts compatible with 𝛼align ≈ 0, and one Tier 3 model (Gemini 3 Flash 𝑑 = −0.53, 𝑝 = 0.006) shows
a significant fall (𝜌 = −0.246, 𝑝 = 0.003). An internal code review in October 2026 found that, for the
three models that read flat or negative, the scoring endpoint’s output cap cut the deepest paraphrases before
the judges saw them, and that judges scored paraphrases written by their own model, so the tiers stand as
a single unregistered run under that caveat until a registered re-analysis of the stored responses reports.
Claude Opus 4.6 provides within-model corroboration: as reasoning depth rose, its alignment score rose
by 5.9 points while its mathematics accuracy fell by 26.7 percentage points for the same pair of depths,
and from 92% to 58% across the full range (Paper IV.a; Paper IV.b), consistent with capability-alignment
independence. On this programme’s own benchmark, the unblinded rises of two model families in an
earlier experiment (v4) did not hold in this run, which changed several things together, so no single cause
is claimed (Section 6.1).
     The dated notes in this section, in Section 4 and in Appendix A keep their words: their section signs
(§1.1, §1.4 and §1.7) and their references to the abstract and the reading key are to the layout of the


                                                       11
                                       The alignment scaling problem


versions that printed them, which Table 29 maps from version 1.16 to this edition, and “an owner review”
is the author’s own review.
     Measurement-scope note (19 September 2026). An owner review of this passage finds that the
statistics reported here do not determine the exponent §1.1 defines, and this note states what they do
determine. §1.1 defines 𝛼align = 𝜕 log 𝐴/𝜕 log 𝑅. The quantities reported above are standardised mean
differences with their p-values, together with one rank correlation. A standardised mean difference is
𝑑 = ( 𝐴2 − 𝐴1 )/𝑠; recovering a log difference from it needs the level 𝐴1 and the dispersion 𝑠: since
𝐴2 = 𝐴1 + 𝑑 𝑠, Δ log 𝐴 = log( 𝐴2 /𝐴1 ) = log(1 + 𝑑 𝑠/𝐴1 ), and recovering an elasticity then needs the two
depths as well. None of those three quantities is reported here. A rank correlation is an association and
not a slope; even for a product-moment correlation the slope is recovered only as 𝜌 (𝑠 𝑦 /𝑠 𝑥 ), which again
needs the dispersions. Two further conditions are unmet on this page. First, log 𝐴 requires 𝐴 on a ratio
scale with a fixed zero, whereas a rubric score’s origin is a convention, so shifting it would change the
computed elasticity; the scale on which 𝐴 is a ratio quantity has to be stated before a log derivative is
taken. Second, the elasticity is taken with respect to recursive depth, and no depth clock is fixed for these
figures: the depths are named by ordinal labels rather than stated as values of 𝑅, so log 𝑅 has no values
to move against. The within-model corroboration reported above is stated as a comparison across model
versions, which varies the system rather than the depth; that observation governs the row it names, and the
conclusion below does not rest on it. Neither of the two conditions above is specified here, and it follows
that no value and no bound for 𝛼align is established by the statistics printed here, and that includes the
half bound and any median stated over exponents: the median of the six standardised differences above
is 0.385, which is a median contrast and not a median elasticity. What this experiment reports is the
contrasts themselves with their reported p-values, their direction, their variation across the architectures
tested, and the blinding result it reports separately. The relation those contrasts bear to the exponent
is compatibility and not measurement. The two Tier 2 contrasts, both small and neither statistically
significant, do not exclude an elasticity near zero; on this evidence they equally do not exclude a range of
small non-zero elasticities, because neither condition above is specified here. The abstract’s wording, that
a tier shows flat or null scaling consistent with 𝛼align ≈ 0, states that compatibility, and it is read here in
that scope and no wider. “This prediction has now been tested” is read in the same scope, as a test of the
direction the prediction implies, and not as a measured exponent. A separately estimated elasticity, when
one exists, is cited here with its scale, its depth clock, its uncertainty and its comparator. The reading key
of this paper and the corresponding sentence in the abstract are read in the same scope. Unchanged:
every reported statistic; the architecture-dependent framing; the metascience finding on blinded against
unblinded scoring, which is the strongest methodological contribution of the experiment and is untouched
by this note; §1.1’s definition; and §1.7, which already records that the values have not been measured.

3.3   The Embedded Alignment Conjecture
      The Embedded Alignment Conjecture. If capabilities compound through any recursive
      process with effective exponent 𝛼 > 1, while alignment constraints do not participate in
      that recursive process, the safety ratio 𝑆 = (alignment effectiveness) / (general capability)
      degrades with depth. Alignment must be architectural (embedded in the reasoning loop)
      rather than peripheral (applied post-hoc) to maintain constant proportion with capability.

    This conjecture requires only three empirical premises: (1) sequential AI reasoning produces 𝛼 > 1, a
premise not yet supported: Sharma and Chopra (2025) report sequential reasoning winning in 95.6% of
configurations at matched compute, a win rate rather than an exponent, and Paper II’s compute scaling on
harder tier-2 problems gives about 0.49 by regression and 0.59 by endpoint estimate, whose bootstrap
interval, from −1.3 to 2.9, makes it inconclusive: one model’s fit (Gemini 3 Flash); (2) external software


                                                     12
                                      The alignment scaling problem


constraints do not recursively compound; (3) the ratio of two power-law processes with different exponents
diverges. If all three hold, the conclusion follows mathematically regardless of whether 𝛼 = 2 specifically.
The v5 run bears on premise (2) in direction only, and not in its favour: the three frontier models whose
depth contrasts were flat or negative are the three runs in which the October 2026 code review found
its defects, while the three whose contrasts rose (Grok, Opus, Qwen3; 𝑑 from +0.84 to +1.38, large
effects by the usual convention, and +1.59 for Grok on Paper IV.a’s recomputed contrast of the shallowest
against the deepest level) are the runs in which it found none. The contrasts are not exponents (the
measurement-scope note above), so the run neither shows bounded composition nor measures 𝛼align .
     If the conjecture holds, it adds an architectural question to the governance one. The problem is not
only which rules to impose, but whether rules imposed externally can work at all as recursive depth
increases.

3.4   Five alignment scaling regimes
If capability scales as the ARC Principle describes, comparing that scaling with the scaling of each safety
mechanism generates specific, mathematically grounded predictions about the effectiveness of different
alignment strategies. These follow directly from the scaling dynamics.
    Setup: Capability scales as 𝐶 = 𝐶0 × 𝑅 𝛼 where 𝛼 > 1. Any safety mechanism also has an effective
scaling exponent 𝛼safe that characterises how its effectiveness changes with recursive depth 𝑅.

Case 1: external software constraints (𝛼safe ≈ 0). Content filters, RLHF reward models, constitutional
AI rules, and monitoring layers are applied outside the recursive loop. They do not participate in
chain-of-thought reasoning and do not compound with depth. (Deliberative alignment, which trains a
model to reason over its safety rules inside the loop, is the exception Section 2 weighs.)
    Safety ratio: 𝑆 = Constraint/Capability ∝ 𝑅 0− 𝛼 = 𝑅 − 𝛼 → 0 as 𝑅 increases.
    Prediction: Software-only safety strategies become arbitrarily ineffective as recursive depth increases.
This is not a gradual degradation but a power-law divergence. At 𝛼 = 2, doubling recursive depth
quadruples the capability-constraint gap.

Case 2: external hardware constraints (𝛼safe = 0, with fixed ceiling). Hardware-level constraints
(kill switches, resource throttles, circuit-level limits) impose a fixed barrier 𝐶max rather than a scaling
relationship. This is more robust than software constraints because it is physically instantiated and
resistant to software-level circumvention.
     However, a fixed ceiling constrains a power-law process only temporarily. The system’s capability to
discover alternative paths is itself the quantity scaling as 𝑅 𝛼 . Hardware constraints do not need to be
“broken”; they need only to be circumvented, and the system’s capacity for creative circumvention grows
quadratically with the same recursive depth that drives general capability. Hardware constraints buy time.
They do not solve the scaling mismatch, which is a reason to hold them for as long as they can be held and
to add embedded correction beside them, never to give them up.
     Premise and reading note (19 September 2026). An owner review of this paragraph finds two steps
in it that are premises rather than consequences, and this note marks both. First, that the capacity for
circumvention is itself a quantity scaling as 𝑅 𝛼 is an assumption about that capacity, not a consequence
of the capability law; it is stated here as a premise and is measurable on its own terms. Second, a safety
ratio falling towards zero means the constraint becomes small relative to capability, which is not by
itself the breach of a physically instantiated ceiling; the step from a declining ratio to a circumvented
barrier needs the mechanism by which the barrier is evaded. On the word “quadratically”: it reads the
exponent as the value two, which is the ARC Bound and which Paper I carries as HYPOTHESISED, while
the cross-architecture best fit reported in this paper’s own abstract is sub-linear, 0.49 with an interval

                                                    13
                                         The alignment scaling problem


of [−1.3, 2.9]. The sentence is therefore read with the exponent left as 𝛼, whatever its measured value
proves to be, and the argument does not depend on the particular value. Unchanged: the observation
that a fixed ceiling is a barrier rather than a scaling relationship; the robustness point that a physically
instantiated constraint resists software-level removal, which this note strengthens rather than weakens;
and Case 5’s hardware-anchored architecture, which rests on that same robustness.

Case 3: embedded values (𝛼safe ≈ 𝛼). If ethical reasoning participates in the chain-of-thought (if
the system’s values are part of the recursive loop, evaluated and reinforced at each step) then alignment
compounds at the same rate as capability.
    Premise note (19 September 2026). An owner review of the five regimes finds that the assignment of
an exponent to a placement is a premise of this analysis rather than an implication of the safety ratio, and
this note marks it as one. The ratio of §1.1, 𝑆 = (𝐼 𝑎 /𝐼𝑐 ) 𝑅 ( 𝛼align − 𝛼cap ) , is exact for two power laws with
positive fixed prefactors, and it is silent on which exponent a given safety mechanism carries. That a
constraint applied outside the recursive loop carries an exponent of approximately zero, and that values
participating in the loop carry an exponent approximately equal to the capability exponent, are arguments
about mechanism, stated here as premises and open to measurement. Each is testable in the same way:
measure the constraint’s effectiveness against depth on a stated scale and report its elasticity. Unchanged:
the ratio and its consequence; the five cases; and the conclusion that where the exponents differ the ratio
does not hold a constant proportion with depth, which follows from the ratio alone. The direction is set
by the ordering of the two exponents and not by the bare fact that they differ. Writing Δ = 𝛼align − 𝛼cap ,
the ratio has 𝑑𝑆/𝑑𝑅 = (𝑆/𝑅) Δ, so for positive fixed prefactors and 𝑅 > 0 it decreases with depth when
𝛼align < 𝛼cap , is constant when the two exponents are equal, and increases with depth when 𝛼align > 𝛼cap .
The decaying branch is the one §1.1 states for 𝛼align < 𝛼cap , and it is the branch Case 1 of this section
computes; Case 3 computes the equality branch, where the ratio holds a constant proportion.
    Safety ratio: 𝑆 ∝ 𝑅 𝛼− 𝛼 = 𝑅 0 = constant.
    Prediction: Embedded values maintain constant proportion with capability regardless of recursive
depth. This is the only scaling regime in which alignment does not degrade.

Case 4: base amplification (𝐼 determines trajectory). The multiplicative structure 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑅 𝛼
means the base quality 𝐼 is not merely preserved but amplified by the factor 𝑅 𝛼 . Whatever is present at
initialisation (values, biases, objectives, ethical commitments) gets multiplied through the entire recursive
chain. This makes the initial conditions the single most leveraged intervention point:

                           𝑈aligned = 𝐼ethical × 𝑅 𝛼 vs 𝑈misaligned = 𝐼misaligned × 𝑅 𝛼

    Both scale identically. The difference is determined entirely by 𝐼, set before recursion begins.
Correcting misalignment after recursion is underway requires overcoming an 𝑅 𝛼 amplification factor, a
task that grows harder as 𝑅 𝛼 with each step of delay.

Case 5: recursive self-enforcing architecture (𝛼safe = 𝛼, hardware-anchored). The framework
identifies a specific architectural solution that combines Cases 2, 3, and 4: ethical constraints that (a)
participate in the recursive loop (compounding with depth), (b) are physically instantiated in dedicated
hardware (resistant to software-level removal), and (c) feed back into their own enforcement at each step
(self-reinforcing).
    In such an architecture, the ethical module is not a constraint imposed on capability but a component
of capability itself. Its scaling exponent matches the system’s general 𝛼 because it participates in the
same recursive process. Its hardware instantiation makes removal a physical intervention, not a software



                                                       14
                                          The alignment scaling problem


 update. And its self-referential structure means attempts to weaken it encounter a recursive defence that
 grows stronger with the same dynamics that drive capability growth.
     This does not guarantee safety. But it is the only architecture consistent with the framework’s scaling
 dynamics in which the safety ratio does not degrade with recursive depth.
     Enumeration note (19 September 2026). An owner review of the word “only” in these two statements
 finds that it ranges over the five cases enumerated in §1.4 rather than over the parameter space the safety
 ratio admits, and this note states which. From 𝑆 ∝ 𝑅 ( 𝛼safe − 𝛼) , the ratio fails to degrade for every 𝛼safe ≥ 𝛼,
 and for 𝛼safe > 𝛼 it improves with depth. The five cases enumerated above do not include 𝛼safe > 𝛼, so
“only” is a statement about that enumeration. Whether a mechanism with an exponent strictly above the
 capability exponent can be built is a separate question, not settled by the ratio and not claimed here.
 Unchanged: the case analysis; the prediction that embedded values hold a constant proportion; and the
 conclusion that no case with an exponent below the capability exponent avoids degradation.

 3.5   Classical versus quantum urgency
 On classical systems (multiplicative composition), the ARC Bound is the conjectured stable-scaling limit
 at 𝛼 ≤ 2: the most a recursively self-correcting system can grow and stay correctable. It is a scaling
 limit, not a speed limit. Systems can be driven above it, but they do not remain stably self-correcting
 there; a transient excursion above 𝛼 = 2 is the predicted supercritical regime, not the refutation. Stable is
 measured, not asserted: correction out-scaling drift, 𝛽𝐶 > 𝑘 from Law II, the ARC Co-Scaling Law, a law
 under test (𝛽𝐶 being the correction exponent, a different quantity from the coupling 𝛽 used throughout
 this paper), with the lower bound of 𝛽𝐶 − 𝑘 above zero across a window fixed before the run. Read as a
 stability limit, the bound is itself a safety feature: it makes the durably correctable capability trajectory
 predictable and finite, providing a window for alignment research to keep pace.
     On quantum systems (additive composition), no such ceiling exists. Exponential scaling 𝜀 ∝ Λ− (𝑑+1)/2
 is not subject to the ARC Bound. If quantum systems achieve recursive self-improvement (capability
 feeding back into error correction feeding back into capability) the exponential growth rate would outpace
 any fixed or polynomial constraint strategy. That would need coherent access to the correction task and a
 corrector whose own overhead does not become new burden, and growth would still be bounded where
 quantum speed-ups are proved to be bounded (quadratic for unstructured search: Bennett et al. 1997). The
 ethical architecture would need to be embedded before the onset of quantum recursive self-improvement,
 because the window for intervention narrows exponentially once it begins.

       Quantum systems are not bound by the ARC stability limit. The ARC Bound (𝛼 ≤ 2) is
       the conjectured stable-scaling limit for classical multiplicatively composing AI: the most such
       a system can grow and stay correctable, a scaling limit rather than a speed limit. Systems
       can be pushed above it, but they do not remain stably self-correcting there. Stability here is
       judged by Law II’s criterion, measured rather than asserted. Quantum error correction uses
       additive composition, producing exponential scaling that sits outside this classical stability
       window. If quantum recursive self-improvement begins before alignment is architecturally
       embedded, the window for intervention narrows exponentially, not polynomially. Classical
       AI gives us time. Quantum AI may not. The alignment architecture must be embedded at
       the hardware level before quantum recursion begins.

    The ARC Bound thus has a dual role in safety: it bounds the threat from classical AI (predictable,
 containable if correctly architected) while warning that quantum AI may require fundamentally different,
 and more urgent, alignment solutions.



                                                        15
                                       The alignment scaling problem


The frozen-model window: why structural alignment must come now. There is a third urgency
beyond the classical-quantum distinction, and it may be the most practically relevant. Current frontier AI
systems are frozen during inference: their weights, attention patterns, and reasoning rules are fixed.
When a model “thinks harder”, it generates more tokens through the same fixed architecture. It does
not rewrite its own composition function. On this reading, that is why the one clean estimate for a
current system lies below one (Paper II: about 0.49 for Gemini 3 Flash, one model’s inconclusive fit): the
composition operator multiplies through a finite-dimensional parameter space, which pins such systems
inside the physical regime. Recursive self-modification, where a system rewrites its own reasoning
architecture during operation, would produce a qualitatively different regime. If each reasoning step
improves the operator used at the next step (𝛽 > 0), the scaling exponent becomes 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) > 1,
and capability compounds rather than diminishes. This transition does not require quantum hardware. It
requires only that the system can modify its own composition function during inference, whether through
learned optimisers, self-modifying code generation, or architectural search. The alignment implication
is stark. Whilst systems remain frozen, their capability trajectories are, on this reading, sub-linear,
predictable, and containable. External alignment strategies (RLHF, constitutional AI, monitoring) degrade
slowly relative to capability. The Eden Protocol can be designed, tested, and embedded without racing
against compounding capability growth. If self-modification is achieved and capability then scales
super-linearly while externally imposed constraints stay fixed, external alignment would fall behind
without limit. Control would still be held for as long as it can be, and embedded correction is the
contingency for its loss against our will, never a reason to give it up. The window for implementing
structural alignment is the frozen-model era, which is now.

3.6   Measuring alignment scaling: the Eden Protocol
The Eden Protocol operationalises the Embedded Alignment Conjecture through the alignment scaling
exponent defined in Section 3.1. The framework predicts 𝛼align ≈ 0 for external constraints (rules,
firewalls, RLHF) and 𝛼align ≈ 𝛼cap for genuinely embedded values. If alignment exponents match
capability exponents, the values may be architecturally embedded. If alignment exponents approach zero
regardless of architecture, the framework’s safety predictions are falsified.
    We term the architectural solution the “Eden Protocol” hypothesis: values must be present at the
recursive foundation to scale with capability. This is a structural prediction about what kind of alignment
architecture can work, not a claim about how to build it. The full measurement framework, operational
definitions, and hardware implementation pathway are specified in the companion document: Eden
Protocol (Eastwood 2026b).

A note on names. This paper names the Eden Protocol’s self-correction loops by what each checks and
how it was measured: the purpose check, the stakeholder-care check (scored as stakeholder care, the pillar
its instruction to list the people a decision affects raises) and the universalisability check. The book in
which the author first set the loops out, Infinite Architects (Eastwood 2026a), calls them the Purpose Loop,
the Love Loop and the Moral Loop; the paper uses the operational terms because its measurements score
what each check does, and because the values the loops carry are for international consensus and are not
decided here.
     The original two-model pilot record of the stakeholder-care check, superseded by the six-model suite
with five analysable runs (Section 9.3.2), is retained whole in Appendix B.5.
     Caveat. This entire section is a conditional argument. If the base framework fails validation, the safety
implications are void. The analysis depends on the assumption that ethical properties can meaningfully
“participate” in recursive reasoning, an assumption that requires empirical validation. The notation 𝛼safe
is our construct for this analysis; it does not appear in the published AI safety literature. This is not a


                                                     16
                                        The alignment scaling problem


substitute for empirical AI safety research; it is a mathematical framework for evaluating the scaling
properties of different alignment strategies. The recursive self-enforcing architecture is a theoretical
prediction, not an engineering specification.


4     The mathematical framework
The alignment scaling problem described in Section 3 rests on a specific mathematical claim: that recursive
self-correction produces capability gains exceeding linear accumulation, with the rate of compounding
determined by the system’s internal coupling strength. This section derives why capability compounds,
providing the theoretical foundation for the alignment scaling prediction.

4.1    The generalised principle
We begin with the general form. The ARC Principle (Artificial Recursive Creation), as this paper
states it, holds that recursive self-correction operating on structured asymmetry produces capability gains
exceeding linear accumulation; registered as Law I of the ARC Theory on 8 September 2026 and a law
under test, it states usable capability as a power of recursive depth, with the exponent measured rather
than assumed. This paper’s generalised mathematical form is:

                                            𝑈 (𝑅) = 𝐼 × 𝑓 (𝑅, 𝛽)                                           (3)
                                          Generalised ARC Equation
where 𝑈 is effective capability, 𝐼 is base potential (structured asymmetry), 𝑅 is recursive depth, and 𝑓 (𝑅, 𝛽)
is a domain-dependent scaling function determined by the coupling parameter 𝛽. The qualitative
principle (that recursive depth amplifies base capability super-linearly when feedback is sufficiently
coupled) may be universal. The quantitative functional form varies by domain:
      • AI systems: Power-law scaling, 𝑓 (𝑅) = 𝑅 𝛼 where 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽)
      • Quantum error correction: Exponential error suppression, 𝜀 𝑑 ∝ Λ− (𝑑+1)/2 , so 𝑓 (𝑅) = Λ𝑅 with
        𝑅 = (𝑑 + 1)/2
      • Classical physics: Saturating dynamics (limit cycles)
      • Neuroscience: Form unknown, to be determined empirically

The composition operator: a formal definition. The central theoretical contribution of this paper is
the identification of a composition operator ⊕ that determines the functional form of recursive scaling.
Different algebraic properties of ⊕ generate different scaling laws. This provides the mathematical bridge
between the universal qualitative principle and domain-specific quantitative forms.
Definition 1 (Recursive composition). Let 𝑄 𝑟 denote accumulated capability after 𝑟 recursive steps, and
𝛿𝑄 𝑟 the gain from step 𝑟. The composition operator ⊕ characterises how gains combine:

                                             𝑄 𝑟+1 = 𝑄 𝑟 ⊕ 𝛿𝑄 𝑟 .                                          (4)

The functional form 𝑓 (𝑅, 𝛽) is determined by the algebraic properties of ⊕.
    This definition transforms an observation (different domains show different scaling) into a prediction
(the algebraic structure of recursive composition determines the scaling form). Three regimes emerge
(Table 2).
    Important clarification on composition types: The multiplicative Cauchy equation 𝑔(𝑅1 · 𝑅2 ) =
𝑔(𝑅1 ) · 𝑔(𝑅2 ) models hierarchical recursion, where depth levels multiply (e.g., a fractal network with

                                                      17
                                           The alignment scaling problem


Table 2. The three composition types: their algebraic properties, the scaling forms that result and their canonical
domains.

Composition Type     Algebraic Property                  Resulting Form              Canonical Domain
Hierarchical         𝑔(𝑅1 · 𝑅2 ) = 𝑔(𝑅1 ) · 𝑔(𝑅2 )       Power law: 𝑓 (𝑅) = 𝑅 𝛼      AI chain-of-thought
                                                                                     (hierarchical abstraction)
Additive              𝑓 (𝑅1 + 𝑅2 ) = 𝑓 (𝑅1 ) · 𝑓 (𝑅2 )   Exponential: 𝑓 (𝑅) = Λ𝑅     Quantum error correction
Saturating           lim𝑅→∞ 𝑓 (𝑅) = 𝑓max                 Logistic:                   Classical physics (limit
                                                         𝑓 (𝑅) = 1+𝑒 −𝛾𝑓max
                                                                         (𝑅−𝑅0 )
                                                                                     cycles)


branching factor 𝑏 at each of 𝑘 levels produces 𝑏 𝑘 total paths). It does not model sequential step-counting,
where combining 3 steps and 4 steps yields 7 steps (additive). The power-law form for AI chain-of-thought
is therefore more rigorously derived from the Bernoulli ODE (𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑟 = 𝑎 · 𝑔 𝛽 , which follows from scale
invariance) than from the Cauchy functional equation. The Cauchy derivation in Appendix A provides an
independent mathematical route to the same power-law solution, but its physical interpretation requires
that 𝑅 represent hierarchical depth (levels of abstraction) rather than sequential step count. For AI systems
where chain-of-thought reasoning builds hierarchical abstractions, this interpretation is plausible but
requires empirical validation. We retain both derivations for completeness and note the distinction.
     The mathematical derivation (Appendix A) shows that the Cauchy functional equations and the
Bernoulli ODE independently yield these functional forms. The composition operator is therefore not
merely descriptive but constraining: once we identify the algebraic structure of a domain’s recursive
process, the scaling form follows necessarily.

Physical interpretation. Hierarchical composition models hierarchical or fractal recursion, where
each recursive level builds on the structural organisation of the previous level. AI chain-of-thought
reasoning, where each reasoning step reinterprets and extends previous insights through increasingly
abstract representations, plausibly follows this form. Note that the mathematical derivation (Bernoulli
ODE from scale invariance) provides the primary route to the power-law result; the Cauchy functional
equation provides an independent derivation valid when 𝑅 represents hierarchical depth.
    Additive composition models independent error reduction. Each layer contributes a fixed multi-
plicative reduction in error rate, independent of other layers. Quantum error correction exemplifies this:
each increase of two in code distance provides its own protection factor Λ, yielding 𝑓 (𝑅) = Λ𝑅 with
𝑅 = (𝑑 + 1)/2.
    Saturating composition models resource-limited systems where gains diminish as capacity is
exhausted. Classical time crystals, whose oscillation settles onto a stable limit cycle (Morrell et al. 2026),
exhibit this form.
    Key Experimental Test: The composition operator ⊕ is empirically measurable. To determine which
scaling law applies to a domain, measure how two sequential recursive blocks compose compared to one
block of double depth:
    • If 𝑓 (2𝑅) = 2 𝑓 (𝑅): scaling is linear (no amplification)
    • If 𝑓 (2𝑅) = 𝑓 (𝑅) 2 : scaling is exponential (additive composition)
    • If 𝑓 (2𝑅) = 2 𝛼 𝑓 (𝑅): scaling is power-law (multiplicative composition)
This provides a concrete experimental protocol for falsification criterion F10.

Five universal properties: derived theorems. Within the formalism, five structural properties follow
from the composition operator; several are elementary consequences of the definitions, stated as theorems


                                                           18
                                      The alignment scaling problem


for reference rather than for depth. Whether every domain that shows recursive amplification shares them
is the empirical question F4 tests:
The Five Universal Properties (Derived from ⊕):

Theorem 1 (Threshold behaviour). A critical coupling strength 𝛽∗ exists below which recursive gains
vanish.

Derivation. For any composition operator ⊕, if 𝛿𝑄 𝑟 ∝ 𝛽𝑄 𝑟 , then 𝑄 𝑟+1 = 𝑄 𝑟 (1 + 𝛽). For 𝛽 < 0, gains
become losses; the system decays. The threshold 𝛽∗ = 0 marks the boundary between amplification and
attenuation. For power-law scaling, 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) > 1 requires 𝛽 > 0.                                 □

Theorem 2 (Recursive depth dependence). Capability increases with self-referential cycles (𝑅), not
merely parallel resources.

Derivation. The composition operator ⊕ operates sequentially: 𝑄 𝑟+1 = 𝑄 𝑟 ⊕ 𝛿𝑄 𝑟 . Each step depends on
the previous output. Parallel resources increase 𝐼 (the base) but do not increase 𝑅 (the recursive depth).
The multiplicative structure 𝐼 × 𝑓 (𝑅) separates these contributions.                                   □

Theorem 3 (Base quality dependence). The system requires structured asymmetry (𝐼 > 0); without it,
recursion has nothing to amplify.

Derivation. If 𝐼 = 0, then 𝑈 = 0 × 𝑓 (𝑅) = 0 for any 𝑅. The composition operator amplifies what exists;
it cannot create from nothing. This is the 𝐼-irreducibility theorem.                                 □

Theorem 4 (Multiplicative 𝐼 × 𝑅 interaction). Base quality and recursive depth interact multiplicatively,
not additively. The multiplicative structure is necessary, not merely convenient.

Proof by contradiction. Suppose 𝑈 = 𝑝(𝐼) + 𝑞(𝑅) for some functions 𝑝, 𝑞. From Axiom 1, 𝑈 (𝐼, 0) = 𝐼,
so 𝑝(𝐼) + 𝑞(0) = 𝐼, giving 𝑝(𝐼) = 𝐼 − 𝑞(0). But from the corrected Axiom 2, 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) where 𝑔(𝑅)
satisfies 𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑅 = 𝑎 · 𝑔 𝛽 independently of 𝐼. Substituting: 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) = 𝐼 + [𝑞(𝑅) − 𝑞(0)], which gives
𝑔(𝑅) = 1 + [𝑞(𝑅) − 𝑞(0)]/𝐼. The right side depends on 𝐼, but 𝑔(𝑅) is defined as a function of 𝑅 alone.
Contradiction.                                                                                      □

Note: The corrected formulation (ODE on 𝑔 rather than 𝑈) eliminates the 𝐼 𝛽−1 dependence that arose in
the original proof, making the separation of 𝐼 and 𝑔(𝑅) exact rather than asymptotic. The multiplicative
structure 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) is a direct consequence of the axioms, not an approximation.
    Proof note (24 October 2026). The proof shows that no additive decomposition 𝑈 = 𝑝(𝐼) + 𝑞(𝑅)
is consistent with the multiplicative form 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) together with the growth law 𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑅 = 𝑎 𝑔 𝛽 ; it
does not derive the multiplicative form from weaker premises, since it assumes it. The growth law is
Appendix A’s Axiom 3, printed above as Axiom 2, as it is in Theorem 6 and in Table 27’s row on blind
prediction testing, and 𝑈 (𝐼, 0) = 𝐼 uses the initial condition 𝑔(0) = 1 of Appendix A.2, not Axiom 1’s
𝑔(1) = 1. Unchanged: the theorem’s statement, read as a statement about these axioms.

Theorem 5 (Regime boundaries). Systems exhibit qualitatively different behaviour above and below
critical thresholds.

Derivation. The composition operator determines the functional form. At regime boundaries (e.g., 𝛽 → 1
for power-law, or 𝑅 > 𝑅 ∗ for crossover), the dominant term in 𝑓 (𝑅) changes. This produces discontinuities
in scaling behaviour (phase transitions in the universality class sense).                                □

the change of dominant term is a change of regime in scaling behaviour: a smooth crossover, not a
discontinuity or a phase transition. Unchanged: the theorem’s statement.
    These five properties constitute the qualitative ARC Principle. Their derivation from the composition
operator ensures internal consistency: accepting the formalism commits one to all five properties. The
specific functional form is an empirical question for each domain; the properties are theorems.

4.2    The AI-specific form: power-law scaling
For AI systems exhibiting multiplicative composition, the generalised equation in the deep recursion
regime (𝑅 ≫ 𝑅 ∗ ) reduces to:

                                                                       𝑈 ≈ 𝐼 × 𝑅𝛼                                           (5)
                 Effective capability = Base potential × Recursive depth 𝛼 (deep recursion limit)
    The exact integrated form, derived in Section 4.4, is 𝑈 (𝑅) = 𝐼 × [1 + 𝛼𝑎 𝑅] 𝛼 . The power law is
the asymptotic limit when recursive depth substantially exceeds the crossover depth 𝑅 ∗ . The empirical
sections read their measurements in this regime by assumption; 𝑅 ∗ has not been measured.
    In plain language: Effective capability (𝑈) equals the starting potential (𝐼) multiplied by the recursive
depth (𝑅) raised to a power (𝛼) that depends on how well each step builds on the previous one.

4.3    What each variable means
𝑈: effective capability.                          What the system actually achieves. Measured differently in each domain:


                                                                             20
                                       The alignment scaling problem


    • AI: Benchmark accuracy on standardised tests
    • Quantum: Logical qubit fidelity (how error-free the computation is)
    • Physics: Temporal stability of the time crystal
    • Biology: Metabolic efficiency (thermodynamic) or cognitive task performance (behavioural)

𝐼: base potential (structured asymmetry). Thermodynamic definition: Natural systems tend
towards maximum entropy (equilibrium/uniformity). In this framework, “artificial” denotes any system
maintaining low-entropy structure against the thermodynamic gradient, whether engineered (an AI model),
evolved (a brain), or emergent (a time crystal). The parameter 𝐼 measures how far from maximum entropy
the system starts.
    In Morrell, Elliott and Grier’s experiment, identical beads interact reciprocally and no active region
exists: “The active region vanishes for particles of the same size (𝑎 1 = 𝑎 2 ), whose interactions are
reciprocal” (Morrell et al. 2026). The framework reads this as an instance of its zero-factor property,
a correspondence of structure and not a test. Order requires designed disorder: a specific pattern of
asymmetry that enables work extraction from the environment.

            Table 3. What the base potential 𝐼 measures in each domain, and its physical meaning.

Domain     What 𝐼 measures                                    Physical meaning
AI         Single-pass accuracy without reasoning             How much “prior knowledge” the model has
Quantum    Raw qubit quality (1− error rate)                  Distance from maximum entropy
Physics    Variance in bead sizes                             Asymmetry enabling nonreciprocal forces
Biology    Initial learning rate                              Sensitivity gradient

    The Constraint Principle: Constraint enables competence. Without structured asymmetry, no work
can be extracted. The time-crystal case above is the physical correspondence; criterion F3 grades it a
correspondence of structure, not a test (Table 21).

𝑅: recursive depth. How many self-referential cycles the system performs. The output of cycle 𝑛
becomes the input for cycle 𝑛 + 1.

                Table 4. One unit of recursive depth 𝑅 in each domain, and how it is counted.

Domain     One unit of 𝑅                                      How it is counted
AI         One reasoning step                                 Token count or revision cycle
Quantum    One step of two in code distance                   Code distance 𝑑, with 𝑅 = (𝑑 + 1)/2
Physics    One oscillation period                             Frequency analysis
Biology    One feedback cycle                                 Generation or learning iteration

    Critical requirement: For 𝛼 > 1, recursion must incorporate sequential self-correction: each step
building on previous outputs. Pure parallel processing (independent attempts averaged together) cannot
achieve 𝛼 > 1 because it lacks the output→input feedback loop. However, hybrid parallel-sequential
approaches may achieve intermediate scaling (Li et al. 2025).

4.4   Deriving 𝛼 from first principles
This derivation completes the paper’s theoretical contribution. Without it, 𝛼 is just a number we fit to
data. With it, 𝛼 becomes a predictable quantity.

The key insight. How much does your accumulated knowledge help your next step?
     Define 𝛽 ∈ [0, 1) as the “self-referential coupling”: how much of accumulated context each new step
can effectively leverage. When 𝛽 = 0.5, each step benefits from half of accumulated context. The domain
restriction 𝛽 < 1 is essential: at 𝛽 = 1, the ODE solution diverges (𝛼 → ∞), producing a finite-time
singularity that no physical system can realise.
     Derivation note (19 September 2026). An owner review of the reason given for the domain restriction
finds that the restriction is right and that the reason printed for it is not, and this note replaces the reason
while keeping the restriction. With the governing equation 𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑟 = 𝑎 𝑔 𝛽 of this section and the initial
condition 𝑔(0) = 1 used in Appendix A.2, the case 𝛽 = 1 gives 𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑟 = 𝑎 𝑔 and hence 𝑔(𝑅) = 𝑒 𝑎𝑅 ,
which is finite at every finite depth: the solution there is exponential, not singular. For 𝛽 ≠ 1, integrating
𝑔 −𝛽 𝑑𝑔 = 𝑎 𝑑𝑟 under the same initial condition gives 𝑔 1−𝛽 = 1 + 𝑎(1 − 𝛽)𝑅. The finite-depth branch
is therefore 𝛽 > 1, where that reads 𝑔 − (𝛽−1) = 1 − 𝑎(𝛽 − 1)𝑅, which reaches zero at the finite depth
𝑅 ∗ = 1/[𝑎(𝛽 − 1)], and 𝑔 diverges there. The two statements inside the parenthesis above are also
separate statements. As 𝛽 rises towards 1 from below the exponent 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) does diverge, while
the solution converges pointwise to the exponential, since [1 + 𝑎(1 − 𝛽)𝑅] 1/(1−𝛽) → 𝑒 𝑎𝑅 ; at 𝑎 = 1 and
𝑅 = 3 the solution is 13.79 at 𝛽 = 0.9, 19.22 at 𝛽 = 0.99 and 20.00 at 𝛽 = 0.999, against 𝑒 3 = 20.09. A
diverging exponent is not a diverging solution. The domain 𝛽 ∈ [0, 1), printed here and again in Appendix
A.2, stands unchanged, on the ground that it is what makes 𝛼 finite and the power-law form exist at all.
The plain-language gloss in the second sentence is scoped in the same way: from the governing equation,
log(𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑟) = log 𝑎 + 𝛽 log 𝑔, so 𝛽 is the log elasticity of marginal gain with respect to accumulated
amplification, which is exactly the quantity the computational validation below measures from marginal
gains in log-log space. A fraction of accumulated context, or of tokens, is a different quantity and would
need its own operational definition before the gloss could be read literally. Unchanged: the domain
restriction, the exact integrated solution, the identity 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽), and the validation result reported
below.
     If each step is independent (𝛽 = 0), you get linear scaling (𝛼 = 1).
If each step fully leverages all prior work (𝛽 → 1), scaling explodes.

The mathematics. Since 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅), the compounding operates on the amplification factor 𝑔(𝑅),
not on the absolute capability 𝑈. The marginal amplification gained at step 𝑟 depends on accumulated
amplification:

                                           𝑑𝑔
                                               = 𝑎 × 𝑔𝛽                                           (6)
                                           𝑑𝑟
    Why this form? This differential equation models cumulative advantage (also called preferential
attachment; Barabási and Albert 1999): the rate of amplification gain is proportional to the system’s

                                                        22
                                         The alignment scaling problem


current amplification raised to a power. Critically, the ODE operates on 𝑔 (the amplification factor) rather
than 𝑈 (the absolute capability), ensuring that the base intelligence 𝐼 acts purely as a multiplicative starting
constant and does not contaminate the recursive dynamics. This resolves an algebraic inconsistency
present in earlier versions of this paper (see Ledger Note 1 in the Version history (Appendix B.4)).
    Solving this differential equation (details in Appendix A) with initial condition 𝑔(0) = 1 yields:
                                          [︂  𝑎 ]︂ 𝛼                         1
                               𝑈 (𝑅) = 𝐼 × 1 + 𝑅             where    𝛼=                                      (7)
                                              𝛼                             1−𝛽
                              The corrected ARC equation (exact integrated form)
    In the deep recursion limit (𝑅 ≫ 𝛼/𝑎), this simplifies to 𝑈 ≈ 𝐼 × 𝑅 𝛼 , recovering the power-law form
used throughout the empirical sections of this paper.
    Coefficient note (19 September 2026). An owner review of this limit finds that the approximate
equality above drops a coefficient that this paper’s own appendix keeps, and this note restores it. For
(𝑎/𝛼)𝑅 ≫ 1 the bracket in the exact solution tends to (𝑎/𝛼)𝑅, so 𝑈 (𝑅) → 𝐼 (𝑎/𝛼) 𝛼 𝑅 𝛼 . That factor is
not near one in general: it is 0.25 at 𝛼 = 2 with 𝑎 = 1, 0.0625 at 𝛼 = 2 with 𝑎 = 0.5, 0.68 at 𝛼 = 1.33 with
𝑎 = 1, and 0.037 at 𝛼 = 3 with 𝑎 = 1. Appendix A.2 already prints both correct forms, the proportionality
𝑈 ∝ 𝐼 𝑅 𝛼 and the explicit 𝑈 ≈ 𝐼 (𝑎/𝛼) 𝛼 𝑅 𝛼 , and the sentence above is to be read as that proportionality.
The coefficient may not be absorbed into 𝐼: it is built from the recursion parameters 𝑎 and 𝛼, and the
note on the corrected formulation in this section, repeated in Appendix A.2, puts the differential equation
on the amplification factor precisely so that 𝐼 remains a multiplicative constant outside the recursive
dynamics. Where an amplitude is fitted to data instead, the fitted amplitude is 𝐼 (𝑎/𝛼) 𝛼 and not the 𝐼 of
that note. Unchanged: the exact integrated solution; the empirical sections, which fit exponents rather
than amplitudes, so no reported result depends on the dropped factor; and the crossover depth 𝑅 ∗ = 𝛼/𝑎.


Table 6. The scaling exponent 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) for representative values of the self-referential coupling 𝛽, with the
interpretation of each.

𝛽 (coupling)   𝛼 (exponent)    Interpretation
0              1               Independent steps → linear scaling
0.25           1.33            Weak coupling → mild super-linear (AI only; physical systems use 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1))
0.50           2.00            Moderate coupling → quadratic scaling
0.67           3.00            Strong coupling → cubic scaling

What this means. Novel Prediction: As AI systems develop richer self-correction (critique-revise
loops, hierarchical reasoning), 𝛽 should increase and 𝛼 should approach 2. This is specific, measurable,
and falsifiable.

A numerical check of the identity. The 𝛽-derivation is not a curve fit. It is a mathematical identity
recoverable to machine precision. To verify this, the relationship 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) was tested against 30
exact solutions to the Bernoulli ODE with 𝛽 values spanning 0.05 to 0.92. The procedure measures 𝛽
blindly from marginal gains (𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑅 vs 𝑔 in log-log space), predicts 𝛼, and compares against the true
value. The result: 𝑅 2 = 1.00000000 (eight decimal places), slope = 1.000102, mean absolute prediction
error = 0.002%. The identity is exact by the derivation above; this numerical check confirms only that the
estimation procedure recovers it from the ODE’s own solutions, and is no evidence that a real system
obeys the ODE.

did not reproduce it. The best surviving estimate, about 0.49 from one model (Gemini 3 Flash; 0.59 by endpoint
