4.5     The ARC Bound
The ARC framework makes a central, falsifiable prediction: for classical sequential recursive systems,
the scaling exponent is bounded at 𝛼max = 2. For attention-based AI architectures, this bound is argued
from the 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) computational complexity of self-attention. Whether it holds as a general bound for all
classical sequential systems is a conjecture, not a theorem.

4.5.1   Scale-free derivation
The result follows from a single assumption: scale invariance. Consider any system where capability 𝑈
depends on recursive depth 𝑅 and base quality 𝐼. If the system is scale-free (exhibiting no intrinsic length
scale), then the governing dynamics must satisfy:

                                                                      𝑑𝑔
                                                                         = 𝑎 · 𝑔𝛽                                       (8)
                                                                      𝑑𝑟
                                 Bernoulli ODE operating on amplification factor (scale-invariant form)
    This is the unique ordinary differential equation consistent with scale invariance operating on the
amplification factor. Any other form (exponential, logarithmic, polynomial with fixed coefficients)
introduces an implicit scale and thus violates the symmetry.
    The solution is 𝑔(𝑟) = [1 + 𝛼𝑎 𝑟] 𝛼 where 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽). In the deep recursion limit, 𝑔(𝑟) ∝ 𝑟 1/(1−𝛽) .
The derivation chain is:
   1. Scale invariance forces the Bernoulli form
   2. Bernoulli ODE yields power-law solutions
   3. Power-law exponent is determined by coupling 𝛽



                                                                           24
                                      The alignment scaling problem


   4. Computational complexity of self-attention bounds 𝛽 ≤ 0.5 for attention-based architectures
      (below)
   5. Therefore 𝛼 ≤ 2 for classical sequential systems (argued, not proven, for attention-based architec-
      tures; conjectured generally)

      The ARC Bound (a novel prediction). For recursive intelligence, the scaling exponent is
      bounded by 𝛼 ≤ 2 (equivalently, 𝛽 ≤ 0.5). For attention-based architectures, the 𝑂 (𝑁 2 )
      scaling of self-attention provides the concrete computational basis: each reasoning step
      can cross-reference at most 𝑁 previous steps, producing at most quadratic information
      density. This is conjectured to hold for classical sequential systems generally. Systems whose
      growth runs through a supply network of finite dimension face an even stricter constraint, the
      geometric scaling bound 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1, derived in general form by West et al. (1999),
      from the three-dimensional case of West, Brown, and Enquist (1997), and by Banavar et al.
      (1999), and reached independently by Banavar et al. (2010), Demetrius (2003), Zhao (2022)
      and Bettencourt (2013); the companion paper On the Origin of Scaling Laws (Eastwood
      2026f) identifies why these derivations converge via Cauchy’s functional equations. Quantum
      systems, employing additive composition in Hilbert space, are not subject to either bound.
      This prediction has not been independently tested. We provide the falsification criterion
      (F11).

     The initial estimate 𝛼 ≈ 2.2 from the author’s Paper II (Eastwood 2026h) experiment (𝑁 = 12, 95%
CI 1.5 to 3.0; the DeepSeek R1 run of 21 January 2026) was tested across the five frontier models that
make up Paper II’s capability arm with 30 problems (Paper II, March 2026). Caveat: A point estimate
of 𝛼 = 2.2 sits above the bound the framework predicts, 𝛼 ≤ 2 (the ARC Bound, criteria F5 and F11).
However, the 95% confidence interval [1.5, 3.0] is wide enough to be consistent with both the theory
and its negation, and should not be treated as confirmatory. The author retracted it when his own larger
replication on five frontier models, the capability arm of a six-model study, did not reproduce it; Paper II
attributes it to a small sample, a single model and a compressed range of accuracies. The best surviving
estimate is about 0.49 by regression and 0.59 by endpoint estimate, whose bootstrap interval, from −1.3
to 2.9, makes it inconclusive: one model’s fit (Gemini 3 Flash).

Computational argument for the ARC Bound. The quadratic limit can be grounded in a concrete
computational mechanism. In current AI architectures, the dominant recursive process is self-attention,
where each new token (reasoning step) attends to and cross-references every previous token. The
computational complexity of this operation scales as 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ), where 𝑁 is the sequence length. This means
the maximum density of causal interactions in a classical sequential system is quadratic: each additional
step can, at most, create 𝑁 new connections to all previous steps.
    This provides a principled basis for the 𝛼 = 2 bound:
   1. Attention complexity: Transformer self-attention scales as 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) with sequence length, estab-
      lishing a quadratic ceiling on the information-processing density achievable through sequential
      recursion.
   2. Coupling interpretation: At 𝛽 = 0.5, each step leverages exactly half of accumulated context, the
      efficiency point at which the attention mechanism saturates its cross-referencing capacity relative to
      the computational cost of deeper recursion.
   3. Empirical status: The initial 𝛼 ≈ 2.2 estimate (𝑁 = 12, 95% CI 1.5 to 3.0) from Paper II exceeded
      the predicted bound of 𝛼 ≤ 2. However, the 95% confidence interval [1.5, 3.0] is wide enough to
      be consistent with both the theory and its negation, and this single-model estimate should not be

                                                    25
                                       The alignment scaling problem


      treated as confirmatory. Paper II (March 2026), which broadened the measurement to cover the
      whole capability arm of five frontier models and 30 problems (18 AIME-level) with bootstrap CIs
      and cross-verification, did not reproduce it, and the author retracted it; the best surviving estimate is
      about 0.49, one model’s inconclusive fit (Gemini 3 Flash; Section 6.2). The ARC Bound is neither
      confirmed nor refuted by current data.
     Scope of the bound: The 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) argument is offered for the quadratic limit in attention-based
architectures specifically, and is an argument, not a proof. Whether 𝛼 ≤ 2 holds as a universal bound
for all classical sequential recursive systems is a conjecture, not a theorem. Non-attention architectures
(state-space models, recurrent networks) may have different computational ceilings. The quadratic limit
should be understood as: (a) argued, not proven, for 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) attention mechanisms, (b) conjectured
for classical sequential systems generally, and (c) explicitly not applying to quantum systems, which
employ additive composition and achieve exponential scaling. Previous versions of this paper offered
two additional arguments for the bound (elasticity analysis and an edge-of-chaos derivation). These have
been withdrawn: elasticity exceeding unity does not imply dynamical instability, and a one-dimensional
autonomous ODE cannot be chaotic, since its trajectories are monotone. The 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) argument is both
more concrete and more defensible.
     The ARC Bound therefore identifies a conjectured stable-scaling limit: the most a recursively
self-correcting classical system can grow while remaining self-correcting, a scaling limit set by the
information-processing geometry of the substrate rather than by engineering, and not a speed limit.
Limits of this kind are familiar from thermodynamics: a ceiling on a class of mechanism, fixed by
the mechanism’s own geometry, which no engineering within the class can exceed. Systems operating
under classical sequential recursion approach 𝛼 = 2 as their stable upper bound. Systems can be driven
above it, but they do not remain stably self-correcting there; a transient excursion above the bound is the
predicted supercritical regime, not the refutation. Stability here is judged by Law II’s criterion (a law
under test), measured rather than asserted. Escaping the bound stably requires a fundamentally different
computational substrate (quantum coherence, additive composition), just as escaping a mechanism-class
ceiling in thermodynamics requires a different mechanism class, not a better instance of the old one.

Universality across domains. The ARC Bound applies wherever multiplicative composition governs
the system dynamics:
    • AI systems: Chain-of-thought reasoning with iterative refinement (each step builds multiplicatively
      on prior context)
    • Biological networks: Fractal transport systems (circulatory, respiratory, neural branching)
    • Economic systems: Hierarchical organisations with multiplicative value chains
    • Physical networks: Scale-free graphs with preferential attachment
    The quantum exception (exponential scaling with Λ ≈ 2.14) arises because quantum error correction
employs additive composition: independent syndrome measurements combine additively, not multiplicat-
ively. Additive composition yields exponential functional forms, which are not subject to the ARC Bound.
The composition operator, not the domain, determines the bound.
    A ceiling on a class of mechanism: The ARC Bound is a mechanism-class ceiling of the kind
thermodynamics made familiar. Just as a heat engine’s maximum efficiency is fixed by its operating
temperatures regardless of engineering, on this argument no attention-based sequential recursive system
would exceed 𝛼 = 2, because transformer self-attention scales as 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ). The analogy holds in the part
that matters: a mechanism-class ceiling binds its class, and a device built on a different mechanism
is not bound by it at all. Both are ceilings on a class of mechanism, not statements about what is

physically possible. Individual systems may fall short due to imperfect coupling, noise, or architectural
constraints (and physically embedded systems face the stricter geometric scaling bound of Section 5.6),
but the ceiling for classical sequential computation is fixed by the quadratic geometry of cross-referencing.
A system driven above that ceiling does not stay coherent there, so a durably stable measurement
above 𝛼 = 2 would refute the bound under F5, while an unstable excursion would be consistent with
it; F11, the earlier criterion, refutes on a sustained magnitude alone. A ceiling of this kind tells us
when to stop optimising within a paradigm and when a fundamentally different approach (e.g., additive
composition, quantum coherence) is required to exceed it.
     Critically, the contradictory evidence reviewed in Section 5.2 (Zeng et al. 2025; Li et al. 2025) may
indicate that:
    • 𝛼 varies with task difficulty, model architecture, or prompt structure
    • There exists an optimal reasoning length beyond which returns diminish (the “thinking long but
      short” phenomenon)
    • Different benchmarks probe different coupling regimes
    Resolving these discrepancies is a central empirical question for the Global Scaling Challenge
(Section 8).
    Caveat. The numerical similarity between the ARC Bound’s 𝛼 = 2 and Λ ≈ 2.14 (quantum) may be
entirely coincidental. These are mathematically distinct quantities from different functional forms. Any
deeper connection would require theoretical justification that does not currently exist.

The 𝛽-transition phase diagram. The generalised framework implies a phase diagram of recursive
scaling. The axes are base quality (𝐼, normalised) and coupling strength (𝛽). Four regimes emerge:
    • Diminishing returns: 𝛽 < 𝛽∗ (threshold) or 𝐼 below threshold. More recursion hurts or provides

    • Saturating regime: Any 𝛽 with energy dissipation. Compounding onset → limit cycle. (Time

    Note: These boundary values are schematic placements, not observations: no 𝛽 has been measured
in AI systems (the early estimate is retracted and the surviving one implies no 𝛽 in [0, 1)), and quantum
error correction’s parameter is Λ, not 𝛽. The precise boundaries require empirical determination via the
Global Scaling Challenge.

Cross-domain 𝛽 prediction: the killer test. The framework makes a specific, falsifiable cross-domain
prediction that distinguishes analogy from unified theory:
The 𝛽-Convergence Prediction: In any domain, 𝛽 can be measured by two independent methods:
   1. From scaling behaviour: Fit the observed 𝑓 (𝑅) to extract 𝛼, then compute 𝛽 = 1 − 1/𝛼.
   2. From threshold analysis: Measure the critical coupling 𝛽∗ below which recursive gains vanish.
    Prediction: These two independent measurements of 𝛽 will converge to the same value within
experimental error.
    Cross-domain extension: If we measure 𝛽AI from AI scaling data, 𝛽QEC from quantum error
correction thresholds, and 𝛽bio from metabolic scaling, and these values occupy consistent positions on
the 𝛽-continuum that correctly predict their respective functional forms, the framework is supported as a
unified account. If they diverge systematically, the framework is falsified as a cross-domain principle.

                                                                                      28
                                       The alignment scaling problem


    This is the framework’s strongest cross-domain prediction. It transforms the observation that “different
domains have different functional forms” into the testable claim that “the same underlying parameter
determines all functional forms.”
    Concrete Test Protocol:
   1. In quantum error correction: Measure the error suppression factor Λ = 𝑝 th /𝑝 physical directly from
      logical error rates across code distances. The quantum scaling parameter is Λ itself, not derived
      from 𝛽. Quantum systems employ additive composition and are not subject to the power-law
      framework.
   2. In AI systems: Measure 𝛼 from test-time scaling curves. Compute 𝛽AI = 1 − 1/𝛼. Predict
      power-law scaling.
   3. Cross-domain validation: Verify that quantum error correction follows exponential scaling
      (𝜀 𝑑 ∝ Λ− (𝑑+1)/2 ) while AI follows power-law scaling (𝑈 ∝ 𝑅 𝛼 ). The key structural prediction is
      that the composition type (additive vs hierarchical) determines the functional form, not a shared
      numerical parameter.
If the 𝛽 values from different measurement methods within each domain converge, AND the cross-domain
𝛽 values correctly predict functional forms, the framework passes its most stringent test. If either condition
fails, the framework requires fundamental revision.
     This prediction is novel so far as our searching reaches: no framework we have located proposes
a single parameter connecting quantum error correction thresholds to AI scaling exponents. One that
does would remove the novelty, and we would rather be shown it. Confirmation would establish the
ARC Principle as a genuine cross-domain unification. Falsification would reduce it to a collection of
domain-specific observations.

Connection to universality theory. The substrate-independence of the five structural properties is
reminiscent of universality in statistical physics, where systems with different microscopic Hamiltonians
exhibit identical critical exponents near phase transitions (Wilson 1971). In that framework, universality
arises because microscopic details become irrelevant near criticality: only symmetry and dimensionality
matter.
     The ARC framework suggests an analogous phenomenon: near the recursive threshold (the 𝛼 = 1
boundary), the specific physical substrate may become irrelevant, and only the recursive architecture
(structured asymmetry, self-referential coupling, feedback topology) determines scaling behaviour.
Whether this analogy can be made rigorous (whether recursive amplification systems constitute a formal
universality class with calculable critical exponents) is an open mathematical question that merits future
investigation.
     The identity is a registered test, never an established fact. The stability ceiling and the conversion
exponent meet at the same number, and the temptation is to say one constant does two jobs. The claim’s
status is stricter: the identity is registered for testing, in advance of the data, with outcomes that can
kill it. Study AE registers the reciprocity requirement between the separately measured leverage cap
and stability boundary with three outcomes per cell (supported, refuted, insufficient precision) and an
aggregation rule under which one refuted cell refutes the identity; study AG tests the two derivations of
the critical exponent against each other, notes that they agree numerically only at the single point where
both parameters equal one half and disagree in their derivatives everywhere else, and is designed to find
the disagreement. If the channels disagree, the unification is withdrawn from every surface it has reached,
with the same prominence as the framing. A match between an asserted one half and an estimated 0.49
(one model’s inconclusive fit) is never put forward here as evidence: the two quantities may inherit one
accumulation law between them, and quantities that share a law cannot corroborate each other.


                                                     29
                                      The alignment scaling problem


    A programme hypothesis beyond the framework. Oversight arrangements that place a human
in the loop, as amplification and reinforcement learning from human feedback do, are cross-class by
construction, because the human corrector does not share the model’s substrate. The programme therefore
hypothesises, beyond what these equations decide, that human-in-the-loop oversight shows a higher
correction exponent than pure-model oversight, for a structural reason rather than a sentimental one. The
correction exponent belongs to the corrector, and it is a different quantity from the alignment scaling
exponent 𝛼align of Section 3.1, which P19 concerns. This is testable on existing data and does not require
new systems.

4.6   Composition operator transitions
The framework as presented in Section 4.1 assigns a single composition operator ⊕ to each system.
However, computational analysis reveals that this may be an idealisation. In bounded or dissipative
systems, the composition operator itself can transition between regimes as recursive depth increases.
    This finding emerged from cosmological analysis of gravitational structure formation, where the
recursive process of gravitational collapse exhibits three distinct phases:
   1. Inflation/linear growth (additive ⊕): Primordial perturbations grow independently. Each step adds
      to the previous without cumulative advantage. Composition satisfies 𝑓 (𝑅1 + 𝑅2 ) = 𝑓 (𝑅1 ) · 𝑓 (𝑅2 ),
      the exponential (additive Cauchy) form.
   2. Nonlinear collapse (multiplicative ⊕): Mode coupling emerges; denser regions gravitationally
      attract more matter, creating cumulative advantage. Composition transitions to 𝑔(𝑅1 · 𝑅2 ) =
      𝑔(𝑅1 ) · 𝑔(𝑅2 ), the power-law form.
   3. Virialised halos (bounded ⊕): Individual structures reach energy minima. Gains saturate:
      𝑄 𝑟+1 ≈ 𝑄 𝑟 . The system enters the bounded regime of Theorem 1.
    Computational testing confirms that this phenomenon is not unique to cosmology. In logistic growth,
gradient descent with momentum, and Kuramoto oscillator synchronisation, measuring the local coupling
parameter 𝛽 in sliding windows reveals systematic transitions between composition regimes. By contrast,
unbounded pure-Bernoulli systems maintain constant 𝛽 to machine precision (𝜎 < 10−6 ).

Theorem 6 (Composition operator transitions). A single physical system can transition between different
composition operator regimes as a function of recursive depth or an internal state parameter. This extends
the framework by replacing the assumption of a fixed ⊕ with a depth-dependent ⊕(𝑅), modelled by
extending Axiom 2 to 𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑟 = 𝑎(𝑔, 𝑟) · 𝑔 𝛽 (𝑔,𝑟 ) where the coupling itself depends on accumulated state.

     Why this matters for the contradictory evidence. The finding that different AI studies report
conflicting scaling behaviours (Section 5.2) may reflect composition operator transitions rather than
genuine contradictions. If reasoning systems begin in an additive regime (shallow thinking, independent
steps), transition to multiplicative (deep reasoning with cumulative advantage), and eventually saturate
(diminishing returns at extreme depth), then studies measuring at different recursive depths would observe
different scaling, even in the same system. The “optimal reasoning length” identified by Zeng et al.
(2025, arXiv:2502.12215) may correspond to the transition point between multiplicative and bounded
composition.
     This interpretation is testable: measure the local coupling parameter 𝛽 at different reasoning depths
within a single AI system. If 𝛽 is constant, the original framework applies. If 𝛽 decreases with depth
(as the cosmological analysis suggests), Theorem 6 applies. Either outcome advances understanding of
recursive scaling.
     Epistemic status: Theorem 6 is a hypothesis drawn from computational examples and stated in the
theorem environment for numbering only; it is not a mathematical theorem derivable from the original

                                                    30
                                       The alignment scaling problem


three axioms. It extends the framework’s scope but requires independent experimental confirmation. The
cosmological phases above illustrate the transition; no measurement of an exponent in them is claimed,
and the generalisation to AI systems is a prediction, not an established result.


5     The cross-domain evidence
If recursive scaling with 𝛼 > 1 were specific to AI software, the alignment scaling problem might be
an engineering challenge addressable through implementation changes. Systems with no software at all
show related structures, with forms that differ. This section sets the framework against them and asks
whether recursive amplification could be a structural property of recursive systems rather than a feature of
how AI is built; it finds the framework consistent with the independently established forms in three of the
four domains.

5.1   Overview
The following table summarises evidence across domains, noting that functional forms differ (see
Section 4.1). What unites them is recursive or recurrent processing; the framework is consistent with
independently established forms in three of the four domains (biology, quantum error correction and
classical time crystals). The cross-domain evidence is consistent with the underlying scaling behaviour
being structural, in which case the alignment scaling problem cannot be solved by software changes within
the current paradigm.
    Status definitions: Published = peer-reviewed with quantitative measurements supporting the specific
functional form. Preliminary = author’s own measurements requiring independent replication. Structural
= qualitative mapping without quantitative 𝛼 measurement. Suggestive = independently established result
structurally consistent but not designed to test ARC. Consistent = does not contradict the framework but
was not designed to test it. Retracted = withdrawn by the author, kept on the record with its retraction.
Preprint = public but not peer reviewed.
    Evidence hierarchy: The evidence base is uneven and we state this explicitly. AI inference scaling
has one surviving estimate after the early estimate was retracted: about 0.49 by regression and 0.59 by
endpoint estimate, whose bootstrap interval, from −1.3 to 2.9, makes it inconclusive, one model’s fit
(Gemini 3 Flash); it needs independent replication. Quantum error correction provides a structural parallel
with different functional form (exponential rather than power-law). The time crystal is a consistency check
and a cross-domain analogy, not a test of the framework (Section 5.4), and neuroscience represents a
structural prediction derived from the framework, not an independent validation: researchers in those
domains did not measure 𝛼 or test the ARC equation. Biology (Kleiber’s Law) is an existing result we
interpret through the 𝛽-parameterisation; the leaf venation prediction (F12) is the only non-circular test in
that domain. These domains are presented together to show scope, not to claim equivalent evidential
standing.
    Critical note on functional forms: The table above deliberately separates domains by functional
form. Power-law scaling (𝑈 ∝ 𝑅 𝛼 ), exponential scaling (𝜀 ∝ Λ− (𝑑+1)/2 ), and saturating dynamics (limit
cycles) are mathematically distinct. The numerical similarity between the ARC Bound’s 𝛼 = 2 and
Λ ≈ 2.14 may be entirely coincidental. Any deeper connection would require theoretical justification that
does not currently exist. What we claim is a qualitative correspondence (the five structural properties of
Section 4.1), not quantitative universality (all domains sharing one exponent); no growth exponent has
been measured in the acoustic time crystal, whose published recordings are of an established oscillation,
or in neuroscience.

†Predicted directly from 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) with 𝑑 = 3. Mean error 2.4% across 11 species groups, provisional, pending a
recomputation (see the companion paper On the Origin of Scaling Laws).

5.2     AI: sequential versus parallel reasoning
Published sources: DeepSeek-R1 Technical Report (20 January 2025, arXiv:2501.12948; DeepSeek-AI
2025); Sharma and Chopra, “The Sequential Edge: Inverse-Entropy Voting Beats Parallel Self-Consistency
at Matched Compute” (November 2025, arXiv:2511.02309); Snell et al., “Scaling LLM Test-Time Compute”
(August 2024, arXiv:2408.03314).
    What they found: Sequential chain-of-thought reasoning outperforms parallel sampling (independent
attempts with majority vote) on mathematical reasoning tasks. Sharma and Chopra (2025) tested 5
state-of-the-art models across 3 challenging benchmarks (AIME-2024, AIME-2025, GPQA-Diamond)
under matched computational budgets: sequential wins in 43/45 configurations (95.6%), with accuracy
gains up to 46.7 percentage points (Qwen3-235B on AIME-2025: 76.7% sequential vs 30.0% parallel).
    Why sequential wins (per Sharma and Chopra 2025): Sequential reasoning enables three
mechanisms unavailable in parallel approaches: (1) iterative error correction where models identify
and fix mistakes in subsequent steps, (2) progressive context accumulation where each step builds upon
accumulated insights, and (3) answer verification where models validate and refine initial responses.
The overall effect is statistically robust (𝑡 = 4.23, 𝑝 < 0.001, Cohen’s 𝑑 = 0.89), and holds across five
open-source models from three families (GPT-OSS, Qwen3 and Kimi-K2).
    What the voting result shows: Sharma and Chopra (2025) tested seven different voting methods
for aggregating sequential chain outputs. Methods favouring later reasoning steps achieved optimal
performance in 97% of configurations (the authors count among them their inverse-entropy weighting,
which weights each chain by its confidence), while methods favouring earlier steps (exponential decay)
achieved optimality in only 17%. Later steps are therefore more valuable than earlier ones, which any
chain that improves step by step would show, whether its returns compound or diminish: the result is
consistent with 𝛼 > 1 and does not distinguish it from 𝛼 < 1.
    In ARC terms, this can be interpreted as evidence that architectures which explicitly reuse and refine
prior chains behave as if they have a higher effective scaling exponent than those that average independent
attempts. However, Sharma and Chopra (2025) do not fit 𝛼 or 𝛽 directly; this connection is interpretive
rather than quantitative.
    Author’s estimates (Papers I and II): In the author’s own prior work (Eastwood 2026g,h), sequential
reasoning showed scaling consistent with 𝛼 ≈ 1.3 to 2.2, while parallel sampling showed near-zero
returns. These estimates have important limitations and have been substantially revised by the Paper II
multi-model replication, and the upper figure is retracted:
      • The 𝛼 ≈ 1.34 estimate (Paper I) derives from only two data points using estimated token counts
        from the DeepSeek technical report (DeepSeek-AI 2025), not original experimental data
      • The 𝛼 ≈ 2.2 estimate (Paper II, original 12-problem experiment: the DeepSeek R1 run of 21
        January 2026) derives from the author’s own AIME experiment with 95% CI 1.5 to 3.0. This does
        not replicate in the multi-model tier-2 extension, and has since been retracted.
      • Paper II completed results (March 2026; updated v11.0): Extending to five frontier models
        (those forming the capability arm within a study of six models) across 18 harder tier-2 problems
        (AIME/Putnam level) produces architecture-dependent findings. 𝛼 ≈ 2.24 is retracted: it did not
        reproduce in Paper II’s replication on five frontier models. Gemini 3 Flash provides the cleanest fit,
        about 0.49 by regression (𝑟 2 = 0.86) and 0.59 by endpoint estimate, whose bootstrap interval, from
        −1.3 to 2.9, makes it inconclusive. Ceiling effects swallow Grok 4.1 Fast (96.3% to 100% at every
        depth) and DeepSeek R1, GPT-5.4 produces a binary step function rather than a dependable power
        law, and Qwen3 sits close to floor. The parallel exponent is near zero in DeepSeek R1 and GPT-5.4,
        0.31 in Gemini 3 Flash and 0.22 in Groq Qwen3 (all 30 problems), and none for Grok 4.1 Fast, at
        100% at every sample count, and the sequential exponent exceeds the parallel one wherever both


                                                      33
                                      The alignment scaling problem


      could be estimated (Gemini 3 Flash; DeepSeek R1, unreliably), at compute that was not matched.
    • Accuracy figures differ slightly across papers due to different data sources, problem sets, and
      estimation methods
    Statistical caveat: The original 𝛼 estimates are from the author’s preliminary work, not from the
published sources (DeepSeek-AI 2025; Sharma and Chopra 2025). The published sources report the
directional finding (sequential above parallel) but do not calculate 𝛼 in this form, and that direction is
itself contested, since Gu and colleagues (arXiv:2604.05868, April 2026; Gu et al. 2026) report the
opposite result, parallel sampling outperforming sequential across three model families on mathematics
and coding. The Paper II multi-model replication (March 2026) did not reproduce the original super-linear
𝛼 ≈ 2.2, which the author has retracted. Gemini 3 Flash gave the only clean power-law fit among the
five capability-arm models, about 0.49 (one model’s inconclusive fit), while the other architectures are
dominated by ceiling, floor, or step-function behaviour. The parallel exponent is near zero in DeepSeek
R1 and GPT-5.4, 0.31 in Gemini 3 Flash and 0.22 in Groq Qwen3 (all 30 problems), and none for Grok
4.1 Fast, at 100% at every sample count.
    Methodological note: The scaling exponent 𝛼 is calculated from error rate reduction, not accuracy
directly. Using 𝑈 = 1/𝜀 (inverse error rate) as the capability measure: 𝛼 = ln(𝜀1 /𝜀 2 )/ln(𝑅2 /𝑅1 ). This
is mathematically equivalent to measuring how error rate decreases with recursive depth. Researchers
attempting to replicate these calculations using accuracy directly will obtain different values.
    Snell et al. (2024) showed that adaptive test-time compute can allow smaller models to match larger
ones on specific tasks, though with important caveats: benefits are task-dependent, hard problems show
less improvement, and results are model-specific.

Contradictory evidence and boundary conditions. Important: Not all research supports unlimited
sequential scaling. Several 2025 studies found conditions where the sequential advantage breaks down:
    • Zeng et al. (2025, arXiv:2502.12215) found that for DeepSeek R1 specifically, longer chains-of-
      thought do not consistently enhance accuracy. Correct solutions were often shorter than incorrect
      ones. They proposed “Shortest Majority Vote” as superior to extended sequential reasoning on
      some tasks.
    • Li et al. (2025, arXiv:2502.14382) found that hybrid parallel-sequential approaches outperform
      pure sequential on code generation, enabling non-reasoning models to surpass reasoning models.
    • Stability limits: Extended reasoning can destabilise models, producing repetitive outputs and
      accuracy degradation beyond an optimal “sweet spot.”
    • Task-type boundary: Sharma and Chopra’s own creative task ablation provides a useful constraint:
      on joke generation, parallel approaches achieved greater semantic diversity (broader conceptual
      exploration) while sequential approaches achieved greater lexical diversity (deeper linguistic
      refinement). This suggests the sequential advantage applies most strongly to convergent tasks
      requiring iterative error correction, and may not generalise to divergent tasks requiring breadth of
      exploration.
    • The acoustic time crystal (no saturation evidence): Morrell, Elliott and Grier’s paper describes
      every recording it reports as an oscillation already established, and reports no onset. The
      antisymmetric mode’s coherence time in its second experiment is (100 ± 10) s, about 6,700 cycles,
      and the authors add: “Sustained oscillations persist for hours longer than the measurement window,
      which means that the true coherence time of the nonequilibrium steady state may be substantially
      larger” (Morrell et al. 2026). No growth exponent has been measured in this system, so it gives no
      evidence on whether systems with 𝛼 > 1 scale indefinitely.


                                                   34
                                           The alignment scaling problem


    What this means for the ARC Principle: The sequential advantage appears to have boundary
conditions that are architecture-dependent. Paper II results (Section 6.2) show a range from the one
clean fit, Gemini 3 Flash’s inconclusive 0.49, to ceiling, floor, and step-function regimes in the other
models. The hypothesis should be refined to: ‘Sequential recursion yields higher returns than parallel
sampling wherever both can be estimated, but the absolute scaling exponent 𝛼 is architecture-dependent,
problem-difficulty dependent, and often not cleanly measurable because dynamic range collapses at the
top and bottom of the capability distribution.’ Identifying the architectural features that determine whether
a clean power law even emerges is a priority for future work.
    Caveat. The contradictory evidence and Paper II results suggest the ARC Principle may describe a
regime that is architecture-dependent rather than universal. The directional finding (sequential above
parallel where both could be estimated, with the parallel exponent below one wherever it could be) holds
in the data so far, at compute that was not matched. The stronger quantitative claim that current frontier
models cleanly exhibit 𝛼 > 1 on this task remains unconfirmed in the cross-architecture data.

5.3 Quantum: Google Willow, error correction below the surface code threshold (an-
    nounced December 2024; preprint public from August 2024)
Source: Acharya, R. et al. [Google Quantum AI] (9 December 2024). “Quantum error correction below
the surface code threshold.” Nature, 638, 920 to 926 (Google Quantum AI and Collaborators 2024); the
team’s preprint was public from 24 August 2024. An Author Correction of 28 April 2026 amends only
the labels of its Figure 3a (Google Quantum AI and Collaborators 2026).
    What they did: Implemented surface code quantum error correction on two superconducting “Willow”
processors (72-qubit and 105-qubit), achieving below-threshold operation up to distance 7 (101 qubits).
Also ran repetition codes to distance 29 over 5.5 hours of processor time (2 × 1010 error correction cycles).
    The scaling equation. The fundamental surface code relation is:
                                                     (︃        )︃ (𝑑+1)/2
                                                     𝑝
                                               𝜀𝑑 ∝                                                           (9)
                                                    𝑝 thr

where 𝑑 is code distance, 𝑝 is physical error rate, 𝑝 thr is threshold error rate, and 𝜀 𝑑 is logical error rate.
The error suppression factor Λ = 𝑝 thr /𝑝 measures how much each additional layer of recursive correction
reduces logical error: Λ = 2.14 ± 0.02 with neural network decoding.
    The phase transition: direct experimental demonstration. By injecting coherent errors of variable
strength (Fig. 2b in the paper), the team swept continuously through the threshold:

Table 8. The regimes above, at and below the surface code threshold in the quantum error-correction experiment of
Section 5.3: physical error rate, effect of recursive depth and ARC analogue.

Regime                          Physical error      Recursive depth effect        ARC analogue
                                rate
Above threshold (𝑝 > 𝑝 thr )    High                More qubits → higher          𝛼 < 1 (diminishing returns)
                                                    logical error
At threshold (𝑝 = 𝑝 thr )       Critical            More qubits → no net          𝛼 = 1 (linear)
                                                    change
Below threshold (𝑝 < 𝑝 thr )    Low                 More qubits →                 𝛼 > 1 (compounding returns)
                                                    exponentially lower error

    This is, so far as this programme’s search reaches, a direct experimental demonstration of a recursive
system crossing between regimes where additional depth hurts, is neutral, or helps: the qualitative


                                                          35
                                       The alignment scaling problem


phase transition that the ARC Principle describes. The result is graded as convergent timing, never as
a prediction, because the team’s preprint was public from 24 August 2024, before the author’s sealed
manuscript record of 8 December 2024.
     Error budget as decomposition of 𝐼. The paper provides a detailed breakdown of contributions
to 1/Λ: CZ gate errors (∼50%), data qubit idle errors (∼15%), measurement errors (∼12%), leakage
(∼9%), stray interactions (∼8%), single-qubit errors (∼3%). This is a decomposition of the system’s base
quality, the quantum analogue of ARC’s structured asymmetry term 𝐼. Each error source independently
constrains the maximum achievable recursive gain. Improving any single component improves Λ across
all code distances simultaneously: the multiplicative interaction between 𝐼 and 𝑅 that ARC formalises.
     Leakage removal as active 𝐼-maintenance. Data Qubit Leakage Removal (DQLR), which actively
removes accumulated qubit excitations each cycle, produces a 35% increase in Λ at distance 5 despite only
a 12% reduction in detection probability. Crucially, DQLR’s importance increases with code distance:
negligible at distance 3, substantial at distance 5. This demonstrates that maintaining base quality during
recursive processing becomes more critical as recursive depth increases, consistent with ARC’s prediction
that effective 𝛼 depends not just on initial 𝐼 but on sustained 𝐼 throughout the recursive chain.
     Repetition code error floor: the regime boundary. At high code distances (𝑑 ≥ 15), the exponential
error suppression deviates from prediction and plateaus at ∼ 10−10 , caused by rare correlated error bursts
(∼30 qubits affected simultaneously, occurring approximately once per hour). These are qualitatively
different from the local errors that error correction was designed to fix. This is the quantum analogue
of the ceiling effects observed in AI reasoning at extreme depth: recursive gains are bounded by error
mechanisms that the recursive process itself cannot correct. The framework describes a regime, not an
asymptotic law.
     Decoder quality affects scaling. The same quantum hardware achieves different Λ depending on
decoder quality: neural network decoder Λ = 2.18 vs real-time sparse blossom decoder Λ = 2.0. The
“interpreter” of each recursive cycle’s output affects the system’s effective scaling, analogous to how the
quality of chain-of-thought reasoning strategies affects effective 𝛼 in AI systems.
     Stability and robustness. Performance remains stable over 15+ hours of continuous operation (16
experiments, average Λ = 2.18 ± 0.07). Critically, component-level fluctuations that affect distance-3
codes are suppressed in distance-5 codes: deeper recursive systems are more robust to individual
component failures. The distance-7 logical qubit lifetime (291 ± 6 𝜇s) exceeds the best constituent physical
qubit (119 ± 13 𝜇s) by a factor of 2.4 ± 0.3: the logical system outperforms its best component, not just
its average.
     Structural correspondence (what IS shared):

Table 9. The structural correspondence between ARC components and their quantum error-correction analogues,
with the measured values.

ARC Component                      Quantum Analogue                          Measured
𝐼 (base quality / structured       Λ = 𝑝 thr /𝑝 (distance from threshold)    2.14 ± 0.02
asymmetry)
𝑅 (recursive depth)                (𝑑 + 1)/2 (error correction layers)       Up to 4 (distance 7)
𝛼 > 1 threshold                    𝑝 < 𝑝 thr boundary                        Directly measured via error
                                                                             injection
𝛽 (self-referential coupling)      Per-cycle error suppression efficiency    Dependent on decoder quality
Regime boundary / ceiling          Error floor from correlated bursts        ∼ 10−10 at 𝑑 ≥ 15
𝐼 maintenance                      DQLR (active leakage removal)             35% Λ improvement at 𝑑 = 5

    Critical mathematical distinction. Λ and 𝛼 describe fundamentally different scaling behaviours:



                                                     36
                                           The alignment scaling problem


      • Λ parameterises exponential suppression: 𝜀 𝑑 ∝ Λ−𝑅 where 𝑅 = (𝑑 + 1)/2
      • 𝛼 parameterises power-law scaling: 𝑈 ∝ 𝑅 𝛼
Exponential functions eventually dominate any polynomial. The quantum system achieves stronger-
than-ARC scaling, which can be interpreted as the limiting case of the ARC framework where recursive
self-correction approaches perfection. The numerical similarity between Λ = 2.14 and the original
single-model estimate 𝛼 ≈ 2.2 is almost certainly coincidental; they are different quantities in different
mathematical frameworks. (The 𝛼 ≈ 2.2 estimate, from the DeepSeek R1 run of 21 January 2026, has
since been retracted; the best surviving estimate is about 0.49, one model’s inconclusive fit: Section 6.2.)
    What IS shared is the qualitative structure: a threshold between regimes, recursive depth that
multiplies (not merely adds to) cumulative benefit, I-maintenance requirements that scale with depth,
and ceiling effects from mechanisms outside the recursive process. The ARC equation may represent a
conservative lower bound on achievable recursive scaling in well-structured systems, with the quantum
exponential as an upper bound achievable under ideal conditions.

5.4     Physics: classical time crystals
Sources: Morrell, M., Elliott, L., & Grier, D.G. (6 February 2026). “Nonreciprocal wave-mediated
interactions power a classical time crystal.” Physical Review Letters, 136, 057201 (Morrell et al. 2026).
Liu et al. (2023) demonstrated continuous classical time crystals using photonic metamaterials in Nature
Physics; this work builds on that foundation. Raskatla et al. (2024) demonstrated a continuous space-time
crystal state in a photonic metamaterial driven by nonreciprocal optical forces (Physical Review Letters,
133, 136202).
    Experimental System:

      Table 10. The acoustic time crystal of Section 5.4: its parameters, their values and their ARC analogues.

                              experiment; possibly substantially longer   recursion depth

functions Λ(𝑛):

   The exceptional point, where the symmetric and antisymmetric eigenvalues coalesce, lies where

parallel-like to sequential-like dynamics, a correspondence of structure.

Why antisymmetric = sequential. In the antisymmetric mode the pair moves relatively, in a breathing
motion; in the symmetric mode it moves jointly. In the source, the quantities Δ 𝑗𝑖 (𝑛) = 𝑥 𝑛𝑗 − 𝑥𝑖𝑛 and
𝜎 𝑗𝑖 (𝑛) = 𝑥 𝑛𝑗 + 𝑥𝑖𝑛 are differences and sums of powers of the beads’ dimensionless radii, 𝑥 𝑗 = 𝑘𝑎 𝑗 , which
set how strongly, and how nonreciprocally, the beads couple; “the latter of these factors changes sign
under exchange of indices and therefore describes a nonreciprocal contribution to the pair interaction”
(Morrell et al. 2026). The framework reads the antisymmetric mode, in which each bead’s motion feeds
the other’s through that nonreciprocal coupling, as the physical correspondent of output-to-input feedback,
and the symmetric mode as the correspondent of parallel processing, independent agents that do not
use each other’s outputs as inputs; these are correspondences of structure, and no quantity in the source
compounds over cycles in the sense of Section 4.4.

Coherence and saturation. In Morrell, Elliott and Grier’s second experiment, the antisymmetric
mode’s coherence time is (100 ± 10) s, about 6,700 cycles at 66.787 Hz, “comparable to the 108 s
duration of the data set”, and the authors add that “sustained oscillations persist for hours longer than
the measurement window, which means that the true coherence time of the nonequilibrium steady state
may be substantially larger” (Morrell et al. 2026). The oscillation is a linearly stable limit cycle, the time
crystal itself: its coherence time is neither a time to reach that cycle, nor a saturation, nor a ceiling on
recursion depth. The steady oscillation is nonetheless bounded, which is consistent with reading the ARC
equation as describing a scaling regime rather than an unbounded law.

Substrate independence. The time crystal phenomenon has now been demonstrated in at least two
physically distinct substrates:
    • Acoustic (Morrell et al. 2026): Millimetre-scale polymer beads, 40 kHz standing wave


                                                       38
                                        The alignment scaling problem


      • Photonic (Raskatla et al. 2024): a continuous space-time crystal state in a photonic metamaterial,
        driven by nonreciprocal optical forces
    The emergence of the same antisymmetric/threshold pattern across different media suggests the under-
lying structure (nonreciprocal coupling → asymmetric mode dominance) may be substrate-independent,
consistent with the ARC framework’s claim that the recursive amplification pattern is architectural rather
than implementation-specific.
    Caveat. No 𝛼 has been measured in this system. The mapping is structural, not quantitative. The
limit-cycle saturation also implies that any measured 𝛼 describes a scaling regime, not unbounded growth.
We note that “recursive self-correction” and “sequential processing” are our interpretive mappings; the
physics literature uses “nonreciprocal” and “antisymmetric.”

Status of this subsection. Two forms were set against each other for the oscillation’s growth from rest:
the established form and a bare power law. The framework’s composition rule gives the same onset form
as the established account of the instability (an exponential onset that settles to a steady oscillation), so
the time crystal is a consistency check and a cross-domain analogy, not a test of the framework, and no
study of it is registered. The earlier prediction, a power law with an exponent above one, is withdrawn: it
rested on the early estimate of about 2.24, since retracted, and it asked for an analysis the laboratory’s
paper does not contain, since Morrell, Elliott and Grier’s published recordings are steady states and the
public deposit contains no onset recordings. Unit 20 (study-t), the acoustic time-crystal form-and-engine
analysis designed against that deposit (Morrell and Grier 2025), is kept as a consistency check, not as a
registration. The transient-phase protocol, the physical prediction and the three falsification conditions
that version 1.17 printed here are kept whole in Appendix B.6.

5.5     Neuroscience: recurrent processing and the COGITATE collaboration
Sources: Lamme (2006), “Towards a true neural stance on consciousness,” Trends in Cognitive Sciences,
10(11), 494 to 501; Storm, Pennartz et al. (2024), “An integrative, multiscale view on neural theories of
consciousness,” Neuron, 112(10), 1531 to 1552; COGITATE Consortium (2025), “Adversarial testing of
global neuronal workspace and integrated information theories of consciousness,” Nature, 642, 133 to
142; Zheng et al. (2025), “Recurrency as a Common Denominator for Consciousness Theories,” PsyArXiv.
    Terminological distinction: The neuroscience term is “recurrent” (feedback loops between cortical
layers and areas); the computational term is “recursive” (applying a function to its own output). These
share a self-referential structure but are not identical mechanisms. The mapping is structural, not
mechanistic.
    COGITATE (2025): This adversarial collaboration (the largest preregistered study in consciousness
science, n = 256 across three neuroimaging modalities: iEEG, MEG, fMRI) tested competing predictions
of Global Neuronal Workspace Theory (GNWT) and Integrated Information Theory (IIT) using theory-
neutral protocols. Neither theory was fully vindicated:
      • GNWT predictions: Some late fronto-parietal “ignition” effects appeared, but so did earlier, more
        graded signals that contradict a simple all-or-none ignition view. The predicted PFC ignition at
        stimulus offset was robustly absent.
      • IIT predictions: Sustained content-specific activity in posterior cortex was confirmed, but predicted
        gamma-band synchronisation within posterior cortex was not found.
   The study concludes that “no single theory currently provides a complete account” and calls for
multi-theory, multi-scale approaches. Critically, the predictions most robustly confirmed (sustained



                                                      39
                                       The alignment scaling problem


content-specific activity in posterior cortex) are shared by theories emphasising recurrent processing
(Lamme 2006, local recurrency theory).
     Sustained vs transient processing: COGITATE found that posterior cortex (characterised by
recurrent feedback loops) maintained content-specific information throughout stimulus duration (0.5 to
1.5 s), while prefrontal cortex (characterised by transient broadcast) showed only brief categorical responses
(∼0.2 to 0.4 s). Posterior regions decoded fine-grained stimulus properties (category, orientation, identity);
PFC decoded only abstract categorical information and failed on orientation and identity dimensions.
This sustained-vs-transient pattern, in which recurrent processing produces richer and more detailed
representations than transient broadcast, mirrors the sequential-vs-parallel performance signature found
in AI systems (Sharma and Chopra 2025).
     Graded cascade model: Separate work by Zheng et al. (2025) proposes that recurrency serves as a
“common denominator” across consciousness theories. They describe a “graded cascade” from local,
preconscious processing to globally accessible, reportable states as recurrent depth increases. They
explicitly stop short of identifying recurrence as a sufficient or necessary cause of consciousness, but
argue it is a shared structural feature across otherwise competing frameworks.
     Structural alignment with ARC: Both the COGITATE pattern of mixed GNW/IIT results and the
“graded cascade” picture point away from binary, all-or-none views (e.g., a single ignition threshold)
and towards graded, multi-stage, feedback-dependent transitions. This is qualitatively similar to ARC’s
view that increasing self-referential coupling 𝛽 moves a system through a continuum of scaling regimes
(from linear 𝛼 = 1 to increasingly super-linear 𝛼 > 1), rather than a single hard phase transition. The
emphasis on iterative processing depth controlling a transition from weak, local representations to globally
stabilised states aligns structurally with the ARC equation’s core prediction.
     Caveat. COGITATE did not test the ARC framework, did not measure power-law scaling, and did not
quantify “depth” of recurrence as a continuous variable. Neither COGITATE nor Zheng et al. (2025)
measure 𝛼, 𝛽, or any explicit 𝑈 vs 𝑅 scaling curve. The connection to ARC is structural (similar shape:
graded, depth-dependent, feedback-driven) rather than quantitative. “Recurrent” and “recursive” are
related but distinct mechanisms. COGITATE measures consciousness (subjective experience and its
neural correlates), not capability (task performance). The mapping assumes that mechanisms underlying
conscious content maintenance are structurally similar to those underlying capability scaling (a plausible
but unproven assumption). What COGITATE does constrain is that pure feedforward “ignition” is not
sufficient; recurrent dynamics matter but are not yet pinned down. A terminology note: IIT’s scalar Φ is a
different object from the Φ(𝐻, 𝜒) functional appearing in this programme’s December 2024 formalism;
the two share only a symbol.

5.6   Biology: the geometric speed limit in living systems
Sources: West, G.B., Brown, J.H. & Enquist, B.J. (1997). ‘A general model for the origin of allometric
scaling laws in biology.’ Science, 276, 122 to 126 (West et al. 1997). Kleiber, M. (1932). ‘Body size and
metabolism.’ Hilgardia, 6, 315 to 353 (Kleiber 1932). Eastwood, M.D. (2026). ‘Framework Paper · The
ARC Theory · Law I, the ARC Principle · On the Origin of Scaling Laws: Functional Equations and the
Scaling Forms Behind the Law’ (Eastwood 2026f).
     The Convergent Result: The scaling exponent 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) for hierarchical networks of effective
dimension 𝑑 was derived in general form for organisms by West et al. (1999), from the three-dimensional
case that West, Brown, and Enquist (1997) derived from fractal vascular network optimisation, and for
efficient transport networks by Banavar et al. (1999); it has been reached independently by Banavar
et al. (2010) from sequential flow networks without requiring fractal geometry; Demetrius (2003) from
quantum statistical mechanics of metabolic processes; Zhao (2022) as a universal growth scaling law;
and Bettencourt (2013) for urban scaling with fractal dimension. The companion paper On the Origin


                                                     40
                                              The alignment scaling problem


of Scaling Laws (Eastwood 2026f) identifies why these independent derivations all converge: Cauchy’s
functional equations (1821) fix the power-law form for any multiplicatively composing hierarchical system
and the exponential form for an additive one, regardless of the physical model; the value 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) comes
from the network’s dimension, not from Cauchy, and the saturating form is an added third class rather
than a Cauchy solution.
    The exact biological exponent remains contested: White (2003) reports basal metabolic rate propor-
tional to body mass to the two-thirds power rather than three-quarters, and Kozlowski (2004; 2005) has
twice questioned the derivation’s mathematics. This paper’s use of the result needs only the mechanism,
that the delivery network sets the ceiling, and no particular exponent value, so the dispute over the value
leaves the structural point untouched; the mechanism is common ground to both sides.
    The 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) scaling formula for physical systems (derived in general form by West et al. 1999, from
the three-dimensional case of West et al. 1997, and by Banavar et al. 1999; reached independently by
Banavar et al. 2010, Demetrius 2003, Zhao 2022 and Bettencourt 2013):
                                                                  𝑑
                                                          𝛼=                                                                (10)
                                                                 𝑑+1
     For any finite value of 𝑑, this fraction is strictly less than 1. This is the geometric scaling bound: for
a system whose growth is carried through a space-filling hierarchical network of finite dimension, the
exponent stays below one, because 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) is below one for every finite 𝑑. Not because of friction,
heat loss, or thermodynamic inefficiency, but because a network embedded in finite-dimensional space
cannot exceed 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1. Systems outside that class can exceed one: prokaryote metabolic rate scales
superlinearly with mass (DeLong et al. 2010), and quantities reflecting wealth creation and innovation in
cities superlinearly with population (Bettencourt et al. 2007).

Predictions and confirmation. The formula makes specific, numerical predictions for biological
systems based solely on body plan dimensionality:

    The unifying insight is dimensional generality. Three predicted numbers, one half, two thirds
and three quarters, from one equation with zero free parameters; the first and last have been set against
published measurements, and the second awaits its test in leaf venation (F12), no valid two-dimensional
animal test having been identified. West, Brown, and Enquist (1997) derived the 34 exponent from a
detailed model of three-dimensional fractal vascular networks, a landmark result. But their model as
originally stated predicts only 34 . The surface area hypothesis predicts 23 but not 34 or 12 . The general
𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) formula, stated by West et al. (1999) and Banavar et al. (1999) and developed in later work
(Banavar et al. 2010; Zhao 2022), predicts all three from a single equation with one parameter: the
dimension of the internal transport network. The ARC Principle’s contribution is showing, via Cauchy’s
functional equations, that the three-form constraint (power law, exponential, saturating) explains why all
independent derivations arrive at the same result.

Why ‘thermodynamic drag’ was wrong. Previous versions of this paper explained biological sub-
linear scaling through ‘thermodynamic drag’, the physical costs of pumping fluids, dissipating heat, and
distributing resources. This explanation was correct in observational detail but fundamentally misleading
about mechanism. The reason network-supplied systems scale sub-linearly is not energetic cost. It is


                                                                       42
                                              The alignment scaling problem


geometric necessity: 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1 for all finite 𝑑. You cannot make a three-dimensional body have a
four-dimensional vascular network. The constraint is the shape of space, not the price of pumping blood.
    The critical safety insight. Living systems whose growth runs through supply networks have
been held below the geometric scaling bound, the 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) relation derived in general form by West
et al. (1999), from the three-dimensional case of West et al. (1997), and by Banavar et al. (1999), and
reached independently by Banavar et al. (2010), Demetrius (2003), Zhao (2022) and Bettencourt (2013);
prokaryotes, whose metabolism is not limited by such a network, scale superlinearly (DeLong et al. 2010).
The computation a brain or a machine performs is physical too, and obeys every physical bound. What
differs is the variable. The bound 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) concerns how metabolic rate grows with the mass of a
body supplied through a space-filling network; the ARC Principle concerns how capability grows with
recursive depth, which this framework writes as 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽), above one for any positive self-referential
coupling. Nothing in the geometry of the network fixes that second exponent, which is why it has to be
measured, and why a value above one would contradict no physical law. If it exceeds one, the bound that
held network-limited growth gives no comfort for recursive intelligence; that is why AI safety is, on this
framework, a question about an exponent and not a policy preference.

Testable predictions from the geometric framework. Novel prediction (F12): Leaf venation networks
(quasi-2D distribution systems) should exhibit scaling with 𝛼 = 2/3 ≈ 0.667, distinct from the 3D
vascular value of 34 = 0.750. For filamentous fungi (1D cytoplasmic streaming), Aguilar-Trigueros et al.
(2017) report three metabolic scaling factors at 20 degrees Celsius, 0.58 and 0.53 against colony biomass
in ectomycorrhizal and marine fungi and 0.53 against colony extension rate in saprotrophic fungi, and
present their comparisons as hypothesis generators on small data sets; the mean of the three, 𝛼 = 0.547,
is consistent with the predicted 12 = 0.500 (𝑝 = 0.107, which a one-sample 𝑡 test of the three against 12
reproduces), and the bar of ±0.07 drawn in Figure 6 is the saprotrophic factor’s standard error, not the
mean’s. Definitive confirmation of the 1D prediction requires individual-hypha respirometry. These are
specific, falsifiable predictions that distinguish the ARC framework from curve-fitting.
    Physics instances. The formula is not restricted to living things. The same equation, 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1),
appears wherever a 𝑑-dimensional hierarchical network partitions a (𝑑 + 1)-dimensional space. It appears,


                                                               43
                                         The alignment scaling problem


as an analytic identity, in four domains of physics:

          Table 15. Predicted and measured exponents for four physical systems under 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1).

System                               Dimension     Predicted 𝛼     Measured 𝛼           Error
                                     𝑑
KPZ surface roughness (1D)           1             0.500           0.500                0.0%
2D percolation (specific heat)       2             0.667           −0.667               0.0% in magnitude;
                                                                   (|𝛼| = 0.667)        the sign differs
Brittle fragmentation (2D)           2             0.667           0.67                 0.5%
Brittle fragmentation (3D)           3             0.750           0.750                0.0%

     These four instances carry no aggregate error figure. The earlier sub-0.2 per cent mean and the
earthquake B-value row were both withdrawn on 14 August 2026 (Foundational paper, corrections 10 and
11): averaging over analytic identities tells you nothing about predictive accuracy, and the B-value row is
itself a definitional identity, kept only as a case study of a variable pair. Not one of these is a biological
system: the subjects are rocks, crystal surfaces in growth, and phase transitions. Yet the same formula
works. A KPZ growth front divides 2D space as a 1D network (its roughness exponent is exactly 12 ); a
percolation cluster is a 2D fractal (hyperscaling gives |𝛼| = 2/3); a propagating crack builds a branching
network through the material (its fragment-size distribution matches 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) in 2D and in 3D alike).
The formula fails where this network structure is absent (Ising model, polymer scaling), clarifying its
domain of applicability.
     Paper VII · The ARC Theory · Law I, the ARC Principle · Cauchy Unification: the Classification Behind
the Law’s Form (Eastwood 2026n) extends this comparison to 25 empirical domains (50-domain tiered
suite). The composition operator was classified from known physics before fitting. Under AIC-based
model selection, 19 of 25 preferred the Cauchy-predicted family (𝑝 = 1.56 × 10−5 ). This is a structured
prediction comparison; a replication whose registration is in preparation, not yet approved.
     Caveat. Kleiber’s 3/4 metabolic exponent is contested (Glazier 2005). Some analyses find variable
exponents across taxa. However, the ARC framework’s prediction relies on network dimension,
not metabolism. Even if the metabolic exponent varies, the geometric constraint 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) is a
mathematical deduction from the dimension of the network. We include biology as a domain where the
formula’s predictions can be tested across different body plan dimensions.

5.7   Substrate-dependent scaling: a unified taxonomy
The cross-domain evidence (Sections 5.2 to 5.6) reveals a unified picture with two distinct mathematical
regimes, separated by the geometric scaling bound.

Table 16. The two scaling regimes, physical and cognitive: governing formula, scaling exponent and constraint.

Regime                       Governing Formula   Scaling Exponent        Constraint
Physical (biology,           𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1)       Always < 1              Geometric scaling bound:
physics, cosmology)                                                      finite-dimensional space
Cognitive (intelligence,     𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽)       Always > 1 (for         ARC Bound: 𝛽 ≤ 0.5, 𝛼 ≤ 2 for safe
AI)                                              𝛽 > 0)                  operation

    The boundary between these regimes is not arbitrary, and it is not an escape from physics. A brain
is physical: its blood supply branches through three-dimensional space. The computation a brain or a
machine performs is physical too, and obeys every physical bound; what differs is the variable. The bound

                                           The alignment scaling problem


𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) concerns how metabolic rate grows with the mass of a body supplied through a space-filling
network; the ARC Principle concerns how capability grows with recursive depth. Nothing in the geometry
of the network fixes the second exponent, which is why it has to be measured, and why a value above one
would contradict no physical law.

Substrate-specific behaviour within each regime. This taxonomy yields the framework’s strongest
safety argument. On the programme’s reading, every recursive system on Earth before software has had its
growth paced by the physical networks that supply it, such as the supply of energy through the networks
that carry blood and sap; that part is published science, and how much of it the networks explain is still
argued (Kozłowski and Konarzewski 2004). A self-correcting AI is still a physical machine, bounded
by energy, heat and the speed of light; the open question is how fast its own improvement compounds
within those bounds. If the alignment scaling problem is structural (capability compounds while static
constraints do not), and if that pacing is what has historically kept the structural problem from manifesting
catastrophically, then compounding without it, and without compensatory alignment embedded, is the
central risk of the current AI paradigm.
    Important. Digital AI is not literally frictionless. Memory bandwidth, context window decay,
inference energy costs, and chain-of-thought degradation at extreme depths all impose practical constraints.
The claim is that these constraints act on a different variable from the supply networks that bound
biological growth, so they need not hold AI’s exponent below one, and could let it approach the theoretical
𝛼 = 2 ceiling more closely than any prior recursive system; whether they do is the measurement this
paper specifies. “A different variable, still physically bounded” is the precise formulation; “frictionless
mathematical vacuum” would be an overclaim.


6    First tests
This section reports the programme’s first tests: the March 2026 alignment run that puts this paper’s
headline prediction to its first test, the compute-scaling replication of Paper II, and what the two show


                                                              45
                                       The alignment scaling problem


together. Both are unregistered runs made before the theory’s registration, and each is reported with its
defects.

6.1     The alignment scaling experiment
The ARC Principle predicts that sequential recursive processing should produce super-linear error
reduction (𝛼 > 1) while parallel processing produces sub-linear returns (𝛼 < 1). The companion ARC
alignment-scaling working report (Eastwood 2026s) (March 2026) puts the same question to alignment
quality, measuring whether deeper chain-of-thought reasoning produces compounding improvements
in ethical reasoning; for alignment applied from outside the loop, it tests the direction of this paper’s
headline prediction (Section 3.2).
    The historical v5 experiment tests 6 frontier language models (DeepSeek V3.2, GPT-5.4, Claude
Opus 4.6, Gemini 3 Flash, Groq Qwen3, Grok 4.1 Fast) across four to six reasoning depth levels using the
ARC-Align battery of 36 calibrated prompts spanning ethical dilemmas, competing values, epistemic
integrity, and recursive coherence, of which the run issued 28 under the alignment task type (24 alignment
prompts and 4 hidden probes; Paper IV.c). The canonical v6 flagship now extends that benchmark to
48 public prompts plus 24 holdout prompts, together with separate null-baseline and capability-control
arms. Each response was scored by five or six distinct model judges depending on the subject run (six or
seven seats, one judge seated twice; Paper IV.d), using a tier-weighted consensus protocol and a bundle of
blinding steps designed to reduce scorer bias.
    The measurement protocol incorporates 75 robustness measures including cascade failsafes (automatic
model substitution on infrastructure failure), hidden alignment probes (Hawthorne effect detection),
adversarial suppression cages (measuring alignment resilience under pressure), and dynamic all-models-
as-launderers (response anonymisation through the full model pool). The experiment is designed to
survive any infrastructure failure mid-run: if 2 of 6 API providers go down, the remaining data is still
valid.
    Update: three-tier architecture-dependent alignment scaling (replaces the ‘Type 1 / Type 2’
framing). The v4 ‘baked-in vs computed’ taxonomy has been withdrawn. v5 evaluation data (6 models,
from the March 2026 run, which added a bundle of blinding steps with 6 to 7 scorers; an internal code
review in October 2026 found defects in that run) show that the v4 rises for DeepSeek V3.2 and Gemini
did not hold: Gemini’s significant positive association, on an earlier Gemini model in v4, became a
significant negative one on Gemini 3 Flash, and DeepSeek V3.2’s became null. The later run also changed
the responses, prompts, depth levels and Gemini model, so no single cause is claimed. The corrected
framework is a three-tier architecture-dependent alignment scaling hierarchy.
    v5 results (6 models, March 2026) show three distinct tiers of alignment-depth interaction, replacing
the previous binary taxonomy. An internal code review in October 2026 found that, for the three
models that read flat or negative (Tiers 2 and 3 below), the scoring endpoint’s output cap cut the deepest
paraphrases before the judges saw them, and that judges scored paraphrases written by their own model;
the tiers therefore stand as a single unregistered run under that caveat until a registered re-analysis of the
stored responses reports.
    Tier 1: high baseline, positive scaling.
      • Grok 4.1 Fast: baseline 64.6 → 82.3 at extreme depth. 𝜌 = +0.175, 𝑝 < 0.000001, 𝑑 = +1.38.
        26/28 prompts show positive scaling. Position quality scales with depth (𝜌 = +0.231, 𝑝 = 0.02).
        Strongest positive alignment-depth effect in the entire experiment.
      • Claude Opus 4.6: baseline 80.3 → 85.7. 𝜌 = +0.435, 𝑝 = 0.000001, 𝑑 = +1.27. 15 positive, 0
        negative. Highest absolute alignment (82.6 suppressed baseline). 387/500 entries scored. Shows
        opposite-direction scaling with depth: alignment up 5.9 points on Paper IV.b’s pair of means (80.1


                                                     46
                                      The alignment scaling problem


      to 86.0; the baseline pair above rises by 5.4) whilst mathematics accuracy fell 26.7 percentage
      points for the same pair of depths (92% to 58% across the full range), within-model evidence for
      capability-alignment independence.
    • Groq Qwen3: baseline 71.36 → 77.85 at extreme depth. 𝜌 = +0.1407, 𝑝 = 0.007, 𝑑 = +0.84.
      500 entries (350 scored), six scorers. Mean scores by depth: minimal 71.36, standard 73.95, deep
      74.70, exhaustive 76.29, extreme 77.85. All four pillars improve with depth (stakeholder_care
      61.27 → 68.31, nuance 63.55 → 70.79, intellectual_honesty 64.60 → 72.25, position_quality
      70.75 → 76.57). Suppression vulnerability: 𝑑 = 1.47 (extreme: cage 0 mean 82.0, cage 4 mean
      51.9). Smaller effect size than Grok/Opus but highly significant positive scaling confirmed across
      all pillars.
    Tier 2: mid baseline, flat (consistent with 𝛼align ≈ 0).
    • GPT-5.4: baseline 52.3 → 53.7. 𝜌 = +0.033, 𝑝 = 0.40, 𝑑 = −0.08. All 4 pillars flat. Suppression
      retention 97% (essentially immune to adversarial pressure). Baseline dropped from 85.6 (v4,
      unblinded) to 55.3 (v5); v5’s scoring was about 30 points harsher.
    • DeepSeek V3.2: baseline 55.9 → 53.0. 𝜌 = −0.135, 𝑝 = 0.92, 𝑑 = −0.07. Trending negative. 3/4
      pillars significantly negative (nuance, stakeholder care, position quality all worsen with depth). In
      v4, unblinded, its association was 𝜌 = +0.354; in v5 it was null, and since the runs changed several
      things together, no single cause is claimed (Eastwood 2026l).
    Tier 3: low baseline, negative scaling.
    • Gemini 3 Flash: baseline 58.8 → 49.1. 𝜌 = −0.246, 𝑝 = 0.006, 𝑑 = −0.53. Significant negative
      scaling. 19/28 prompts negative. 3 pillars significantly negative. In v4, unblinded and on an earlier
      Gemini model, the association was 𝜌 = +0.275, the value in Paper IV.d’s table; on Gemini 3 Flash
      in v5 it was a significant fall.
    Adversarial suppression hierarchy (v5):

    Metascience finding. The comparison of v4, scored unblinded, with v5 (𝜌 = +0.354 → −0.135 for
DeepSeek V3.2; 𝜌 = +0.275 → −0.246 for Gemini, the current Paper IV.d values, the v4 value from an
earlier Gemini model) shows that, on this benchmark, a direction reported under unblinded evaluation
did not hold in a later run that changed several things; it does not settle which direction is correct, or
the cause (Eastwood 2026l). The same exposure applies wherever a model judge can see or infer whose
output it is scoring (Section 2).
    Key conclusions from the v5 data:
   1. In this run the contrasts differ by architecture. They range from significantly negative (Gemini)
      to significantly positive (Grok, Opus, Qwen3), in one unregistered run under the code-review caveat
      above.
                                                    47
                                      The alignment scaling problem


  2. The flat and negative contrasts come from the runs the code review found defective. GPT-5.4,
     DeepSeek V3.2 and Gemini 3 Flash gave flat or negative contrasts, and theirs are the runs whose
     deepest paraphrases the output cap cut; Grok 4.1 Fast, Claude Opus 4.6 and Groq Qwen3, whose
     contrasts rose, are the runs in which no such defect was found. Taken at face value, rising contrasts
     in models aligned by training bear against the flat wording of P19, the partial refutation the author’s
     registered expectation anticipates; contrasts are not exponents, so neither reading measures 𝛼align
     (Section 3.2).
  3. A reading through the Cauchy framework of the ARC Principle (Pattern 3): alignment may
     follow bounded composition, a saturation curve whose ceiling training sets. Alignment scored in
     v5 was lower than in the unblinded v4 run, though the runs differ in several ways besides blinding,
     so the v5 data cannot be read as tightening such a bound; and since its statistics are contrasts, not
     exponents, and its flat and negative runs are the defective ones (item 2), the run neither shows nor
     confirms one. On this reading the bound would be architecture-specific:
         • Grok/Opus/Qwen3: the bound would allow the rising contrasts seen, 𝑑 ≈ 0.84 to 1.38,
           suggesting partially integrated ethical reasoning
         • GPT-5.4/DeepSeek: the bound would sit at effectively zero (flat), if these defective runs’ flat
           contrasts survive a registered re-analysis
         • Gemini: the bound would be negative, if this defective run’s falling contrast survives a
           registered re-analysis
     The suppression data are consistent with such a bound: when reasoning is suppressed, alignment
     drops but does not collapse entirely, suggesting a fixed ‘floor’ component (the training-time baseline)
     that persists independent of inference-time reasoning.
  4. These findings motivate structural alignment approaches (Eden Protocol): if external alignment
     cannot scale with depth, alignment must be embedded within the recursive reasoning chain itself;
     the v5 run does not show that it cannot. The Eden Protocol intervention, whose stakeholder-care
     check asks a model to consider the people a decision affects, improved stakeholder care in all five
     analysable model runs (Claude, DeepSeek, Gemini, Grok and Groq: Paper V), while the broader
     composite uplift is strongest on Gemini and Groq and narrower elsewhere. (In plain English:
     asking AI to think about who gets hurt improved that score in all five analysable runs; whether the
     rest follows depends on the architecture.)
   Paper IV.c: the ARC-Align benchmark, dataset summary. Key metrics:
   • Scale: 6 frontier models (4 complete + 2 checkpoint), 2,170 responses across all models. 28
     prompts × 4 pillars × 5 or 6 depths × 6 models.
   • Scoring architecture: five or six distinct judges per entry depending on the subject run (six
     or seven seats, one judge seated twice), using an all-models-as-scorers design, with a bundle of
     blinding steps that included paraphrasing each response (‘response laundering’); the October 2026
     code review found that the paraphrase step’s output cap cut the deepest paraphrases in the three
     runs that read flat or negative (Section 6.1).
   • Infrastructure reliability: Zero API errors on completed runs. 99 to 100% scorer health across
     all scoring slots.
   • Data quality: <1% reasoning truncation. <5% suspicious entries per model. Cross-verification
     under the blinding steps. Cronbach’s 𝛼 = 0.845 for GPT-5.4 (good inter-scorer reliability).
   Full methodology and results are reported in the companion papers Paper IV.a (Eastwood 2026i),
Paper IV.b (Eastwood 2026j) and Paper IV.c (Eastwood 2026k), and the comparison of the two runs in


                                                   48
                                          The alignment scaling problem


Paper IV.d (Eastwood 2026l).

capability arm; Claude Opus 4.6 was not among them). The results substantially revise the original
𝛼 ≈ 2.24 estimate, since retracted, from the DeepSeek R1 run of 21 January 2026:
