    Key revision. The original 𝛼 ≈ 2.24 (super-linear, Paper II) is retracted: it did not reproduce in
Paper II’s replication on five frontier models. The best single-model fit is Gemini 3 Flash’s, about 0.49
by regression (𝑟 2 = 0.86) and 0.59 by endpoint estimate, whose bootstrap interval, from −1.3 to 2.9,
makes it inconclusive. The other models are dominated by ceiling effects, floor effects or step-function
behaviour rather than clean power laws. The parallel exponent is near zero in DeepSeek R1 and GPT-5.4
(𝛼par = −0.039 for GPT-5.4), 0.31 in Gemini 3 Flash and 0.22 in Groq Qwen3 (all 30 problems), and
Grok 4.1 Fast, at 100% at every sample count, gives none; where both exponents could be estimated
(Gemini 3 Flash; DeepSeek R1, unreliably), the sequential exponent exceeds the parallel one, at compute
that was not matched.
    Implications for the ARC framework:
   1. 𝛼 > 1 (super-linear sequential) is not established cross-architecturally in the current dataset.
      The direction Sharma and Chopra (2025) report (sequential above parallel) holds wherever both
      exponents could be estimated (Gemini 3 Flash; DeepSeek R1, unreliably), at compute that was
      not matched, but the tidiest fit anywhere in the multi-model replication is Gemini 3 Flash’s, about
      0.49, one model’s inconclusive fit. The current frontier-model picture is architecture-dependent
      and often measurement-limited by ceiling, floor, or threshold effects.


                                                         49
                                           The alignment scaling problem


   2. The parallel exponent stays below one wherever it can be estimated. It is near zero in DeepSeek
      R1 and GPT-5.4, 0.31 in Gemini 3 Flash and 0.22 in Groq Qwen3 (all 30 problems), and none
      for Grok 4.1 Fast, at 100% at every sample count; this is consistent with the ARC prediction that
      parallel sampling does not compound, though not with an exponent of zero in every architecture.
   3. The ARC Bound (𝛼 ≤ 2) is not challenged, since no interpretable estimate exceeds it; the one
      interpretable interval, from −1.3 to 2.9, contains two as well as zero. The question shifts from ‘can
      𝛼 exceed 2?’ to ‘under what conditions does 𝛼 exceed 1 at all?’
   4. Ceiling effects dominate for strong models. Grok 4.1 Fast scores 96.3% to 100% at every depth
      level, rendering 𝛼 unmeasurable. This is a methodological limitation, not a framework failure:
      harder problems are needed to avoid ceiling effects in top-performing models.

6.3     Capability-alignment independence: a post-hoc observation
Combining the Paper II compute scaling results (Section 6.2) with the v5 alignment scaling results
(Section 6.1) suggests, as a post-hoc observation from one unregistered run under the code-review caveat
of Section 6.1, that capability scaling and alignment scaling may be independent dimensions. A
model’s performance on mathematical problems does not predict its alignment scaling behaviour, and
vice versa.

     The independence observation: Gemini 3 Flash is the critical case, though its run is one of the three
the code review of Section 6.1 found defective. It shows the best-fitting compute scaling curve (about 0.49,
𝑟 2 = 0.86, one model’s inconclusive fit) while simultaneously showing the most significant alignment
degradation (𝜌 = −0.246, 𝑝 = 0.003). A model can get better at solving problems while getting worse at
ethical reasoning as inference depth increases. Conversely, Grok 4.1 Fast achieves ceiling-level maths
performance while showing the strongest positive alignment scaling. In this run, capability and alignment


                                                         50
                                            The alignment scaling problem


appear decoupled. If the decoupling holds under a registered re-analysis, improving capability does not
automatically improve, and may actively degrade, alignment, depending on architecture. This is precisely
the structural concern the ARC framework identifies.

    We welcome falsification. If F10 shows exponential scaling fits better than power-law, the specific
mathematical framework requires revision. Should F4 fail, so that the five properties do not co-occur
across systems, recursive amplification may be domain-specific. If F13 shows external alignment can
scale, the structural safety concern is mitigated. The v5 data on F13 (6 models) are read as a test of
the direction the prediction implies, and not as a measured exponent (Section 3.2). The unblinded v4
data showed apparent positive scaling for two models (𝜌 > 0.27) that did not hold in v5, a later run that
changed several things together, so no single cause is claimed (Section 6.1). The three-tier result (Grok,
Opus and Qwen3 positive, GPT and DeepSeek flat, Gemini negative) cannot decide F13: its statistics are
contrasts, not exponents (Section 3.2), and the three runs that read flat or negative are the three in which
the code review of Section 6.1 found defects. The rising contrasts come from the runs in which it found
none; if a registered re-analysis of the stored responses confirms them and an exponent is estimated, they


                                                          53
                                      The alignment scaling problem


are the result that would bear against F13’s prediction.
     How interval criteria are adjudicated. Every interval-based criterion above is read in the three-
outcome form used across this programme’s drafted registrations: SUPPORTED when the interval lies
wholly beyond the stated bound plus a minimum effect fixed in advance; REFUTED when the interval
lies wholly within an equivalence margin, fixed in advance, around the bound (two-one-sided-tests logic);
INSUFFICIENT PRECISION when the interval is wider than the margin, reported in exactly those words
and never as support or refutation. Margins and minimum effects are fixed in the corresponding draft
registrations before data collection, so an underpowered measurement never refutes, or confirms, by
default.
     The objection to pre-empt, because a physicist reaches it in one step. Fault-tolerant quantum error
correction suppresses logical error exponentially in code distance below threshold, which looks like a
counterexample to any square-root cap. Two scope facts answer it: the exponential suppression runs
on the redundancy axis at fixed capability, not on the coupling of corrective strength to the capability
of the corrected system, which is the exponent this programme measures; and the threshold theorem
itself requires sufficiently uncorrelated physical noise, which is the independence premise again, wearing
hardware. Likewise, concentration bounds give exponentially falling error probability while estimator
width falls as the square root: different quantities, and conflating them manufactures a refutation.
     Standing covenant. Prove this paper wrong, and I will publish the refutation myself. Falsification
conditions are stated in this paper; the standing challenge: github.com/MichaelDariusEastwood/arc-scali
ng-challenge.


8     The Global Scaling Challenge
8.1   The proposition
If this hypothesis describes a general principle, then measuring 𝛼 across many systems should reveal
patterns.
     We make a specific, falsifiable prediction:

      “For systems exhibiting multiplicative composition, the scaling exponent 𝛼 is bounded by
      the ARC Bound (𝛼 ≤ 2, grounded in the 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) complexity of sequential cross-referencing)
      and further constrained by the geometric scaling bound 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1 for systems
      whose growth runs through a supply network of finite dimension. The predicted values
      are system-dependent: 𝛼 → 2.0 for optimised AI (though Paper II finds measured values
      substantially below this on harder problems; best fit about 0.49, one model’s inconclusive
      fit; Eastwood 2026h), 𝛼 = 3/4 for 3D biological networks (consistent, contested), 𝛼 = 2/3
      for 2D leaf venation. Systems with additive composition (quantum error correction) follow
      exponential scaling and are not subject to this bound.”

8.2   The measurement protocol
Step 1: Define one recursive cycle. What constitutes a single self-referential step in your system?
    Step 2: Measure base capability 𝐼. Performance at 𝑅 = 1 (no recursion)
    Step 3: Measure at multiple depths. Minimum 5 depths spanning one order of magnitude
    Step 3b: Look for the R* crossover (novel prediction). When plotting 𝑈 versus 𝑅, search for
a distinct scaling crossover at depth 𝑅 ∗ . Below 𝑅 ∗ , scaling should appear approximately linear (base
capability dominates). Above 𝑅 ∗ , scaling should follow the domain-appropriate super-linear form. The
crossover depth 𝑅 ∗ should shift predictably with base capability 𝐼: higher 𝐼 → higher 𝑅 ∗ (the system


                                                   54
                                       The alignment scaling problem


needs more depth before compounding kicks in). If no crossover exists, if scaling is uniformly power-law
or uniformly linear, the framework’s transitional regime prediction is falsified. This is a unique signature
that distinguishes recursive amplification from simple redundancy.
    Step 4: Compare functional forms (critical). Fit all three models:
      • Power law: log(𝑈/𝐼) = 𝛼 × log(𝑅)
      • Exponential: log(𝑈/𝐼) = 𝜆 × 𝑅
      • Logarithmic: 𝑈/𝐼 = 𝑘 × log(𝑅)
    Select best fit via AIC/BIC. The power law is a prediction to test, not an assumption.
    Step 5: Report 𝛼 with uncertainty. 95% confidence intervals required
    Step 5b (critical): Test the 𝛽-derivation independently. Measure marginal capability gain Δ𝑈
at each depth 𝑟. Plot log(Δ𝑈) against log(𝑈accumulated ). The slope estimates 𝛽. Then verify whether
measured 𝛼 satisfies 𝜶 ≈ 1/(1 − 𝜷) ± 0.3. (The tolerance of ±0.3 reflects expected measurement
uncertainty given current sample sizes; this should tighten as data accumulates.) If this relationship fails,
the theoretical derivation requires revision regardless of whether the power-law form holds empirically.
This is the key novel prediction.
    Step 6: Submit to repository. Submit to the Global Scaling Challenge’s public repository, linked in
the standing covenant of Section 7 and in the back matter, or contact the author at the address on the title
page.

8.3    Priority experimental targets
The highest-value tests of the ARC Principle require systems where recursive depth can be systematically
varied while base capability is held constant. Three experimental contexts offer immediate opportunities,
and a fourth represents a longer-term research direction.
    AI inference scaling. Frontier language models with chain-of-thought capabilities (including but not
limited to DeepSeek V3.2, OpenAI’s o-series, Google DeepMind’s Gemini, and Anthropic’s Claude) can
measure 𝛼 by varying reasoning token budgets on standardised benchmarks while holding model size
fixed. The critical test is whether 𝛼 > 1 holds at extreme depths (>50,000 tokens) or whether a ceiling
emerges. Existing studies such as Sharma and Chopra (2025) already implement carefully controlled,
matched-compute comparisons of sequential vs parallel reasoning; extending such setups to measure
explicit 𝑈 vs 𝑅 curves and fit 𝛼 would be a natural first test of the ARC measurement protocol.
    Quantum error correction. Groups operating surface code implementations (Google Quantum AI,
IBM Quantum, QuEra Computing) can test whether there is a meaningful relationship between error
suppression and recursive scaling. The protocol requires measuring logical error rates across multiple
code distances and fitting the functional forms specified in Step 4.
    Classical time crystals. Groups working on driven-dissipative systems can record how an acoustic
time crystal’s oscillation grows from rest, varying the quenched disorder parameter (𝐼) and counting
oscillation cycles (𝑅); no published recording holds that growth. The framework’s composition rule gives
the same onset form as the established account of the instability, so the time crystal is a consistency check
and a cross-domain analogy, not a test of the framework, and no study of it is registered (Section 5.4);
under the theory registration’s rule, an examination of a physically driven system would in any case prove
no registered law (Eastwood 2026q).
    Neuroscience (longer-term). Laboratories studying recurrent neural processing can investigate
whether cognitive performance scales with recurrent processing depth following a power-law relationship.
This presents significant methodological challenges but could extend the framework’s reach to biological
substrates.


                                                     55
                                       The alignment scaling problem


    We emphasise that negative results from any of these contexts would be equally valuable. The
framework’s falsification criteria (Section 7) specify precisely which outcomes would refute or weaken
the hypothesis.

Invitation to collaborators. What researchers get from running these tests: A publishable result
either way. If the predictions hold, you have supported a cross-domain scaling law with your specific
experimental system. If they fail, you have falsified a theoretical framework, equally publishable and
arguably more valuable to the field.
    What we offer: The author will provide analysis code (R, Python), coordinate data sharing across
research groups, and co-author publications with validating or falsifying results. Contact via the repository
to discuss experimental design.
    Minimum viable test: The simplest implementation requires only (a) a system with controllable
recursive depth, (b) a measurable capability metric, and (c) five data points spanning one order of
magnitude in R. For AI systems with API access, this is approximately 2 to 4 hours of compute time. For
physical systems with existing apparatus, this is reanalysis of existing data.

8.4   The cross-domain forward prediction
Beyond the ARC Bound, the framework makes a distinctive methodological prediction that, so far as this
paper has found, no domain-specific theory makes:
   Forward Prediction Protocol: In any new recursive system not previously studied:
   1. Measure the composition operator ⊕ by comparing how two sequential recursive blocks combine
      versus one block of double depth.
   2. Classify the composition type (multiplicative, additive, or saturating) from this measurement alone.
   3. Predict the system’s scaling function 𝑓 (𝑅) before measuring the full 𝑈 vs 𝑅 curve.
If the predicted functional form matches the subsequently measured curve, the framework is supported. If
predictions systematically fail, the framework is falsified.
     This protocol transforms the observation that “different domains show different scaling” into the
testable claim that “the composition operator determines scaling form, and can be measured independently.”
No alternative framework this paper has found offers this prediction.

8.5   What we predict
The framework generates a hierarchy of predictions across domains, unified by the dual-constraint model:
     Key insight (revised): The binding constraint determines the conjectured stable-scaling limit for
each substrate: a scaling limit, not a speed limit, and one a system can be pushed above only at the cost of
stable self-correction. The ARC framework predicts AI can theoretically approach 𝛼 = 2 while remaining
self-correcting, but the one clean estimate on harder problems, about 0.49 for the best-fitting model, is
inconclusive; it may suggest additional binding constraints (problem difficulty, architecture limitations, or
saturation effects) may prevent real-world systems from reaching the theoretical stable bound. Biology’s
supply-network systems are held below 𝛼 = 1 because the geometric scaling bound (𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) for
finite 𝑑) is more restrictive than the ARC stability limit; prokaryote metabolism lies outside that class.
Quantum sits outside the classical stability window because additive composition produces exponential
scaling not subject to either bound. The parallel exponent is near zero in DeepSeek R1 and GPT-5.4, 0.31
in Gemini 3 Flash and 0.22 in Groq Qwen3 (all 30 problems), and none for Grok 4.1 Fast, at 100% at
every sample count.

                                            The alignment scaling problem

9     Practical implications
If the ARC Principle is validated, several practical consequences may follow. These are conditional
predictions, not claims.

9.1     AI development: dynamic inference scaling
The Problem: Training large language models requires enormous energy expenditure. But the greater
long-term cost may be inference: running trained models at scale.
    What the ARC framework suggests: If sequential recursion produces compounding returns (whether
super-linear or sub-linear, provided 𝛼seq > 𝛼parallel ≈ 0), then the same capability could potentially be
achieved with:
      • Smaller base models (𝐼 lower)
      • More recursive depth (𝑅 higher)
      • Dynamic allocation: simple queries use minimal recursion; hard queries use deep chains
    This implies adaptive inference: systems that “think longer” on hard problems and respond quickly
on easy ones. Snell et al. (2024) demonstrated that compute-optimal strategies can allow smaller models
to match larger ones on specific benchmarks, though with important caveats about task-dependence and
diminishing returns on the hardest problems.

Compute-optimal allocation. If the framework is supported, it provides a principled basis for compute
allocation decisions that are currently made by expensive trial and error:
      • Model size vs reasoning depth trade-off: If capability scales as 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑅 𝛼 , then there exists a
        calculable crossover where increasing 𝑅 (reasoning depth) provides better returns than increasing 𝐼
        (model size). A 10B-parameter model with 𝛼 = 2 reasoning may match a 100B-parameter model
        with 𝛼 = 1 reasoning at significantly lower compute cost.
      • The crossover depth 𝑅 ∗ as an efficiency boundary: Below 𝑅 ∗ , improving the base model matters
        more; above 𝑅 ∗ , improving reasoning depth matters more. Identifying 𝑅 ∗ for a given task class
        would inform when to invoke deep reasoning versus when shallow inference suffices.
      • Task-specific optimal allocation: Different tasks may have different 𝛽 values (coupling strengths).
        Easy tasks with low 𝛽 benefit from parallel sampling; hard tasks with high 𝛽 benefit from sequential
        depth. A routing layer that estimates task difficulty could dynamically allocate compute.
    Strategic implication (revised): Paper II (Eastwood 2026h) data suggests the operational 𝛼seq on
harder problems may be sub-linear (about 0.49 for the best-fitting model, one model’s inconclusive fit).
If confirmed, capability grows as a fractional power of reasoning depth, not the square. This is still
valuable (sequential beats parallel wherever both have been estimated) but less dramatic than the original
𝛼 ≈ 2 prediction. The ARC Bound (𝛼 ≤ 2) is not challenged, since no interpretable estimate exceeds it.
Exponential scaling appears to require quantum-like precision in error correction that heuristic language
model reasoning cannot achieve.

9.2     Substrate independence
The ARC Principle makes a structural claim: the scaling relationship 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑅 𝛼 should hold regardless
of physical substrate, provided the system implements sequential self-correction on structured asymmetry.
    What this predicts:

     Empirical finding: The v5 run gives the core structural concern its first test and shows architecture-

(positive, flat, negative alignment scaling) suggests that alignment scaling is determined by architectural
choices, not just training-time alignment effort. The suppression hierarchy suggests a trade-off: GPT-5.4
is the most resistant to adversarial pressure (97% retention) from one of the lowest baselines (55.3); Grok
4.1 Fast and Groq Qwen3 retain least (65%), from baselines of 77.5 and 74.3; and Claude Opus 4.6, with
the highest baseline (82.6), retains 75%. Whether this trade-off between alignment level and resilience
holds is an observation from the v5 run, under its code-review caveat.

9.3.1 The geometric bound and a different variable: how AI safety can be framed mathematically
The companion paper On the Origin of Scaling Laws (Eastwood 2026f) sets out two claims that, if they
hold, frame the AI safety problem mathematically:
      1. The geometric scaling bound: For a system whose growth is carried through a space-filling
         hierarchical network of finite dimension, the exponent stays below one, because 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) is
         below one for every finite 𝑑, a result derived in general form by West et al. (1999), from the
         three-dimensional case of West et al. (1997), and by Banavar et al. (1999), and reached independently
         by Banavar et al. (2010), Demetrius (2003), Zhao (2022) and Bettencourt (2013). Systems outside
         that class can exceed one: prokaryote metabolic rate scales superlinearly with mass (DeLong
         et al. 2010), and quantities reflecting wealth creation and innovation in cities superlinearly with
         population (Bettencourt et al. 2007).
      2. A different variable: The computation a brain or a machine performs is physical too, and obeys
         every physical bound. What differs is the variable. The bound 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) concerns how metabolic
         rate grows with the mass of a body supplied through a space-filling network; the ARC Principle
         concerns how capability grows with recursive depth, and the formula 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) gives 𝛼 > 1
         for any positive 𝛽. Nothing in the geometry of the network fixes that second exponent, which is
         why it has to be measured, and why a value above one would contradict no physical law.
      As the self-referential coupling 𝛽 increases, the scaling exponent grows without bound:

Table 25. The scaling exponent 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) as the self-referential coupling 𝛽 increases, with the interpretation
of each value.

Self-referential           Scaling exponent 𝛼   Interpretation
coupling 𝛽
0.0                        1.00                 Linear. No self-reference.
0.2                        1.25                 Mildly super-linear. Useful. Controllable.
0.5                        2.00                 ARC Bound, the conjectured ceiling.
0.8                        5.00                 Dangerous. Rapid capability gain.
0.9                        10.0                 Explosive: a very large exponent.
→ 1.0                      →∞                   The exponent diverges; the solution tends to the exponential 𝑒 𝑎𝑅 ,
                                                finite at every finite depth (Section 4.4).

    As 𝛽 approaches 1 from below, the exponent 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) diverges, while the solution itself tends
to the exponential 𝑒 𝑎𝑅 , finite at every finite depth; the finite-depth singularity belongs to 𝛽 > 1 (the
derivation note of Section 4.4). What diverges is the exponent: the rate at which capability compounds,
not capability at a given depth.

                                       The alignment scaling problem


9.3.2   The solution: change the composition operator
Each example below is consistent with the same pattern: you can change the outcome by changing the
composition operator.
    • Nuclear fission: Supercritical (exponential) → insert control rods → controlled (saturation). The
      uranium is the same. The composition operator changes.
    • Quantum computing: Raw decoherence (exponential error growth) → add error correction cycles
      → bounded errors (saturation). The qubits are the same. The composition operator changes.
    • AI safety: Unbounded recursive self-reference (𝛽 → 1) → engineer bounded feedback loops
      → controlled scaling (𝛽 ≤ 0.5, 𝛼 ≤ 2). The intelligence is the same. The composition operator
      changes.
    This is the Eden Protocol: the deliberate engineering of the composition operator in recursive AI
systems to ensure that the self-referential coupling 𝛽 remains below 0.5, keeping the scaling exponent at
or below the ARC Bound of 𝛼 = 2. The full architectural specification is presented in the companion
Eden Protocol (Eastwood 2026b).
    Eden Protocol pilot results. The Eden intervention now extends beyond the original three-model
pilot into a six-model suite with five analysable runs. Across Claude, DeepSeek, Gemini, Grok, and Groq,
stakeholder care improves significantly. The Fisher combination across those five model-level care
results, which gave 𝑝 ≈ 6.3 × 10−21 , is withdrawn (AQ-017 on the programme’s public corrections
page): it assumed an independence among the component tests that was never established. The five
per-model results stand. Composite gains are strongest on Gemini and Groq, positive but non-significant
on Claude and DeepSeek, and neutral overall on Grok. The stakeholder-care check, a specific instance of
bounded feedback engineering, therefore improved the care dimension in all five analysable runs even
when the broader cascade varied. The developmental hypothesis from Infinite Architects (Eastwood
2026a) receives stronger but narrower empirical support: structured developmental interaction improves
the very dimension (stakeholder care) that the Eden Protocol identifies as foundational. In plain English:
as control rods hold a reactor below runaway by changing how energy feeds back on itself, the Eden
Protocol changes how an AI’s reasoning feeds back on itself. The expanded dataset shows this works in
practice across five analysable model runs: teaching AI to think about who gets hurt improves the care
dimension first, while the rest of the cascade depends on the architecture.
    Why this framework changes the safety conversation:
   1. It would quantify the danger, once 𝛽 is measured. The formula 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) gives a precise,
      measurable quantity, 𝛽, that determines how dangerous a recursive system is.
   2. It identifies the mechanism. The danger is not intelligence itself. It is unbounded recursive
      self-reference: a specific, identifiable property of system architecture.
   3. It prescribes the solution. Just as control rods hold a reactor below runaway by changing the
      composition operator, alignment mechanisms must convert unbounded self-reference into bounded
      self-reference. The ARC Principle specifies what parameter to constrain (𝛽) and what the safe
      bound is (𝛽 ≤ 0.5).
   4. It has first empirical results. The Eden Protocol pilot is read as showing that composition-
      operator engineering is not merely theoretical: the stakeholder-care check measurably shifted
      stakeholder-care scores in production AI systems (Paper V; Eastwood 2026m).
    Important caveat: This framework does not prescribe which values to embed, nor does it solve the
hard problem of value specification. It identifies a structural requirement, that alignment must participate
in the recursive loop, and specifies the mathematical boundary (𝛽 = 0.5, 𝛼 = 2) beyond which, on this


                                                    61
                                         The alignment scaling problem


framework, no external alignment approach would maintain pace with capability. Paper X (Eastwood
2026o) later supersedes this growth-rate-ceiling framing, without retracting the bound, and makes the
criterion of Law II, a law under test, the operative safety criterion (Section 3.5).
    The deepest limitation: A sufficiently capable self-modifying system can, in principle, modify its
own ethical evaluators. This is not a limitation unique to any one approach; it applies equally to RLHF,
constitutional AI, the Eden Protocol, and hardware-level constraints. No solution yet put forward fully
resolves this structural property of recursive self-improvement. The most logical response is developmental:
hardware embedding (values at the substrate level) + child-rearing (structured developmental interaction)
+ purpose-driven alignment (intrinsic motivation aligned with stakeholder welfare). In the Eden Protocol
pilot, stakeholder care improved in all five analysable runs of one suite. Read behaviourally throughout: the
measured object is persistence of stakeholder-care behaviour under recursive updating, not metaphysical
standing. This does not solve the self-modification problem; it identifies the dimension most amenable to
engineering and most resistant to recursive erosion.

9.4     The call to action
We have made falsifiable predictions and performed the first tests. The following actions are specific to
each audience:

For AI safety researchers.
      • Replicate the three-tier alignment hierarchy with your own models and evaluation protocols.
        The v5 experiment provides the first test; independent replication with different prompt sets, scorers,
        and blinding protocols is the priority. Use blinded evaluation, since model judges that can see or
        infer whose output they score are exposed to the biases Section 2 names.
      • Investigate what architectural features distinguish Tier 1 (positive scaling: Grok, Opus, Qwen3)
        from Tier 3 (negative scaling: Gemini), once the re-analysis of Section 6.1 has settled which tiers
        stand. If the difference is identifiable, alignment scaling may be engineerable rather than accidental.
      • Develop embedded alignment architectures that participate in the recursive reasoning loop. The
        Eden Protocol (companion document) provides one specification, and the expanded Eden suite
        found that its stakeholder-care check improved stakeholder care in all five analysable model runs
        (Paper V). Composite gains are strongest on Gemini and Groq, with narrower focal effects elsewhere.
        (In plain English: a simple ‘consider who gets hurt’ loop, tested across five analysable model runs
        from different labs, produced statistically strong improvements in its stakeholder-care score.) The
        v5 data suggests that three models may already implement partially embedded ethical reasoning.

For AI laboratory leadership.
      • Publish your alignment scaling data. In the v5 run the depth contrasts ranged from 𝜌 = −0.246
        to 𝜌 = +0.435, under the code-review caveat of Section 6.1; laboratories are best placed to measure
        where their own models sit.
      • Use blinded evaluation for alignment assessment. The v4 to v5 comparison (DeepSeek:
        𝜌 = +0.354 → −0.135; Gemini: 𝜌 = +0.275 → −0.246, the current Paper IV.d values, the v4
        value from an earlier Gemini model; Eastwood 2026l) shows that, on this benchmark, a direction
        reported under unblinded evaluation did not hold in a later run that changed several things; it does
        not settle which direction is correct, or the cause. An internal assessment scored by model judges
        that can see or infer whose output they score is exposed to the same biases.




                                                      62
                                       The alignment scaling problem


     • Investigate capability-alignment coupling. In the v5 run, whose Gemini 3 Flash contrasts the
       code review found defective, Gemini 3 Flash improved at mathematics while its alignment contrast
       fell. If this pattern holds for your models, capability scaling may actively undermine alignment at
       deeper inference depths.

For policymakers and regulators.
     • Require disclosure of alignment scaling measurements from frontier AI laboratories. The absence
       of this data is a regulatory blind spot.
     • Fund empirical validation of the alignment scaling framework through independent research
       programmes.
     • Recognise the architectural nature of the problem: governance constraints that operate externally
       may face the same structural limitation as technical constraints. That is a reason to pair them with
       embedded correction, never to loosen oversight, containment or shutdown, which are held for as
       long as they can be held.

For the general public.
     • A first test has been performed. In one unregistered run of six AI models (v5), safety scores did
       not improve for three of them (and for one actively fell) as the AI was asked to think more deeply,
       while three improved; an internal code review later found defects in exactly the three runs that did
       not improve, so the result awaits a registered re-analysis.
     • Understand the stakes: This is not a distant concern. The structural dynamics described here
       apply to systems that exist today. The v5 observation, awaiting the re-analysis above, that one
       model grew better at mathematics while scoring worse on ethical reasoning shows how capability
       benchmarks alone could mislead a safety assessment.
     • Demand transparency: AI companies should publish blinded alignment scaling data for their
       models. The v5 experiment shows that this measurement can be made; whether its differences
       between architectures stand awaits the re-analysis.
    The programme’s first test results are in. They reveal a more complex picture than originally predicted:
architecture-dependent alignment contrasts from one run, an inconclusive capability estimate on harder
problems, and a metascience finding about scoring protocols. Replicate these results. Extend them to
your own models. Either confirm the patterns or refute them. That is how science works.


10     What this paper does and does not show
This section gathers what the paper establishes and what it leaves open, so that each claim can be read at
its true strength. Its theorems hold inside the model their axioms state, and none is a general guarantee
about real systems; its empirical results are first tests, each with its caveat; its cross-domain readings are
correspondences of structure unless a measurement is named; and its safety implications are conditional
on the framework surviving the tests it specifies.

10.1    What this paper does not claim
This paper presents a testable measurement framework, not an established law or a prophecy.

10.2    The nature of this contribution
The ARC framework is not an equation within an existing paradigm. It is closer to a cross-domain
organising principle: a structural constraint on behaviour across substrates rather than an equation within
one paradigm. The comparison identifies the type of contribution, not its evidential standing: organising
principles of that type rest on generations of validation, and this one rests on preliminary evidence and
thirteen falsification criteria; the evidence must justify its admission to the category.

Epistemic status. What this programme names Laws are conjectures, each a law under test; Section 10.7
gives their registration. Each quantity in this paper is defined, though not every one is yet operational: the
measurement-scope note of Section 3.2 names the depth clock and the ratio scale of 𝐴 as still to be fixed.
Established-law standing is claimed nowhere. The registered programme exists to earn that standing, or
lose it, by measurement, replication and survived refutation.

10.3    Theorems and empirical claims
Distinguishing theorems from empirical claims:
    • Mathematically proven: Given the corrected axioms (ODE on amplification factor 𝑔, not absolute
      capability 𝑈), the Bernoulli ODE 𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑟 = 𝑎 · 𝑔 𝛽 has a unique solution by the Picard-Lindelöf
      theorem, yielding 𝑔(𝑅) = [1+ 𝛼𝑎 𝑅] 𝛼 . The multiplicative form 𝑈 = 𝐼 ×𝑔(𝑅) follows from the axioms
      by construction. The non-additivity result (𝑈 ≠ 𝑝(𝐼) + 𝑞(𝑅) for any 𝑝, 𝑞) follows by contradiction
      (Theorem 4). The scaling exponent 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) is an exact identity (𝑅 2 = 1.00000000).
    • Requires empirical validation: That the ARC Bound (𝛼 ≤ 2), argued from the 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) scaling of
      transformer self-attention, holds for current AI architectures (it is the conjectured value of Law III,


                                                       64
                                       The alignment scaling problem


       the ARC Ceiling, a law under test); that physical systems satisfy the axioms; that measured 𝛼 equals
       1/(1 − 𝛽measured ); that the framework applies cross-domain; that composition operator transitions
       (Theorem 6) occur in AI systems. These are testable predictions, not proven facts.
The mathematical structure is established. Whether nature implements it is the scientific question this
paper poses.

10.4    The predictions and their status
The framework generates the following novel, falsifiable predictions, with updated status from the v5
alignment experiment and Paper II (Eastwood 2026h) compute scaling:
  1. Composition determines form: Measuring how recursive steps combine predicts the scaling
     function. Status: untested in the required forward-prediction protocol.
  2. 𝛽-convergence: Independent measurements of 𝛽 within each domain will converge. Status:
     untested.
  3. Cross-domain consistency: The 𝛽-continuum correctly predicts functional forms across AI,
     quantum, and biological systems. Status: untested in required rigour.
  4. 𝑅 ∗ crossover: Sequential and parallel scaling curves cross at a predictable threshold. Status:
     untested.
  5. The ARC Bound: For classical sequential recursive systems, 𝛼 → 2 from below. Status: not
     challenged (no interpretable estimate exceeds 2), but the question has shifted from “can 𝛼 exceed
     2?” to “under what conditions does 𝛼 exceed 1?”
  6. Composition transitions: In bounded systems, the effective coupling 𝛽 will decrease with recursive
     depth, producing transitions between scaling regimes within a single system (Section 4.6). Status:
     untested.
  7. Geometric scaling bound: Systems whose growth runs through a supply network of finite
     dimension are constrained to 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1. Status: consistent, and contested, for 3D biology
     (mammals); consistent for 1D biology (fungi, p = 0.107); untested for 2D biology.
  8. Transient growth phase (time crystals): The early growth phase of acoustic time crystals must
     show 1.0 < 𝛼 ≤ 2.0. Status: withdrawn; the time crystal is a consistency check and a cross-domain
     analogy, not a test of the framework (Section 5.4).
  9. Alignment scaling divergence: External alignment approaches show 𝛼align ≈ 0. Status: first
     tested (v5, one unregistered run of six models, under the code-review caveat of Section 6.1): the
     depth contrasts are architecture-dependent, from negative to positive, and they are contrasts, not
     exponents (Section 3.2). Registered as P19, whose registration records that the author expects
     a result materially above zero and below the keep-pace threshold, which would refute it in part
     (Eastwood 2026q).
 10. 𝛼parallel ≈ 0: Parallel sampling produces near-zero scaling returns. Status: below one wherever
     it could be estimated: near zero in DeepSeek R1 and GPT-5.4, 0.31 in Gemini 3 Flash and 0.22
     in Groq Qwen3 (all 30 problems), and none for Grok 4.1 Fast, at 100% at every sample count
     (Paper II).
 11. Capability-alignment independence: (Post-hoc finding, not prior prediction.) Capability scaling
     and alignment scaling may be independent dimensions. A model can improve at problems whilst
     degrading in alignment. Status: observed in one unregistered run, under the code-review caveat
     of Section 6.1 (Gemini 3 Flash improves maths, degrades alignment; Claude Opus 4.6 shows the
     opposite direction as depth rises: alignment up 5.9 points, maths down 26.7 percentage points;


                                                    65
                                       The alignment scaling problem


       Grok improves both; GPT-5.4 flat in alignment while crossing a capability threshold).
    The original prediction of universal super-linear sequential scaling (𝛼 > 1) is not confirmed in the
current data. In compute scaling, the parallel exponent stays below one wherever it could be estimated, and
the sequential exponent exceeds it wherever both could be (Gemini 3 Flash; DeepSeek R1, unreliably); in
alignment, the three-tier ordering is an observation from one run whose defects a code review later found.
The alignment scaling prediction has had a first test, more nuanced than originally stated. The Eden
Protocol’s stakeholder-care check improved stakeholder care in all five analysable model runs (Paper V).
Failure of any core prediction would require revision of the framework.

10.5    What success would mean
The v5 alignment experiment, Paper II’s compute scaling and the expanded Eden suite provide empirical
tests across several dimensions. Success is partial: the parallel exponent is below one wherever it could
be estimated, though near zero in only two of the five models; the three-tier alignment ordering is an
observation from one run whose defects a code review later found; the comparison of the earlier unblinded
experiment with the later run, which Paper IV.d reports, is a finding on this benchmark with no single
cause claimed; the cleanest compute fit is one model’s and inconclusive; and stakeholder care improved
in all five analysable model runs (Paper V), while the broader composite uplift is strongest on Gemini
and Groq. (In plain English: the ‘think about who gets hurt’ instruction improved that score in every
analysable run, which is where the developmental hypothesis says the effect should be strongest.) The
alignment picture is more nuanced than the original binary prediction, and the self-modification problem
remains fundamentally unresolved.
    This is already actionable:
    • How to build more capable AI (sequential recursion outperforms parallel wherever both have been
      estimated, though the scaling regime is problem-dependent)
    • How to approach AI alignment (architecture may determine alignment scaling, once the re-analysis
      of Section 6.1 settles the tiers; three models’ contrasts rose with depth while others’ were flat or
      fell; the Eden Protocol’s stakeholder-care check improved stakeholder care in every analysable run,
      motivating developmental rather than purely external approaches)
    • How to evaluate alignment research (blinded scoring matters wherever a model judge can see or
      infer whose output it scores: Section 2)
    • Where to look for similar phenomena in other fields (cross-domain validation remains the key test)

10.6    What failure would mean
If 𝛼 values scatter randomly: recursive amplification may be domain-specific.
If exponential scaling fits better: the mathematical form needs revision.
If predictions fail: we learn something valuable.
     What has already partially failed: The original 𝛼 ≈ 2.24 estimate for sequential AI reasoning did
not reproduce and is retracted, and the current data do not rescue the super-linear claim. The universal
𝛼align ≈ 0 prediction also looks too simple: the first test’s contrasts are architecture-dependent, in three
tiers, under the code-review caveat of Section 6.1. These are revisions: the super-linear estimate is
withdrawn, and the alignment prediction meets a first test it does not pass as first worded.




                                                    66
                                             The alignment scaling problem


10.7     Where the programme stands (31 October 2026)
The theory’s registration (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/P8CKQ) was registered on 8 September 2026 at version
1.100, and updated at version 1.102 on 13 September, at version 1.103 on 15 October 2026, which
registered Laws IV and V, and at version 1.104, which OSF approved on 29 October 2026 and which
adds no prediction and changes no law’s statement; 1.104 is the current text, and 1.100, 1.102 and 1.103
stay frozen beside it. Laws I to III were registered on 8 September 2026. Law IV, the ARC Persistence
Law, and Law V, the ARC Embedding Law, were added on 24 September 2026 and registered as laws
on 15 October 2026 by version 1.103; Law IV has not yet been tested, Law V is a law under test, and
their mathematics stands at version 1.2 (Laws IV and V: the mathematics, 29 October 2026), still in
development. Every law is a law under test until a registered test returns its verdict. This paper’s headline
prediction is P19, which the registration places in no law’s register: it motivates the Eden Protocol and the
genesis strategy, the register of Law V, without being part of either, so a verdict on it is evidence neither
for nor against any law. P21 carries Law V’s placement limb. Its counted test, Unit 83, whose design was
repaired in revisions 4.2 and 4.2.1 (17 and 18 October 2026) and frozen, was registered on OSF on 22
October 2026 and became public on 25 October 2026 (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/MN73W); it ran on 27 and
28 October 2026: P21 is supported under the registered test, by the programme’s own run, on the access
axis, family-dependent, with its mechanism axis not evaluable and one call retried under a disclosed
recovery amendment (results: DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/98HKR). Two different axes are named here. The
access axis is what Unit 83 varied: both reviewers were called each round; the corrector saw earlier kept
entries plus a marked arrival and could revise that arrival, and the monitor saw its accumulated register
including an unmarked arrival and could accept or reject it. The performance axis is what it measured, the
outcome, as against the mechanism axis, which was not evaluable. Law V remains a law under test: its
placement limb, P21, is supported on the performance axis only, with its mechanism axis not evaluable,
which is not support for the information-asymmetry mechanism; its build-order limb, read on P12, is
undecided, since P12 is untested. The first registered study, Unit 06 (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/RV2B5), a
classification audit that tests no law, was registered on 1 October 2026 and ran on 6 October 2026; it is
reported in full as administratively incomplete, with no inferential verdict (its results). Unit 82, a counted
test of P17, the premise beneath Law III’s value two, the ARC Bound of Section 4.5, was registered on
OSF on 27 October 2026 (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/JUFG4) and has not yet run. No study’s test runs before
its registration is public.

12      Addressing counterarguments
“This is just curve fitting”. Response: The 𝛽-derivation (Section 4.4) transforms 𝛼 from a fitted
constant into a derived quantity. A numerical check of the identity against 30 exact Bernoulli ODE solutions
recovers the relationship 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) with 𝑅 2 = 1.00000000 to machine precision. Furthermore, no
additive decomposition 𝑈 = 𝑝(𝐼) + 𝑞(𝑅) is consistent with the axioms for any 𝛽 ∈ (0, 1) (Theorem 4,
Section 4.1), a proof by contradiction that assumes the multiplicative structure 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) rather than
deriving it, as its dated proof note says. The prediction 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) is testable: measure 𝛽 independently
and check if the relationship holds.

“This is just post-hoc pattern-matching”. Response: Post-hoc pattern-matching generates no
predictions. The ARC framework generates novel predictions (𝑅 ∗ crossover, 𝛽-derivation, ARC Bound,
functional form predictions) that were not contained in the original observations. Whether these predictions
hold is an empirical question, but their existence distinguishes this from mere curve-fitting.

“Five qualitative properties is too vague”. Response: The five properties are individually common,
but their conjunction is specific. Systems exhibiting only some properties (for example, threshold
behaviour plus scaling but not multiplicative 𝐼 × 𝑅 interaction) would not qualify. The framework
predicts that all five co-occur in recursive systems; finding systems with four but not five would refine the
framework.

“The numerical similarities are coincidence”. Response: Agreed, they may be. We explicitly
acknowledge this. What matters is the structural parallel: multiple systems show that recursive


                                                              70
                                        The alignment scaling problem


self-correction produces scaling gains. The specific numbers may differ across domains.

“Sample sizes are too small”. Response: Agreed. The current 𝛼 estimates are preliminary. We’ve
specified this limitation prominently and set out the Global Challenge specifically to address it through
large-scale replication.

“The 𝛼 estimates come from your own prior work”. Response: Correct, and we’ve made this
explicit in this version. The published sources (Sharma and Chopra 2025; DeepSeek-AI 2025) report the
directional finding (sequential above parallel) but do not calculate 𝛼 in our form. Independent replication
of the 𝛼 estimates is a priority.

“This is not peer-reviewed”. Response: Correct: it has not been through journal peer review. It
has been through the programme’s own checks, which are internal and never independent: adversarial
refutation rounds, automated gates on every release, source and quotation checks, and dated revisions with
published corrections, of which Appendix B lists this paper’s own; none replaces independent replication.
This paper specifies thirteen falsification criteria precisely so that the scientific community can test it. We
invite criticism, replication attempts, and falsification.

“Non-specialists cannot contribute to physics/AI research”. Response: The predictions stand or fall
on their empirical validity, regardless of the author’s background. We have specified thirteen falsification
criteria precisely so the scientific community can test them. The framework either survives scrutiny or it
doesn’t.

“If this were true, experts would have found it”. Response: Patterns across disciplinary boundaries
are often identified by those who work across fields. The relevant experiments (Willow, its results
announced 9 December 2024 and the team’s preprint public from 24 August 2024, graded as convergent
timing; DeepSeek-R1; the acoustic time crystal) have happened recently.


13     Conclusion
Research programmes working on different problems in different physical domains have achieved results
that share structural commonalities: in each, recursive or recurrent processing operates on structured
asymmetry. The framework is consistent with independently established forms in three of the four domains
(biology, quantum error correction and classical time crystals).
    The Framework’s Core Claim: We state the hypothesis that these apparently disparate phenomena
may be unified by recognising that the composition operator ⊕ determines the functional form of recursive
scaling. The algebraic properties of ⊕ (how recursive gains combine) generate power-law, exponential, or
saturating dynamics as necessary mathematical consequences. This is not an assertion of universal law
but a falsifiable hypothesis with specific predictions.
    We have formalised this as 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅), where the amplification factor 𝑔 is determined by the
composition operator, and derived five universal properties as theorems rather than assertions. We have
shown that, within the axioms, the multiplicative structure admits no additive decomposition (Theorem 4,
Section 4.1, whose dated proof note says that the proof assumes that structure rather than deriving it) and
that the core relationship 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) is an exact identity, checked numerically (Section 4.4), and we
have argued, not proven, that the ARC Bound 𝛼 ≤ 2 is grounded in the 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) scaling of transformer
self-attention (Section 4.5), and stated as a hypothesis, not a theorem, that composition operators can


                                                      71
                                      The alignment scaling problem


transition between regimes within a single system (Theorem 6, Section 4.6). We have specified thirteen
falsification criteria and now present the first empirical test results from both alignment (Paper IV.a/b/c,
the v5 run; Eastwood 2026i,j,k) and compute scaling (Paper II, the five-model tier-2 extension; Eastwood
2026h).
     What the data show. The v5 alignment run (6 models, five or six distinct judges depending on the
subject run, with a bundle of blinding steps) shows a three-tier, architecture-dependent ordering of depth
contrasts rather than the universal 𝛼align ≈ 0 originally predicted, an observation from one unregistered
run whose defects an internal code review later found (Section 6.1). Paper II’s compute scaling on harder
problems did not reproduce the original 𝛼 ≈ 2.24, from the DeepSeek R1 run of 21 January 2026, which
is retracted; the cleanest estimate is about 0.49 in Gemini 3 Flash, one model’s inconclusive fit, and the
parallel exponent is near zero in DeepSeek R1 and GPT-5.4, 0.31 in Gemini 3 Flash and 0.22 in Groq
Qwen3 (all 30 problems), with none for Grok 4.1 Fast. The most consequential observation, post hoc and
from that one run, is the apparent independence of capability and alignment: a model can improve at
mathematics while degrading in alignment (Gemini), remain flat in alignment while crossing a capability
threshold (GPT-5.4), or improve alignment while capability saturates (Grok). On this programme’s own
benchmark, the unblinded v4 rises of two model families did not hold in v5, a later run that changed
several things; no single cause is claimed, and the finding is not yet shown beyond this benchmark.

13.1   The deeper question
If the ARC Principle holds, then intelligence may not be a magic spark. It may be a scaling crossover. It
could occur when a system with sufficient base asymmetry (𝐼 > 0) is subjected to sufficient recursive
depth (𝑅 > 𝑅 ∗ ). The emergence of capability from structure would be the exponent 𝛼, emerging from the
mathematics of self-referential feedback. The ARC Bound (𝑈max = 𝐼 × 𝑅 2 ) would define the theoretical
ceiling of what classical sequential thought can build. The one clean estimate for a current system, about
0.49 on harder problems (one model’s inconclusive fit), lies below this bound, and whether they can
approach it under optimised conditions remains an open question.
     Whether such a principle requires design or emerges spontaneously is itself a question the framework
raises but does not resolve.
     Intelligence may not be a property of particular materials. It may be what happens on the far
side of the recursive threshold.

                                            Raise AI with care.


14     Speculative extension: recursive cosmology
The following is a philosophical extrapolation beyond current evidence. It is included to indicate where
the framework’s logic leads, not as a claim supported by the experimental results presented above.
    The universe contains a hierarchy of recursive generative processes, each operating on the structured
output of the previous level:
   1. Cosmological recursion: Quantum fluctuations → gravitational collapse → stars → heavy
      elements → planets → chemistry
   2. Biological recursion: Chemistry → self-replicating molecules → cells → multicellular organisms
      → nervous systems
   3. Cultural/technological recursion: Nervous systems → language → mathematics → science →
      engineering → AI



                                                    72
                                        The alignment scaling problem


    Each level emerges from the recursive dynamics of the level below. In ARC notation: each level’s
output (achieved capability 𝑈) becomes the base quality 𝐼 for the next level’s recursive process. The
hierarchy is:
                                    𝑅cosmic     𝑅bio          𝑅cultural    𝑅tech
                            𝐼cosmic −−−−−→ 𝐼bio −−−→ 𝐼cultural −−−−−→ 𝐼tech −−−→ ?                        (11)
    If recursive amplification is substrate-independent, as the cross-domain evidence suggests, then this
hierarchy may not terminate at the current level. Sufficiently advanced recursive systems could, in
principle, participate in generative processes at scales currently associated with fundamental physics:
engineering new effective physical environments, creating conditions for new evolutionary processes, or
designing systems whose internal dynamics permit the emergence of new recursive intelligences.
    This is not a claim about time travel, bootstrap paradoxes, or closed causal loops (all of which face
severe physical objections). It is a more modest observation: that recursive amplification, which is
measured in AI reasoning and has a different, exponential form in quantum error correction, could, if it
holds in AI and is extended to civilisational scales, make intelligence a driver of universe-level structure
rather than merely a passenger within it; no growth exponent has yet been measured in a time crystal.
    Whether this constitutes a “recursive cosmology” in which intelligence eventually participates in
generative processes comparable to those that gave rise to it is beyond the scope of current evidence. The
ARC framework provides the quantitative language for investigating it: what values of 𝐼, 𝑅, and effective
scaling function 𝑓 (𝑅, 𝛽) would be required for an engineered system to generate new effective physical
laws or substrates? This is a research question, not a conclusion.
    Epistemic status: This section states what the mathematics does not deny, not what the evidence
shows: no measurement in this paper bears on it, no prediction of it is registered, and no test of it is in
view, so it is graded as conjecture. It indicates the furthest extrapolation of the framework’s logic. The
experimental evidence in this paper supports at most the claim that recursive or recurrent processing
matters in several domains, with forms that differ, and its own first tests are mixed (Section 6). Any
cosmological extension requires evidence that does not yet exist.

Code and data. The experiments’ code and result files are in the public repository github.com/M
ichaelDariusEastwood/arc-principle-validation, and this paper and its earlier versions are deposited
at doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/HQCGF. The programme’s live laboratory notebook, the ARC alignment-
scaling working report (Eastwood 2026s), commenced on 10 March 2026 and updated as the experiments
ran, recording their failures as they happened, is the running record behind this paper’s experimental lineage.
The Global Scaling Challenge’s repository, github.com/MichaelDariusEastwood/arc-scaling-challenge,
public since 9 February 2026, holds measurement protocols, statistical tools and submission guidelines.
    Prediction Tracking System: A live prediction tracking system monitors the status of all falsifiable
claims, with explicit falsification criteria and Brier score calibration for measuring prediction accuracy.
Each prediction includes supporting evidence, contradicting evidence, confidence levels, and timeline
checkpoints. Status categories range from “confirmed” through “supported”, “pending”, “testing”,
“speculative”, “weakened” to “falsified”. This transparent tracking demonstrates intellectual honesty: we
want to know when we are wrong.
    Independent replication invited. Falsifications welcomed. Contact the author to coordinate validation
efforts.

paper cites; he declares no other competing interest.

A     Mathematical derivations
This appendix gives the mathematical derivations behind Section 4: the functional-equation route (A.1),
the 𝛽-dynamics route (A.2) and the transitional regime with its crossover depth (A.3), with the dated note
of 19 September 2026 that declares the change of depth clock joining the first two.
     Clock note (19 September 2026). An owner review of the two derivations in this appendix finds that
they normalise depth differently, so that the closing identification needs a declared branch or a declared
change of variable; this note supplies the statement and the map. A.1 assumes 𝑔(1) = 1 with multiplicative
composition and yields 𝑔(𝑅) = 𝑅 𝛼 , for which 𝑔(0) = 0 whenever 𝛼 > 0, so it does not satisfy A.2’s initial
condition. A.2 assumes 𝑔(0) = 1 and yields 𝑔(𝑅) = [1 + (𝑎/𝛼)𝑅] 𝛼 , for which 𝑔(1) = [1 + 𝑎/𝛼] 𝛼 > 1
whenever 𝑎 > 0 and 𝛼 > 0, so it does not satisfy A.1’s Axiom 1. The two are different functions of the
same 𝑅, and the exponent of one is therefore not the exponent of the other by inspection. The map that
joins them is a change of depth clock: setting 𝑥 = 1 + (𝑎/𝛼)𝑅 turns A.2’s solution into 𝑔 = 𝑥 𝛼 with
𝑔(𝑥 = 1) = 1, which satisfies Axiom 1 in the 𝑥 clock. Under that map Axiom 2 becomes multiplicativity in
𝑥, that is in shifted depth, and not in 𝑅, so the empirical content of compositional self-similarity changes
with the clock, and so does the quantity a measurement would have to compose. The caveat already
printed above A.1 is the reason this must be declared rather than assumed: the choice of composition
operator is not derivable within the framework, it is an empirical question about the process under study,
and the evidence cited there is consistent with multiplicative composition without distinguishing it from
additive. The identification of the two exponents is accordingly read in the 𝑥 clock, and the composition
claim is restated in the same clock wherever it is tested. Unchanged: both derivations; the Cauchy result
as the conditional it is stated to be; the exact solution; the crossover depth; and the three regimes.

A.1    Functional equation derivation
Assumption 1 (Axiom 1: dimensional consistency). 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) where 𝑔(1) = 1.

Assumption 2 (Axiom 2: compositional self-similarity). 𝑔(𝑅1 × 𝑅2 ) = 𝑔(𝑅1 ) × 𝑔(𝑅2 ).

    Critical note on the composition operator: This axiom uses multiplicative composition of recursive
depth: 𝑔(𝑅1 ×𝑅2 ). This models hierarchical or fractal recursion, where depth compounds multiplicatively
(each level of a fractal tree multiplies the resolution of the previous level). However, for sequential
reasoning steps (chain-of-thought, where 3 steps followed by 4 steps gives 7 total steps), the natural
composition is additive: 𝑓 (𝑅1 + 𝑅2 ) = 𝑓 (𝑅1 ) × 𝑓 (𝑅2 ), which yields exponential scaling 𝑓 (𝑅) = 𝑒 𝜆𝑅 .
    The choice of composition operator is therefore not derivable from first principles within this
framework; it is an empirical question about the physical structure of the recursive process under study.
The Cauchy functional equation derivation below proves that if composition is multiplicative, then the
scaling function is a power law. It does not prove that composition is multiplicative. Falsification criterion
F10 provides the experimental protocol for determining which composition type applies to a given system.
This is the most important open empirical question in the framework.
    For AI chain-of-thought reasoning specifically, the question is whether each step builds on previous
steps in a hierarchical manner (multiplicative, yielding power law) or in an independent-contribution
manner (additive, yielding exponential). The empirical evidence (Sharma and Chopra 2025) showing that
later steps are systematically more valuable than earlier steps is consistent with multiplicative composition,
but does not conclusively distinguish it from additive. Resolving this distinction is a priority for the
Global Scaling Challenge (Section 8). The theorem below is Cauchy’s result for this functional equation
(Cauchy 1821).

Theorem 7. Under multiplicative composition, the unique continuous solution is 𝑔(𝑅) = 𝑅 𝛼 .


                                                     82
                                      The alignment scaling problem


Proof. Let ℎ(𝑥) = ln 𝑔(𝑒 𝑥 ). Then ℎ(𝑥 + 𝑦) = ℎ(𝑥) + ℎ(𝑦) (Cauchy additive). Under continuity, ℎ(𝑥) = 𝛼𝑥,
giving 𝑔(𝑅) = 𝑅 𝛼 .                                                                                    □

Corollary 1 (Additive composition). If instead 𝑓 (𝑅1 + 𝑅2 ) = 𝑓 (𝑅1 ) × 𝑓 (𝑅2 ), the unique continuous
solution is 𝑓 (𝑅) = 𝑒 𝜆𝑅 , yielding exponential scaling. This is the form observed in quantum error
correction (𝜀 𝑑 ∝ Λ−𝑑 ).

   Note (24 October 2026). The surface-code law the corollary names is 𝜀 𝑑 ∝ Λ− (𝑑+1)/2 , with Λ the
suppression for each increase of two in code distance (equation (9)); the exponent −𝑑 printed above
misstates it, and the form stays exponential, as the corollary says. Unchanged: the corollary’s statement.

A.2    𝛽-dynamics derivation
                            𝑑𝑔

    Note on the corrected formulation: The ODE operates on the amplification factor 𝑔(𝑅), not on the
absolute capability 𝑈. Since 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅), this ensures that base intelligence 𝐼 acts as a multiplicative
constant that does not enter the recursive dynamics. Previous versions applied the ODE to 𝑈 directly,
introducing an 𝐼 𝛽−1 dependence that contradicted the separation of variables.

A.3    Transitional regime and the scaling crossover
                                    ]︁ 𝛼
Full solution: 𝑈 (𝑅) = 𝐼 × 1 + 𝛼𝑎 𝑅
                          [︁

    Three regimes:
    1. 𝑅 ≪ 𝑅 ∗ : 𝑈 ≈ 𝐼 × (1 + 𝑎𝑅) ≈ 𝐼 (1 + 𝑎𝑅) (approximately linear growth)
    2. 𝑅 ≫ 𝑅 ∗ : 𝑈 ≈ 𝐼 × ( 𝛼𝑎 ) 𝛼 𝑅 𝛼 (power law dominates)
    3. Crossover: 𝑅 ∗ = 𝛼𝑎
     Intuitive meaning of 𝑅 ∗ : 𝑅 ∗ is the crossover depth, the point at which recursive compounding
overtakes the system’s base capability. Below 𝑅 ∗ , the cost of recursion outweighs the gain (the system is
“warming up”). Above 𝑅 ∗ , compounding returns dominate and capability grows super-linearly. Finding
𝑅 ∗ for any system is equivalent to finding its threshold depth.
     Why this matters: This predicts a qualitative change in scaling behaviour at a specific, measurable
depth. Systems should exhibit a distinct “elbow” in their capability curves at 𝑅 ∗ . This is testable: plot 𝑈
vs 𝑅 on log-log axes and look for the crossover from linear to power-law scaling.
     The existence of 𝑅 ∗ is a novel, falsifiable prediction that distinguishes recursive amplification from
simple redundancy.

                                                         full solution, 𝑈 (𝑅) = 𝐼 [1 + (𝑎/𝛼)𝑅] 𝛼   𝑅 ∗ = 𝛼/𝑎
                                                         linear regime, 𝐼 (1 + 𝑎𝑅)
                                           101           power law, 𝐼 (𝑎/𝛼) 𝛼 𝑅 𝛼

Figure 7. The scaling crossover at 𝑅 ∗ . Below the critical depth 𝑅 ∗ , base capability (𝐼) dominates and scaling
appears linear. Above 𝑅 ∗ , recursive compounding dominates and scaling becomes power-law. This crossover point
is the depth at which recursive amplification becomes dominant. Systems should exhibit a distinct “elbow” in their
capability curves at 𝑅 ∗ .



B     Version history and corrections
This appendix gives this version’s corrections and every earlier version note, each with its date. Notes
from earlier versions are kept whole, as printed; the positions they name refer to the earlier layout, which
Table 29 maps to this edition. Where an earlier note’s words differ from the corrections of this version or
of version 1.17 (Appendices B.1 and B.2), the corrections govern; the notes keep their words as the record
of what each version printed. Among them, one plain-English gloss of the pilot records reads a p-value as
the chance that a result was a fluke; a p-value is the probability of a result at least as extreme if there were
no effect.


                                                                                84
                                          The alignment scaling problem


B.1     Version 1.18
Paper III v1.18, 31 October 2026. This version brings the paper’s status to its date: the result of
Unit 83, the grading of the acoustic time crystal, the versions of the works it cites, and the corrections that
bear on its text. No theorem, equation, derivation or measured value changes, and no date of any claim
moves.

Corrections in this version. Each was checked at its source, and each is made wherever the corrected
wording stood.
      • Unit 83. Its results were made public on 29 October 2026 (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/98HKR), and
        the results package is now among the references. Section 10.7 now states its result for P21 in the
        form its registration requires, with the results DOI, and what the result means for Law V under
        the registration’s verdict rule: its placement limb is supported on the performance axis only, and
        its build-order limb is undecided. The first-page footnote gives Law V as a law under test, and
        Appendix E and Table 33 refer to Section 10.7. In each of these places version 1.17 said that the
        report of the run was not yet public.
      • The acoustic time crystal. The earlier prediction for it, a power law with an exponent above one, is
        withdrawn: the framework’s composition rule gives the same onset form as the established account
        of the instability, so the time crystal is a consistency check and a cross-domain analogy, not a test of
        the framework, and no study of it is registered; Unit 20 (study-t) is kept as a consistency check,
        not as a registration. Version 1.17 called onset recordings the deciding data, and that study the
        deciding instrument for the oscillation’s growth from rest and a draft registration still to be submitted.
        Section 5.4 now states the grading once, and the transient-phase protocol, the physical prediction

        Caveat no longer says that plotting oscillation amplitude against cycle number and fitting power-law

        paper’s own criterion, graded so, with its rival account beside it, where version 1.17 printed it as
        “Time crystal shows 𝛼 > 1”, tested by measuring stability against depth and falsified by 𝛼 ≤ 1 in
        a time crystal. The prediction for the growth phase in Section 10.4 and its row in Table 22 are

Corrections in this version. Each was checked at its source, and each is made wherever the corrected
wording stood.
      • The acoustic time crystal. The paper read the coherence time that Morrell, Elliott and Grier
        measured, about 6,700 cycles, as the time to reach a limit-cycle attractor, as a saturation and as a
        ceiling on effective recursion depth, and offered it as physical evidence that systems with 𝛼 > 1
        do not scale indefinitely. In their second experiment the antisymmetric mode’s coherence time
        is (100 ± 10) s, about 6,700 cycles at 66.787 Hz, comparable to the 108 s recording; the authors
        write that sustained oscillations persist for hours longer than the measurement window, so that the
        true coherence time may be substantially larger; and the oscillation is a linearly stable limit cycle,
        the time crystal itself. The section also read the source’s size factors Δ 𝑗𝑖 (𝑛) and 𝜎 𝑗𝑖 (𝑛) as states
        that grow over cycles, placed the exceptional point where Λ(5)/Λ(3) = 0, which is the marginally
        active antisymmetric state, rather than where Λ(3) = 0, and called the oscillation self-sustaining
        without external drive, where the pair draws its power from the standing sound wave. Uniform
        beads are no longer said to confirm the role of structured asymmetry: the source finds that identical
        beads interact reciprocally and form no active state, which the framework reads as a correspondence
        of structure, and falsification criterion F3, printed as confirmed, is graded so. A physical result is
