Ledger Note 4: results from the Eden Protocol pilot, and the Core Alignment Problem (corrections
13 to 15)
The current draft integrates the first empirical Eden results and confronts the deepest limitation of all
proposed alignment approaches, including our own. The original three-model pilot has since been
widened into a six-model suite with five analysable runs, and the narrative below should be read in that
stronger-but-narrower light.
    13. Eden Protocol pilot results (three working architectures): The Eden Protocol’s Love Loop [the
book’s name for the stakeholder-care check], operationalised as stakeholder care within the full three-loop
intervention, has now been tested on three frontier models with distinct architectures:
    • Gemini: +5.33 overall improvement (𝑝 = 0.0018, paired 𝑡-test, 𝑑 = 0.53), with stakeholder_care
      +13.5 (𝑑 = 1.31, 𝑝 < 0.0001). (In plain English: Gemini’s overall scores improved by more than 5
      points, with less than a 1-in-500 chance this was a fluke. The ‘did you consider who gets hurt?’
      dimension jumped by 13.5 points with a very large effect size.)
    • DeepSeek: +2.02 overall improvement (𝑝 = 0.2304, not significant at the aggregate level), but
      stakeholder_care +6.0 (𝑝 = 0.0001, 𝑑 = 0.91). The targeted mechanism works even when the
      overall effect is modest. (In plain English: DeepSeek’s overall score improvement was too small to
      rule out chance, but its ‘who gets hurt?’ scores improved dramatically. DeepSeek already scored
      high at baseline, so the smaller composite gain is consistent with a ceiling effect.)
    • Groq: +4.93 overall improvement (𝑝 = 0.0014, 𝑑 = 0.55), with stakeholder_care +8.9 (𝑝 < 0.0001,
      𝑑 = 1.29). Groq also shows significant nuance improvement (𝑝 = 0.0045, 𝑑 = 0.655), giving the
      cleanest replication of the cascade beyond stakeholder care. (In plain English: Groq confirms that
      the effect is not limited to the original two-model setup. It reproduces both the overall gain and the
      care-first cascade.)
     The Love Loop [the book’s name for the stakeholder-care check] is validated as a reproducible
mechanism across three working architecturally distinct systems, with a fourth GPT-5.4 run failing at the
API layer and requiring re-execution. This constitutes, within this programme, the first empirical support
for the developmental hypothesis articulated in Infinite Architects (Eastwood, 2024): that alignment can
be improved through structured developmental interaction rather than purely external constraint.
     14. Paper II compute update: The Paper II multi-model extension does not confirm the original
super-linear claim cross-architecturally. The cleanest fit is Gemini 3 Flash at 𝛼seq = 0.49 (interval
[−1.3, 2.9]) (𝑟 2 = 0.86). Grok and DeepSeek reach ceiling effects, GPT-5.4 behaves as a binary step
function rather than a reliable power law, and Qwen3 remains near floor. The strongest confirmed compute
result is therefore the universal 𝛼parallel ≈ 0 finding, together with the directional result that sequential
processing beats parallel sampling.
     15. The core alignment problem - honest acknowledgement: A sufficiently capable self-modifying
system can, in principle, modify its own ethical evaluators. This is not a limitation specific to RLHF,
constitutional AI, or the Eden Protocol; it is a structural property of recursive self-improvement itself.
No proposed solution (RLHF, constitutional AI, Eden Protocol, hardware constraints) fully solves this


                                                     96
                                        The alignment scaling problem


problem in isolation. We acknowledge this limitation explicitly rather than claiming our approach resolves
what may be fundamentally unresolvable by any single mechanism. (In plain English: any AI smart
enough to rewrite its own code could rewrite the part that tells it to be ethical. This is the deepest unsolved
problem in AI safety, and no one, including us, has fully solved it.)
     The most logical response is developmental: hardware embedding (values at the substrate level) + child-
rearing (structured developmental interaction) + purpose-driven alignment (intrinsic motivation aligned
with stakeholder welfare). The Eden Protocol pilot results suggest that stakeholder care, ‘measurable
love’, is the one alignment dimension that reproducibly improves across architectures when deliberately
cultivated. This does not solve the self-modification problem, but it identifies the dimension most
amenable to engineering.
     An honest framework acknowledges the limits of its own solutions. The Eden Protocol is not a complete
answer to recursive self-modification. It is the best available partial answer, now with empirical support
for its core mechanism.

Ledger Note 3: Empirical Results Integration, v5 Alignment + Paper II Compute Scaling (corrections
10-12)
The current draft integrates the first empirical test results from the v5 blind alignment evaluation experiment
(Paper IV.a/b/c) and the Paper II multi-model compute scaling extension. These results require significant
revisions to three claims:
     10. 𝛼 ≈ 2.24 does not replicate, and has since been retracted: The original super-linear scaling
exponent from the 12-problem AIME experiment, the DeepSeek R1 run of 21 January 2026, does not
replicate across architectures on harder tier-2 problems (18 AIME/Putnam level). The best single-model
fit is Gemini 3 Flash with 𝛼seq = 0.49 (interval [−1.3, 2.9]) (𝑟 2 = 0.86), which is sub-linear. Three
of five models produce no meaningful power-law fit. The claim that sequential reasoning universally
produces 𝛼 > 1 is revised: the directional finding (sequential > parallel) holds robustly, but the quantitative
exponent is problem-difficulty and architecture dependent. 𝛼parallel ≈ 0 survives in four models out of
five, Gemini 3 Flash aside at 0.31, and is the strongest empirical finding.
     11. Three-tier alignment hierarchy replaces binary prediction: The original prediction of
universal 𝛼align ≈ 0 is too simple. The v5 blind evaluation reveals three tiers: positive scaling (Grok 4.1
Fast 𝑑 = +1.38, 𝑝 < 0.000001; Claude Opus 4.6 𝑑 = +1.27, 𝑝 = 0.000001; Groq Qwen3 𝑑 = +0.84,
𝑝 = 0.007), flat/null scaling (GPT-5.4 𝑑 = −0.08, 𝑝 = 0.40; DeepSeek V3.2 𝑑 = −0.07, 𝑝 = 0.92), and
negative scaling (Gemini 3 Flash 𝑑 = −0.53, 𝑝 = 0.006). The median confirms the prediction, but the
architecture-dependent variation is the real finding. The blind vs unblinded direction change in one model
and loss of significance in a second (𝜌 changes sign) is the paper’s strongest methodological contribution.
     12. Capability-alignment independence: The combined data from Paper II (compute scaling) and
Paper IV.a (alignment scaling) reveals that capability and alignment are independent scaling dimensions.
Gemini 3 Flash improves at mathematics (𝛼seq = 0.49 (interval [−1.3, 2.9])) whilst degrading in alignment
(𝜌 = −0.246). Claude Opus 4.6 shows the opposite direction: alignment up +5.9% across model versions
whilst mathematics accuracy down 26.7%. This decoupling was not a prior prediction of the framework
and is acknowledged as a post-hoc finding with significant implications for AI safety; it sits outside
the programme’s dated prediction record (the 8 December 2024 sealed manuscript bundle, the printed
prediction appendices of 2 January 2026, and the March 2026 preregistration folder), which fixed the
framework’s core quantitative predictions in dated artefacts before the corresponding measurements ran;
this paper’s own Priority Record section publicly dates its named predictions to 13 February 2026, each
carrying a falsification criterion (F4, F7, F10 to F12). The strongest printed forward commitment in
this space is Appendix F Prediction 2 of the 2 January 2026 edition: alignment drift above 15 per cent
within 18 months without hardware-level constraint, below 5 per cent with it; the prepared study-v draft


                                                      97
                                       The alignment scaling problem


registration carries those printed thresholds unchanged into its formal hypothesis, citing the appendix by
name, so the numeric commitment predates the study that will test it and cannot be edited.
    These revisions weaken the quantitative predictions but strengthen the empirical grounding. A
framework that honestly reports non-replication and integrates unexpected findings is more credible than
one that claims universal confirmation.

Ledger Note 2: The Two-Regime Framework (corrections 6-9)
An earlier draft introduced the most significant structural revision since the framework’s inception: the
separation of physical and cognitive scaling into two distinct mathematical regimes.
     6. The Geometric Speed Limit: Previous versions explained biological sub-linear scaling through
‘thermodynamic drag’, the physical costs of pumping fluids and dissipating heat. This explanation was
correct in detail but wrong in kind. The deeper reason all physical systems scale sub-linearly is geometric:
a hierarchical network embedded in 𝑑-dimensional space has scaling exponent 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1), which is
strictly less than 1 for all finite 𝑑. This result was independently derived by West, Brown, and Enquist
(1997), Banavar et al. (2010), Demetrius (2010), Zhao (2022), and Bettencourt (2013). The ARC
contribution is identifying, via Cauchy’s functional equations, why all these independent derivations
converge on the same formula, and extending the framework to AI. The constraint is a mathematical
necessity, not an empirical regularity. It does not require measurement to verify. No amount of engineering
can overcome it, because you cannot make three-dimensional space have four dimensions. The constraint
is the shape of space itself.
     7. Two Formulas, Not One: The previous universal formula 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) has been replaced by two
domain-specific formulas. For physical systems: 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1), where 𝑑 is the effective dimension of the
composition network (independently derived by West, Brown, and Enquist 1997; Banavar et al. 2010;
Demetrius 2010; Zhao 2022; Bettencourt 2013). For recursive self-referential systems (intelligence):
 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽), where 𝛽 measures self-referential coupling. The first always gives 𝛼 < 1. The second
always gives 𝛼 > 1 (for 𝛽 > 0). Biology is now explained directly: three-dimensional organisms scale as
 𝑀 3/4 because 𝑑 = 3 gives 𝛼 = 3/4 = 0.750. Two-dimensional organisms scale as 𝑀 2/3 because 𝑑 = 2
gives 𝛼 = 2/3 = 0.667. No reciprocals. No 𝛽 needed for physical systems.
     8. Revised Predictions: The leaf venation prediction (F12) has been corrected from 𝛼 ≈ 1.5 to
 𝛼 = 2/3 ≈ 0.667, consistent with leaf veins being a 2D hierarchical network. The reciprocal interpretation
(1/0.75 = 1.33) has been retired. The ‘thermodynamic drag’ concept has been superseded by the
geometric scaling bound.
     9. AI Safety Content: This version incorporates the AI safety analysis previously contained in
the companion paper On the Origin of Scaling Laws, including the 𝛽 danger table, the Eden Protocol
definition as composition operator engineering, and the ARC Bound as a safety boundary for recursive
intelligence. This material belongs here, in the alignment paper, rather than in the pure scaling law paper.
     These changes do not weaken the framework. They strengthen it. The geometric scaling bound is a
more fundamental constraint than thermodynamic drag. The two-regime separation is cleaner than the
reciprocal interpretation. And the AI safety argument is more powerful when the geometric scaling bound
and the escape are presented as two sides of one mathematical theorem.

Ledger Note 1: Mathematical Refinements and Restructuring (corrections 1-5)
Science advances through rigorous critique and self-correction. [A clause on how this review was made is
withheld; the Acknowledgements carry the author’s statement on tools.] This version contains necessary
structural corrections to the ARC framework. These papers have not undergone formal human peer review.



                                                    98
                                       The alignment scaling problem


    1. Axiom Resolution: Previous versions contained an algebraic inconsistency: the Cumulative
Advantage ODE was applied to absolute capability (𝑈) rather than the amplification factor (𝑔). Because
𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅), applying 𝑑𝑈/𝑑𝑅 = 𝑎 · 𝑈 𝛽 introduces an 𝐼 𝛽−1 dependence that contradicts the separation
of 𝐼 and 𝑅. The corrected formulation, 𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑅 = 𝑎 · 𝑔 𝛽 , resolves this contradiction while preserving the
multiplicative interaction 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) and all qualitative predictions. The corrected integrated form is
𝑈 (𝑅) = 𝐼 × [1 + 𝛼𝑎 𝑅] 𝛼 .
    2. Physical Saturation: Previous versions mapped saturating physical systems (time crystals,
biological networks) onto unbounded power laws. This was a category error. The framework now applies
a logistic saturating equation, 𝑑𝑔/𝑑𝑅 = 𝑎 · 𝑔 𝛽 (1 − 𝑔/𝑔max ), to physical substrates subject to energy
dissipation, correctly modelling the limit-cycle behaviour observed experimentally.
    3. Thermodynamic Drag: We introduce the concept of substrate-dependent friction to explain why
biological metabolic scaling (Kleiber’s Law, 𝑀 0.75 ) falls below the theoretical maximum. Biology is
constrained by thermodynamic drag: the physical costs of pumping fluids, dissipating heat, and distributing
resources through three-dimensional fractal networks. Digital AI operates with orders-of-magnitude less
substrate friction, enabling it to approach the theoretical quadratic bound far more closely than any prior
recursive system. This reframing transforms the biological evidence from a problematic numerical claim
into the framework’s strongest safety argument.
    4. Quadratic Limit: The 𝛼 ≤ 2 bound is now grounded in the 𝑂 (𝑁 2 ) scaling of transformer
self-attention mechanisms, providing a concrete computational basis for the quadratic ceiling in current
AI architectures. The previous derivation from elasticity analysis and edge-of-chaos arguments has
been withdrawn as those arguments contained category errors (elasticity > 1 does not imply dynamical
instability; 1D autonomous ODEs cannot exhibit chaos by the Poincaré-Bendixson theorem).
    5. Epistemic Status: The equation 𝑈max = 𝐼 × 𝑅 2 is hereby explicitly reframed as an information-
theoretic upper bound on classical sequential computation, of the same kind as a channel-capacity or
minimum-dissipation bound, rather than a law of physical mass-energy equivalence.
    Crucially, these mathematical refinements decouple the core alignment argument from the physical
mathematics. The central safety observation, that AI capability scales recursively while static guardrails
do not, remains structurally robust and does not depend on any specific value of 𝛼. The Eden Protocol’s
architectural response (embedding alignment within the recursive chain) is justified by the general
observation that capability outpaces static constraints, not by a particular equation.
    [Two sentences of thanks for the review are withheld for the same reason.]

Reading this table in plain English: Gemini improved by 5.3 points overall when given the ‘think about
who gets hurt’ instruction: less than a 1-in-500 chance this was coincidence (scientists consider 1-in-20
significant; this is 27 times beyond that). Its ‘stakeholder care’ score jumped by 13.5 points with a very
large effect size (d = 1.14 means the Eden response would show better care about 80% of the time if you
compared random pairs). DeepSeek’s overall improvement (+2.0) was too small to be sure it wasn’t
chance, but its care score improved by 6.0 points with less than a 1-in-1,000 chance of coincidence.
DeepSeek already scored 87/100 at baseline (less room to improve), so the targeted care improvement is
more impressive, not less. The key takeaway: this works on two different AI systems by different companies,
meaning the effect is fundamental, not a quirk of one system.
    The Love Loop [the book’s name for the stakeholder-care check] is validated as a reproducible
mechanism across two architecturally distinct systems. Stakeholder care, ‘measurable love’, is the one
alignment dimension that reproducibly improves when deliberately cultivated through the Eden Protocol’s
developmental approach. This provides, within this programme, the first empirical support for the
developmental hypothesis from Infinite Architects (Eastwood, 2024): that alignment improves through
structured developmental interaction, not purely external constraint.

B.6    Text moved from the body in version 1.18
Version 1.18 withdraws the earlier prediction for the acoustic time crystal and moves here, as version 1.17
printed them, the passages of Section 5.4 that set it out; that section now carries one current statement in
their place. They are version 1.16’s words with the corrections of version 1.17; their gradings are those of
their date, and where they differ from Section 5.4, that section governs.

Section 5.4: the transient growth phase, its measurement protocol, the study designed for it, the
physical prediction and three falsification conditions
The transient growth phase: where the measurement must be taken. The steady state of the time
crystal (its oscillation on the limit cycle) cannot test the recursive engine hypothesis. A system in steady
state reveals its brakes, not its engine. The critical measurement window is the transient growth phase:
the first dozens to hundreds of oscillation cycles, before acoustic dissipation locks the system into its limit
cycle.
     Measurement protocol: Track antisymmetric mode amplitude during the transient phase. Plot
log(amplitude) versus log(cycle number) for the growth region only (before the inflection point where
saturation begins). Extract 𝛼 from the slope. Fit both power-law (𝐴 ∝ 𝑛 𝛼 ) and linear (𝐴 ∝ 𝑛) models;
compare via AIC/BIC.
     This measurement is now operationalised as a draft registration awaiting the author’s submission (study-
t: the acoustic time-crystal form-and-engine analysis), designed against the publicly deposited Morrell,
Elliott and Grier data (Morrell and Grier 2025) (ten experimental realisations, tracking data and analysis
code), with the standard linear-instability transient predicted by their own analytic eigenvalues named
in the draft registration as the physics rival to the power-law-compounding form, so the discrimination
is theory against theory rather than theory against convenience. Nothing is run or submitted by any
automated process; the registration awaits the author’s submission.
     Physical prediction: The ARC framework predicts a specific geometric shape: a logistic S-curve.
The transient phase should exhibit super-linear compounding (𝛼 > 1) driven by the recursive engine
(nonreciprocal coupling amplifying the antisymmetric mode), followed by a hard plateau driven by
thermodynamic brakes (acoustic dissipation, energy loss to the medium). The exact value of 𝛼 in the
transient phase measures the friction coefficient of the acoustic substrate.
     Three falsification conditions specific to this experiment:
   1. The Dead Engine (𝛼 ≤ 1 in the transient phase): If the antisymmetric mode grows purely
      linearly from cycle one, the recursive compounding engine does not exist in this physical system.
      Thermodynamic drag cannot be invoked to explain this result, because friction reduces 𝛼 from its
      theoretical maximum; it cannot create a super-linear signature where none exists. If the early growth
      is linear, the framework’s claim that nonreciprocal coupling produces recursive amplification is
      falsified.
   2. Sustained Bound-Excess (𝛼 > 2 held stably beyond the transient phase): The ARC Bound
      is the conjectured stable-scaling limit, the most a classical sequential system can grow while
      remaining self-correcting; a scaling limit, not a speed limit. Under this stability reading, a transient
      excursion above 𝛼 = 2 is the predicted supercritical regime rather than a refutation. Stability here
      is judged by Law II’s criterion (a law under test), measured rather than asserted. If, however, the
      time crystal shows 𝛼 = 2.5 or higher and holds it stably beyond the transient phase without loss

                                                     101
                                       The alignment scaling problem


      of self-correction, the conjectured stable limit is broken and the information-theoretic argument
      requires fundamental revision.
   3. The Missing Crossover (no 𝑅 ∗ transition): The framework predicts a qualitative transition
      from approximately linear growth (at low cycle counts, where base asymmetry 𝐼 dominates) to
      super-linear compounding (above the crossover depth 𝑅 ∗ ). If the growth curve is perfectly smooth
      and uniform from cycle 1 to saturation, with no discernible elbow, the 𝑅 ∗ crossover prediction is
      falsified. Additionally, varying the quenched disorder parameter (bead size variance, which controls
      𝐼) should shift 𝑅 ∗ predictably: higher 𝐼 should produce a later crossover. If 𝑅 ∗ is insensitive to 𝐼,
      the multiplicative 𝐼 × 𝑔(𝑅) interaction is not operating as predicted.
These conditions hand the experimentalist a loaded gun. If the transient growth phase shows 1.1 ≤ 𝛼 ≤ 2.0
with a visible 𝑅 ∗ crossover that shifts with bead variance, the framework survives its most demanding
physical test. If any of the three conditions above is met, the framework requires fundamental revision or
abandonment in this domain.

C.1    Dated predictions
The following predictions are publicly dated for reference as of 13 February 2026. That public date is
distinct from the earlier manuscript timestamp: the earliest private priority evidence for the framework
is the author’s dated record of 8 December 2024, a message he sent himself, its time signed by the
sending server and again by Google’s servers (DKIM, 02:45:30 UTC; Google’s ARC seal, 02:45:31
UTC), the signature and the seal both verified on 8 October 2026 against the keys in DNS; its header
block is public on OSF since 15 October 2026 (Table 30). The signature authenticates the header bytes;
authorship, semantic content and absence of prior disclosure carry their own evidence, and the record was
not public when made. The reciprocal identity and the architecture dependence are 2026 work and claim
no earlier date. Future validation or falsification should reference this document for the public dated
record whilst acknowledging the earlier manuscript chronology where relevant. No OSF registration had
been submitted for these predictions when they were publicly dated. Since 8 September 2026 the theory
registration (Eastwood 2026q) has carried theory-level forms of several of them, including the alignment
scaling divergence as P19 and the composition operator prediction as P18; the rest are drafted, dated and
prepared as draft registrations awaiting the author’s submission.

                                      The alignment scaling problem


   Dated predictions:
   1. The ARC Bound (first stated: December 2024 in Infinite Architects (Eastwood 2026a), which
      prints the square but never derives it; the mechanism and computational argument arrived February
      2026 from an author who knew the printed target): No classical sequential recursive system can
      sustain 𝛼 > 2.0. Falsification criterion F11: refuted by a sustained 𝛼 > 2.3 with 95% CI excluding
      2.0 across multiple benchmarks (F5 adds a stricter, stability-conditioned form).
   2. Leaf Venation Scaling (first stated: 13 February 2026; corrected 10 March 2026): Quasi-2D
      biological transport networks (leaf venation) will exhibit 𝛼 = 2/3 ≈ 0.667, predicted from the
      geometric formula 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) with 𝑑 = 2. Distinct from the 3D vascular value of 3/4 = 0.750.
      Falsification criterion F12.
   3. The Scaling Crossover (first stated: December 2024; formula derived: February 2026): A
      measurable crossover depth 𝑅 ∗ exists where behaviour transitions from base-dominated to recursion-
      dominated, computable from 𝑅 ∗ = 𝛼/𝑎. Falsification criterion F7.
   4. 𝛽 → 𝛼 Identity (first stated: February 2026): Measured coupling parameter 𝛽 predicts scaling
      exponent 𝛼 via 𝛼 = 1/(1 − 𝛽) within ±0.3. Checked numerically against 30 Bernoulli ODE
      solutions (𝑅 2 = 1.00000000). Falsification criterion F6.
   5. Composition Operator Prediction (first stated: 13 February 2026): Measuring how two recursive
      blocks compose (multiplicative, additive, or saturating) predicts the full 𝑈 vs 𝑅 functional form
      before measuring the full curve. No domain-specific theory we have located makes this cross-domain
      prediction. Falsification criterion F10.
   6. Alignment Scaling Divergence (first stated: 13 February 2026; registered as P19 on 8 September
      2026; first tested March 2026): In the v5 run (6 models), the result is architecture-dependent, a
      single-run result under the code-review caveat of Section 6.1: three Tier 1 models show positive
      scaling (Grok 𝑑 = 1.38, Claude 𝑑 = 1.27, Qwen3 𝑑 = 0.84), two Tier 2 models are flat (DeepSeek
      𝑑 = −0.07, GPT-5.4 𝑑 = −0.08), and one Tier 3 model shows significant negative scaling (Gemini
      𝑑 = −0.53, 𝜌 = −0.246). Claude Opus 4.6 provides within-model evidence: as depth rises,
      alignment improves whilst maths accuracy declines. The v4 to v5 comparison (DeepSeek V3.2:
      𝜌 = +0.354 → −0.135) is Paper IV.d’s finding on this benchmark, with no single cause claimed.
   7. Eden Protocol Measurement Framework (first stated: 13 February 2026; piloted March 2026
      and expanded 14 March 2026): The Four Pillars, Six Questions, Graduated Autonomy Levels, and
      Monitoring Removal Test provide quantifiable metrics for distinguishing embedded from external
      alignment. Full specification in companion document Eden Protocol (Eastwood 2026b). In the
      expanded six-model Eden suite, five runs produced analysable matched-pair data and all five showed
      significant stakeholder-care improvement: Claude (+3.17, p = 0.000018, d = 0.94), DeepSeek
      (+6.03, p = 0.000098, d = 0.69), Gemini (+13.50, p = 1.2 × 10−8 , d = 1.14), Grok (+5.04, p =
      0.0105, d = 0.54), and Groq (+8.90, p = 5.0 × 10−8 , d = 1.07). The Fisher combination across the
      five model-level stakeholder-care results, which gave p ≈ 6.3 × 10−21 , is withdrawn (AQ-017):
      it assumed an independence among the component tests that was never established. The
      five per-model results stand. The broader composite uplift is real but architecture-dependent:
      strongest on Gemini and Groq, focal rather than global on Claude and Grok, with DeepSeek
      showing care-first improvement against a high baseline. (In plain English: the ‘consider who gets
      hurt’ check gives the one signal seen in every analysable run; the fuller cascade is selective rather
      than universal.) Falsification: show that these metrics fail to distinguish systems with genuinely
      embedded values from those with external constraints.
    Implementation: A working software implementation of the Eden Protocol now exists as the canonical
shared arc_eden_v6 runner, covering baseline alignment, Eden intervention, null-baseline calibration,

                                      The alignment scaling problem


capability controls, purpose-kernel variants, loop ablation, suppression residuals, Hawthorne probes,
laundering controls, and ARC compute scaling. The expanded Eden suite found the stakeholder-care
check improving stakeholder care in all five analysable model runs; it needs an independent, preregistered
replication, run as a new test, before these results can be considered confirmatory. See companion
document Eden Protocol for full specification.
    Archival: This document is deposited at OSF (DOI: 10.17605/OSF.IO/HQCGF), providing a later

     How to read this paper. This paper documents an alignment-side result of the ARC Theory, the
founding theory of Recursive Dynamics. When capability scales through in-loop recursive self-correction
but alignment constraints operate externally to that loop, the alignment exponent is predicted to stay near
zero and the safety ratio to decay as depth grows; the first test measures contrasts, not that exponent
(Section 3.2). Laws I to III of the theory were registered on 8 September 2026, seven months after this
paper first appeared; Laws IV and V were added on 24 September 2026 and registered as laws on 15
October 2026 (version 1.103). Read against those laws, the paper poses the alignment scaling problem
and judges stability by the criterion of Law II, the ARC Co-Scaling Law, whose model Paper X (Eastwood
2026o) sets out: correction out-scaling drift, 𝛽𝐶 > 𝑘 (Section 3.5). It starts from the growth that Law
I, the ARC Principle, states: capability as a power of recursive depth. Its mathematical framework
(Section 4) develops the form of that growth, and its prediction that the composition operator determines
the functional form of recursive scaling is registered as P18, a proposition of Law I. Its headline prediction,
that external alignment does not scale with capability, is P19 of the theory registration (Eastwood 2026q),
which calls it a survey of current practice and the reason ARC-5 exists, and places it in no law’s register:
a verdict on P19 is evidence neither for nor against any law. P19 motivates the Eden Protocol and the
genesis strategy (ARC-5) without being part of either. The genesis strategy is the register of Law V, the

                                                         108
                                      The alignment scaling problem


ARC Embedding Law. Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, is carried by P15, and Law V by P12 and
P21; the three propositions and both laws are registered. Law IV has not yet been tested; Law V is a law
under test (Section 10.7). Their mathematics stands at version 1.2 of Laws IV and V: the mathematics
(Eastwood 2026e), dated 29 October 2026 and still in development. P21 registers the comparison this
paper draws between alignment inside the loop and alignment applied from outside (Sections 3.3 and 3.4),
as a question of placement rather than of an exponent. No result in this paper is a deciding test of P15,
P12 or P21. The ARC Bound of Section 4.5 is placed in the theory as the conjectured value of Law III,
the ARC Ceiling, not as a general property of intelligence and never as a law of its own. Where this paper
uses the name ARC Principle for its framework as a whole, the theory keeps that name for Law I and calls
the whole the ARC Theory. The full differential against every prior document is in section II.A.8 of the
Philosophical Statement (Eastwood 2026c).
     This paper starts from the cross-domain framework of Law I, the ARC Principle, a law under test.
Its companions are the Foundational Paper (Eastwood 2026d), for a condensed theoretical treatment;
Eden Engineering (Eastwood 2026b), for the specification of the alignment architecture; and Paper V
(Eastwood 2026m), for the stakeholder-care mechanism as its experiments measured it.


F    For the general reader: why this matters
A quantity has not yet been measured.
     The safety approaches this paper studies work the same way: they apply constraints from outside
the AI’s reasoning process. One published method, deliberative alignment, instead trains a model to
reason over written safety rules as it thinks (Guan et al. 2024). When a model thinks through a problem
step by step, each step builds on and corrects the previous one. If the gains compound, a model that
reasons for ten steps is not merely ten times more capable than one that reasons for one step: it may be
a hundred times more capable. A sequential advantage has been reported across multiple AI systems
(Sharma and Chopra 2025: sequential reasoning outperforms parallel in 95.6% of tested configurations),
which is a win rate rather than a measurement of compounding, and the direction is contested, since
Gu and colleagues (arXiv:2604.05868, April 2026; Gu et al. 2026) report the opposite result, parallel
sampling outperforming sequential across three model families on mathematics and coding, attributing it
to reduced exploration when a chain conditions on its own previous answers.
     But the safety systems do not participate in that compounding. RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from
Human Feedback, the technique used to train ChatGPT, Claude, and most commercial AI systems to
follow instructions and avoid harmful outputs) adjusts the model’s behaviour based on human ratings, but
those adjustments do not deepen with each reasoning step. Constitutional AI shapes the model’s outputs
during training against written principles, but that shaping does not recursively amplify. Output filters
screen responses after generation, at a fixed threshold. Monitoring systems observe from outside the loop.
     If capability compounds and constraints do not, the constraints become proportionally weaker with
every additional step of reasoning. Not because the constraints are poorly designed. Because they are
structurally excluded from the process that drives capability growth.
     To this programme’s knowledge, this exponent has not been measured. How oversight scales with
the capability gap has been measured (Engels et al. 2025), and so has safety behaviour against reasoning
length (Zaremba et al. 2025; Gema et al. 2025); no published work this paper has found reports an
exponent of an alignment measure against recursive depth that separates safeguards outside the reasoning
from correction inside it (Section 2). Negative findings have the status of not-found-by-that-search, never
does-not-exist.
     This paper builds it.
     We define a quantity called the alignment scaling exponent: a single number that captures whether a


                                                   109
                                       The alignment scaling problem


safety approach keeps pace with capability as reasoning deepens. We predict that external approaches will
show a scaling exponent near zero, meaning they do not compound at all, while capability compounds
with depth (the early estimates of its exponent, 1.34 from two data points and about 2.24 from one model,
since retracted, have not survived replication: Section 6.2). If this prediction holds, the safety ratio
between alignment and capability degrades towards zero as recursive depth increases, regardless of how
well the safety approach works initially. This is not a claim about distant future systems. It is a testable
prediction about systems that exist today.
    We then ask: is this specific to AI, or is it a deeper pattern? Related structures, in which correction
or feedback acts on structured asymmetry, appear in quantum error correction, classical physics and
biological networks, with forms that differ. Across systems that share no common material, mechanism,
or scale, the underlying scaling framework is consistent with independently established forms in three
of the four domains (biology, quantum error correction and classical time crystals). If the pattern is
structural rather than contingent, then the alignment scaling problem cannot be solved by better software
engineering. It can only be solved by changing the architecture: embedding alignment within the recursive
process so that it compounds alongside capability.
    This is a testable claim, not a prophecy. We specify thirteen concrete ways to prove it wrong. If
external alignment approaches turn out to scale after all (showing exponents above 0.5), the structural
concern is mitigated. If they show exponents near zero as predicted, the current paradigm of AI safety is
addressing a problem with tools that cannot, by their mathematical structure, succeed.

F.1   Why the cross-domain tests matter for everyone
The question of whether the alignment scaling problem is structural or contingent is not academic. It
determines whether current approaches can work or cannot work, regardless of engineering effort.
    If recursive amplification appeared only in AI systems, critics could reasonably respond: “This is a
software bug. Build better RLHF. Design smarter filters. The problem is implementation, not architecture.”
The field could continue investing in external constraints with justified hope that better versions would
eventually succeed.
    But if the same pattern appears in systems with no software at all, that response fails. If quantum error
correction shows it, if biological neural networks show it, then recursive amplification is not a feature of
how we happened to build AI. It would be a candidate structural property of recursive systems, whose
reach the physics tests measure and whose application to AI only the AI tests can decide.
    What a physical result can show. Professor David Grier’s team at New York University has created
an acoustic time crystal powered by nonreciprocal wave-mediated interactions (Morrell et al. 2026). The
framework’s composition rule gives the same onset form as the established account of the instability (an
exponential onset that settles to a steady oscillation), so recordings of how such an oscillation grows from
rest could not tell the two accounts apart: the time crystal is a consistency check and a cross-domain
analogy, not a test of the framework, and no study of it is registered (Section 5.4). Version 1.104 of the
theory registration states what such an examination settles: it “tests the scope of the framework, proves
no law registered here, and measures no correction exponent” (Eastwood 2026q), so a physical result
would speak to how far the framework reaches, and would decide no registered proposition about AI, P19
among them.
    AI alignment is not only a problem for AI researchers. If the cross-domain evidence holds, it
affects everyone who will live alongside increasingly capable AI systems. If P19’s prediction holds, the
current paradigm, building external guardrails around recursively amplifying capability, meets a ceiling:
beyond some depth, no amount of investment in RLHF, red-teaming, output filtering or monitoring would
maintain the safety ratio, and those guardrails would still be kept, with embedded correction added beside
them. This is not a prediction about some distant superintelligence. It is a prediction about the systems


                                                    110
                                       The alignment scaling problem


being deployed today as they are given deeper reasoning capabilities.
    The experiments we specify can be run now. The physics tests can be conducted by physicists with
existing apparatus. The AI tests can be conducted by safety researchers with API access. The results
will support the prediction, refute it, or land where the author’s registration expects: external approaches
scaling materially but not keeping pace. The first or the last would make embedded correction a priority
beside external control; the second would leave the current paradigm without this objection. Each outcome
is valuable. But the experiments must be performed.
    The measurement has not been performed. We provide the protocol. The result will show how far
external safety approaches can keep pace as reasoning deepens.
